This fact is made precise by the quadratic variation of Brownian motion.  If
\(0=t_0<t_1<\cdots<t_n=T\) is a partition whose mesh tends to zero, then
\[
\sum_{k=1}^{n}
  \bigl(W_{t_k}-W_{t_{k-1}}\bigr)^2
\longrightarrow T
\]
in probability.  The corresponding Itô multiplication rules are
\[
\boxed{
(\dd t)^2=0,
\qquad
\dd t\,\dd W_t=0,
\qquad
(\dd W_t)^2=\dd t.
}
\]
The last identity is shorthand for quadratic variation, not an ordinary
algebraic identity between infinitesimals.

\section{Itô's Lemma}

\begin{theorem}[Itô's lemma]
Let \(X\) satisfy
\[
\dd X_t=a(t,X_t)\,\dd t+b(t,X_t)\,\dd W_t,
\]
and let \(f\in C^{1,2}\), meaning that \(f\) is once continuously
differentiable in time and twice continuously differentiable in its spatial
variable.  Then
\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\dd f(t,X_t)
={}&\left[
\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}
+a(t,X_t)\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}
+\frac12 b^2(t,X_t)\frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x^2}
\right]\dd t\\
&+b(t,X_t)\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\,\dd W_t.
\end{aligned}}
\]
All derivatives are evaluated at \((t,X_t)\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Derivation from the Itô rules]
Apply the second-order Taylor expansion:
\[
\dd f=f_t\,\dd t+f_x\,\dd X_t
+\frac12 f_{xx}(\dd X_t)^2.
\]
Since
\[
(\dd X_t)^2
=\bigl(a\,\dd t+b\,\dd W_t\bigr)^2
=b^2\,\dd t,
\]
substitution and collection of the \(\dd t\) and \(\dd W_t\) terms gives
the stated formula.
\end{proof}

The formula separates the change in \(f\) into three effects: direct time
dependence, exposure to the drift and random movement of \(X_t\), and the
additional curvature correction
\(\tfrac12 b^2 f_{xx}\,\dd t\).

\subsection{Example: Squared Brownian Motion}

Take \(X_t=W_t\) and \(f(x)=x^2\).  Here \(a=0\), \(b=1\),
\(f_x=2x\), and \(f_{xx}=2\).  Itô's lemma gives
\[
\boxed{
\dd(W_t^2)=2W_t\,\dd W_t+\dd t.
}
\]
Equivalently,
\[
W_t^2=t+2\int_0^t W_s\,\dd W_s.
\]
The extra \(\dd t\) term has no counterpart in the ordinary chain rule.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{0.26\textwidth}L{0.31\textwidth}L{0.31\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{Ordinary calculus} & \textbf{Stochastic calculus} \\
\midrule
Function & \(f(x)=x^2\) & \(f(W_t)=W_t^2\) \\
Result & \(\dd(x^2)=2x\,\dd x\) &
\(\dd(W_t^2)=2W_t\,\dd W_t+\dd t\) \\
Second-order term & Vanishes in the first-order limit &
Survives through \((\dd W_t)^2=\dd t\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\section{Solving Geometric Brownian Motion}

Suppose that a stock price follows
\[
\dd S_t=\mu S_t\,\dd t+\sigma S_t\,\dd W_t,
\qquad S_0>0.
\]
Apply Itô's lemma to \(f(S)=\log S\).  Since
\[
f_S=\frac1S,
\qquad
f_{SS}=-\frac1{S^2},
\]
we obtain
\[
\begin{aligned}
\dd\log S_t
&=\frac1{S_t}\,\dd S_t
+\frac12\left(-\frac1{S_t^2}\right)
  \sigma^2S_t^2\,\dd t\\
&=\left(\mu-\frac12\sigma^2\right)\dd t
+\sigma\,\dd W_t.
\end{aligned}
\]
Integrating from \(0\) to \(t\) yields
\[
\log\frac{S_t}{S_0}
=\left(\mu-\frac12\sigma^2\right)t+\sigma W_t,
\]
and therefore
\[
\boxed{
S_t=S_0\exp\!\left[
\left(\mu-\frac12\sigma^2\right)t+\sigma W_t
\right].
}
\]
The term \(-\sigma^2/2\) is precisely the Itô curvature correction.  It
would be missed by applying the ordinary chain rule.

For observations separated by a time step \(\Delta t\), the log-return is
\[
\log\frac{S_{t+\Delta t}}{S_t}
\sim\mathcal{N}\!\left(
\left(\mu-\frac12\sigma^2\right)\Delta t,
\sigma^2\Delta t
\right).
\]
Thus the sample mean of log-returns estimates the log-growth rate
\(\mu-\sigma^2/2\), while the sample variance estimates
\(\sigma^2\Delta t\).

\section{Girsanov's Theorem}

\subsection{Changing the Probability Measure}

Girsanov's theorem explains how the drift of a diffusion changes when the
underlying probability measure is changed.  The sample paths themselves are
not altered; instead, their probabilities are reweighted.  In finance this
provides the mathematical bridge from the real-world measure \(\Prob\) to a
risk-neutral measure \(\mathbb{Q}\).

\begin{theorem}[Girsanov's theorem, Brownian version]
Let \(W^{\Prob}\) be Brownian motion under \(\Prob\), and let
\(\theta_t\) be an adapted process satisfying Novikov's condition
\[
\E^{\Prob}\!\left[
\exp\!\left(\frac12\int_0^T\theta_s^2\,\dd s\right)
\right]<\infty.
\]
Define
\[
Z_T=\exp\!\left(
-\int_0^T\theta_s\,\dd W_s^{\Prob}
-\frac12\int_0^T\theta_s^2\,\dd s
\right),
\qquad
\frac{\dd\mathbb{Q}}{\dd\Prob}\bigg|_{\Fscr_T}=Z_T.
\]
Then
\[
\boxed{
W_t^{\mathbb{Q}}
=W_t^{\Prob}+\int_0^t\theta_s\,\dd s
}
\]
is Brownian motion under \(\mathbb{Q}\) on \([0,T]\).
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Risk-Neutral Stock Dynamics}

Under the real-world measure, suppose
\[
\dd S_t=\mu S_t\,\dd t+\sigma S_t\,\dd W_t^{\Prob},
\]
and let \(r\) be the continuously compounded risk-free rate.  Choose the
market price of risk
\[
\theta=\frac{\mu-r}{\sigma}.
\]
Because \(\dd W_t^{\Prob}=\dd W_t^{\mathbb{Q}}-\theta\,\dd t\), substitution
gives
\[
\boxed{
\dd S_t=rS_t\,\dd t+\sigma S_t\,\dd W_t^{\mathbb{Q}}.
}
\]
The unknown real-world drift \(\mu\) has disappeared.  Under standard
integrability assumptions, the discounted stock price
\(e^{-rt}S_t\) is a martingale under \(\mathbb{Q}\).  Consequently, a claim
with payoff \(H\) at time \(T\) has the risk-neutral value
\[
\boxed{
V_t=\E^{\mathbb{Q}}\!\left[
e^{-r(T-t)}H\mid\Fscr_t
\right].
}
\]

\begin{remark}
Girsanov's theorem changes drift, not instantaneous volatility.  It does not
by itself prove that a risk-neutral measure exists or is unique; those facts
depend on the absence of arbitrage and market completeness.
\end{remark}

\section{How the Ideas Connect}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    node distance=3.5mm,
    stage/.style={diagramBox,text width=12.2cm,minimum height=8mm,
                  font=\footnotesize,inner sep=3pt},
    note/.style={font=\scriptsize,fill=white,inner sep=1pt}
]
\node[stage] (bm) {\textbf{Standard Brownian motion}\quad
\(W_t\sim\mathcal{N}(0,t)\); continuous paths, independent Gaussian increments};
\node[stage,below=of bm] (abm) {\textbf{Brownian motion with drift}\quad
\(\dd X_t=a\,\dd t+b\,\dd W_t\), so \(X_t\sim\mathcal{N}(X_0+at,b^2t)\)};
\node[stage,below=of abm] (gbm) {\textbf{Geometric Brownian motion}\quad
\(\dd S_t=\mu S_t\,\dd t+\sigma S_t\,\dd W_t\); prices remain positive};
\node[stage,below=of gbm] (ret) {\textbf{Log-returns and estimation}\quad
normal log-returns identify the log-growth rate and volatility};
\node[stage,below=of ret] (ito) {\textbf{Itô's lemma}\quad
\((\dd W_t)^2=\dd t\) produces the curvature correction and solves the GBM SDE};
\node[stage,below=of ito] (gir) {\textbf{Girsanov's theorem}\quad
change from \(\Prob\) to \(\mathbb{Q}\) so that the stock drift becomes \(r\)};
\node[stage,below=of gir,fill=diagramGreen!10] (price) {\textbf{Risk-neutral pricing}\quad
discounted asset prices are martingales and claims are discounted expectations};

\draw[diagramArrow] (bm) -- node[note,right]{add drift and scale} (abm);
\draw[diagramArrow] (abm) -- node[note,right]{exponentiate} (gbm);
\draw[diagramArrow] (gbm) -- node[note,right]{take logarithms} (ret);
\draw[diagramArrow] (ret) -- node[note,right]{account for quadratic variation} (ito);
\draw[diagramArrow] (ito) -- node[note,right]{remove real-world drift} (gir);
\draw[diagramArrow] (gir) -- node[note,right]{discount and take expectation} (price);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{From Brownian motion to risk-neutral derivative pricing.}
\label{fig:ito-girsanov-roadmap}
\end{figure}

\section{Key Formula Reference}

\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.29\textwidth}L{0.64\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Concept} & \textbf{Formula} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Concept} & \textbf{Formula} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Standard Brownian motion &
\(W_t\sim\mathcal{N}(0,t)\), with \(W_0=0\) \\
Brownian increment &
\(W_t-W_s\sim\mathcal{N}(0,t-s)\) for \(t>s\) \\
Brownian motion with drift &
\(\dd X_t=a\,\dd t+b\,\dd W_t\) \\
GBM SDE &
\(\dd S_t=\mu S_t\,\dd t+\sigma S_t\,\dd W_t\) \\
GBM solution &
\(S_t=S_0\exp[(\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2)t+\sigma W_t]\) \\
Log-return distribution &
\(\log(S_{t+\Delta t}/S_t)\sim
\mathcal{N}((\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2)\Delta t,\sigma^2\Delta t)\) \\
Simple and log returns &
\(\log(S_t/S_{t-1})\approx(S_t-S_{t-1})/S_{t-1}\) for small returns \\
Itô multiplication rules &
\((\dd t)^2=0\), \(\dd t\,\dd W_t=0\), \((\dd W_t)^2=\dd t\) \\
Itô example &
\(\dd(W_t^2)=2W_t\,\dd W_t+\dd t\) \\
Itô's lemma &
\(\dd f=(f_t+af_x+\tfrac12b^2f_{xx})\dd t+bf_x\,\dd W_t\) \\
Girsanov density &
\(Z_T=\exp[-\int_0^T\theta_s\,\dd W_s^{\Prob}
-\tfrac12\int_0^T\theta_s^2\,\dd s]\) \\
Risk-neutral dynamics &
\(\dd S_t=rS_t\,\dd t+\sigma S_t\,\dd W_t^{\mathbb{Q}}\) \\
Risk-neutral value &
\(V_t=\E^{\mathbb{Q}}[e^{-r(T-t)}H\mid\Fscr_t]\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\section{Core Takeaways}

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Standard Brownian motion is a continuous pure-noise process whose
    variance grows linearly with time.

    \item Adding drift produces a trend but does not prevent negative values;
    exponentiating the process produces the positive geometric Brownian
    motion model used for stock prices.

    \item Brownian increments are of order \(\sqrt{\dd t}\), so their squares
    are of order \(\dd t\).  This is why second-order terms survive in
    stochastic calculus.

    \item Itô's lemma adds the curvature term
    \(\tfrac12b^2f_{xx}\,\dd t\) to the ordinary chain rule and produces the
    \(-\sigma^2/2\) correction in the explicit GBM solution.

    \item Girsanov's theorem reweights path probabilities so that drift can
    be changed consistently while volatility remains unchanged.

    \item Under the risk-neutral measure, discounted tradable asset prices
    are martingales, allowing derivatives to be valued as discounted
    conditional expectations of their future payoffs.
\end{enumerate}

\chapter{Factors Affecting Option Prices}

\section{Option Price Fundamentals}
\subsection{The Option Price Equation}
When you purchase an option --- whether a Call or a Put --- you pay a \textbf{premium}. This premium is not a single monolithic number; it is composed of two distinct components:

\[
\boxed{\text{Option Price (Premium)} = \text{Intrinsic Value} + \text{Extrinsic Value}}
\]

Understanding what drives each component is the foundation of options pricing.

\medskip
\subsection{Intrinsic Value}
\textbf{Intrinsic value} is the "built-in" or "real" worth of an option --- the amount you would gain if you exercised the option \emph{right now}, at the current market price. It is sometimes called the option's \textbf{immediate exercise value}.

Think of it as the option's objective, factual value based purely on where the underlying price stands relative to the strike price at this exact moment.

\textbf{For a Call Option} (the right to \emph{buy} the underlying at strike price K):

\[
\text{Intrinsic Value (Call)} = \max(S - K, \ 0)
\]

\begin{itemize}
\item If $S > K$ (In-The-Money, ITM): Intrinsic Value = $S - K$ (you could buy at K and immediately sell at S for a profit)


\item If $S \leq K$ (At-The-Money or Out-of-The-Money): Intrinsic Value = 0


\end{itemize}
\textbf{For a Put Option} (the right to \emph{sell} the underlying at strike price K):

\[
\text{Intrinsic Value (Put)} = \max(K - S, \ 0)
\]

\begin{itemize}
\item If $K > S$ (ITM): Intrinsic Value = $K - S$ (you could buy at S and immediately sell at K for a profit)


\item If $K \leq S$ (ATM or OTM): Intrinsic Value = 0


\end{itemize}
\begin{quote}
\textbf{Critical Rule:} Only \textbf{in-the-money (ITM)} options have intrinsic value. At-the-money (ATM) and out-of-the-money (OTM) options have zero intrinsic value.

\end{quote}
\textbf{Example:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Call option with strike K = ₹90, current stock price S = ₹100


\item Intrinsic Value = 100 \(-\) 90 = \textbf{₹10}


\item If the option trades at ₹12.50, then: Extrinsic Value = 12.50 \(-\) 10 = \textbf{₹2.50}


\end{itemize}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=7mm and 16mm]
\node[diagramBox,fill=diagramBlue!10] (total) {\textbf{Total option value}\\Rs.~12.50};
\node[diagramBox,below left=of total,fill=diagramGreen!10] (intrinsic)
{\textbf{Intrinsic value}\\Rs.~10.00\\{\footnotesize Immediate exercise value}};
\node[diagramBox,below right=of total,fill=diagramOrange!10] (extrinsic)
{\textbf{Extrinsic value}\\Rs.~2.50\\{\footnotesize Time and volatility premium}};
\draw[diagramArrow] (total) -- (intrinsic);
\draw[diagramArrow] (total) -- (extrinsic);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Decomposition of the option premium.}
\end{figure}
\medskip
\subsection{Extrinsic Value (Time Value)}
\textbf{Extrinsic value} --- also called \textbf{time value} --- is the additional premium beyond intrinsic value that option buyers pay for \emph{optionality}: the probability that the option could become more valuable (or move from OTM to ITM) before expiration.

An option carries extrinsic value \textbf{as long as there is time remaining} before it expires. At expiration, extrinsic value = 0, and the option price collapses to its intrinsic value (or zero if OTM).

Extrinsic value is affected by two things:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Time until expiration} --- More time remaining = more opportunity for favorable price movement = higher extrinsic value


\item \textbf{Implied Volatility} --- Greater expected price swings = higher chance of the option ending ITM = higher extrinsic value


\end{enumerate}
\begin{quote}
\textbf{Key insight:} Even a deeply OTM option (zero intrinsic value) can have significant extrinsic value if there is a lot of time remaining and/or volatility is high. The option represents a \emph{possibility} of profit, and that possibility has a price.

\end{quote}
\medskip
\section{The Six Key Factors Affecting Option Prices}
\subsection{Underlying Price (Related Greek: Delta)}
The price of the underlying asset is the most direct driver of option value. Its effect is \textbf{directionally opposite} for calls vs. puts.

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.19\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Scenario} & \textbf{Call Option} & \textbf{Put Option} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Scenario} & \textbf{Call Option} & \textbf{Put Option} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Underlying price ↑ & Value ↑ & Value ↓ \\
Underlying price ↓ & Value ↓ & Value ↑ \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\textbf{Why?} A call is a bet that the price will rise. When it rises, the call moves deeper ITM (or from OTM toward ITM), increasing its value. The opposite holds for puts.

\textbf{Example:} Mr. XYZ buys a \textbf{Call} and Mr. ABC buys a \textbf{Put} on "Bright Industries," both with strike K = ₹50:

\emph{Scenario 1 --- Stock rises to ₹70:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Mr. XYZ (Call): \textcolor{diagramGreen}{\\(\\checkmark\\)} Gains (call is now ₹20 ITM; intrinsic value = ₹20)


\item Mr. ABC (Put): \textcolor{bearc}{\\(\\times\\)} Loses (put is OTM; intrinsic value = 0)


\end{itemize}
\emph{Scenario 2 --- Stock falls to ₹40:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Mr. XYZ (Call): \textcolor{bearc}{\\(\\times\\)} Loses (call is OTM; intrinsic value = 0)


\item Mr. ABC (Put): \textcolor{diagramGreen}{\\(\\checkmark\\)} Gains (put is ₹10 ITM; intrinsic value = ₹10)


\end{itemize}
The \textbf{Delta (\(\Delta\))} Greek measures this sensitivity:

\begin{itemize}
\item For calls: \(\Delta\) \(\in\) (0, 1) --- a delta of 0.6 means the call's price rises by ₹0.60 for every ₹1 rise in the stock


\item For puts: \(\Delta\) \(\in\) (\(-\)1, 0)


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\subsection{Strike Price}
The strike price K is fixed at the time the options contract is written. It determines whether and how much intrinsic value the option has, and directly affects the option's premium.

\textbf{For Call Options:}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Lower strike K} → Option is more likely to be ITM (or deeper ITM) → Higher intrinsic value → \textbf{Higher premium}


\item \textbf{Higher strike K} → Option further OTM → Lower probability of profit → \textbf{Lower premium}


\end{itemize}
\textbf{For Put Options:}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Higher strike K} → Option more likely to be ITM → \textbf{Higher premium}


\item \textbf{Lower strike K} → Option further OTM → \textbf{Lower premium}


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Example:} A call option with K = ₹90, S = ₹100:

\begin{itemize}
\item Intrinsic Value = ₹100 \(-\) ₹90 = ₹10


\item Total premium = ₹12.50 = ₹10 (intrinsic) + ₹2.50 (extrinsic)


\end{itemize}
As the option moves deeper ITM, the premium grows (intrinsic value component increases). As it goes deeper OTM, only extrinsic value remains, which eventually approaches zero.

\medskip
\subsection{Time Until Expiration (Related Greek: Theta)}
Time is one of the most powerful forces in options pricing. Options are \textbf{wasting assets} --- their extrinsic (time) value erodes continuously as expiration approaches. This erosion is called \textbf{time decay} or \textbf{theta decay}.

\textbf{Key relationships:}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Longer time to expiration} → Higher probability that OTM option turns ITM → Higher extrinsic value → \textbf{Higher premium} (for both calls and puts)


\item \textbf{As expiration approaches} → Extrinsic value decays, and the rate of decay \emph{accelerates} near expiration


\item \textbf{At expiration} → Extrinsic value = 0; option worth only intrinsic value (or zero if OTM)


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Intuition --- The Insurance Analogy:} Options function like insurance policies. A 1-year car insurance policy costs more than a 1-month policy because there is a greater window of time during which an accident can occur. As the coverage period shrinks, the premium drops. Similarly, the longer an option has until expiration, the more time there is for a favorable price move --- hence higher premium.

\textbf{Analogy --- Perishable Goods:} Think of options like perishable goods in a grocery store. As the expiration date approaches, the store discounts the items to move them before they become worthless. An option approaching expiry is similarly discounted toward its intrinsic value.

\textbf{ATM options are most sensitive to time decay:} Because ATM options have zero intrinsic value, their entire premium is extrinsic. This premium erodes fully to zero by expiration.

\textbf{Theta (\(\Theta\))} measures the rate of time decay --- specifically, the dollar amount an option loses per day from time passing alone (all else equal). Theta is always negative for long options.

\medskip
\subsection{Volatility of the Underlying (Related Greek: Vega)}
Volatility is arguably the \textbf{most critical and nuanced} factor in options pricing --- and the only one that is not directly observable in the market. It must be estimated or implied from other market data.

\textbf{Definition:} Volatility (\(\sigma\)) is the \textbf{standard deviation of the logarithmic returns} of the underlying asset over a given period. Intuitively, it measures how much the asset's price swings around its average path.

\textbf{Why high volatility benefits option buyers:} Options provide asymmetric payoffs --- you profit on favorable moves, and your maximum loss is limited to the premium paid. High volatility means large moves in \emph{either} direction are possible:

\begin{itemize}
\item For call buyers: large upward moves → large profits


\item For put buyers: large downward moves → large profits


\item The maximum loss is always just the premium


\end{itemize}
Therefore:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Higher volatility}
&\Rightarrow \text{higher probability of large moves}\\
&\Rightarrow \text{higher call and put premiums}.
\end{aligned}
\]

\textbf{The insurance analogy again:} Options are insurance contracts. A neighborhood with high crime rates has expensive home insurance --- because the probability of a claim is higher. Similarly, a highly volatile stock has expensive options, because the chance of large price moves (claims on the option) is higher.

\textbf{Practical observation:} Before major events (RBI monetary policy announcements, quarterly earnings, Budget Day), volatility spikes and option premiums become expensive. After the event, the uncertainty resolves and volatility --- along with option premiums --- collapses. This post-event collapse is called a \textbf{volatility crush}.

\textbf{Vega (\(\nu\))} measures how much the option price changes for a 1\% change in implied volatility. A Vega of 0.10 means the option price rises by ₹0.10 for every 1\% increase in volatility.

\textbf{Vega and time to expiration:} Vega decreases as expiration approaches. With more time remaining, the implied volatility has a longer window to affect the option outcome --- so the premium is more sensitive to volatility changes. An option with 1 day to expiry has near-zero Vega because there is almost no time for volatility to matter.

\textbf{How volatility is measured:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Magnitude of price changes} --- how large were the daily moves?


\item \textbf{Speed of changes} --- how quickly did those changes occur?


\item \textbf{Historical price change patterns} --- do large moves tend to follow large moves? (volatility clustering)


\item \textbf{Expected future events} --- are there upcoming announcements that could cause large moves?


\end{enumerate}
\medskip
\subsection{Dividends}
Dividends have a small but real effect on option prices, transmitted through their impact on the underlying stock price.

\textbf{Mechanism:} When a stock goes ex-dividend, its price drops approximately by the dividend amount on the ex-dividend date (shareholders receive the dividend in cash, so the stock's total value is lower by that amount).

\textbf{Effect on options:}

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.15\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Event} & \textbf{Effect on Stock Price} & \textbf{Effect on Call} & \textbf{Effect on Put} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Event} & \textbf{Effect on Stock Price} & \textbf{Effect on Call} & \textbf{Effect on Put} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Dividend paid & Stock price ↓ & Call value ↓ & Put value ↑ \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\textbf{Why:} Since the stock price drops (all else equal), calls --- which benefit from price increases --- become less valuable. Puts --- which benefit from price decreases --- become more valuable.

This effect is small for low-dividend stocks but can be significant for high-dividend-paying stocks or for long-dated options. The Black-Scholes formula (to be discussed) assumes no dividends in its basic form; adjusted versions (e.g., Black-Scholes-Merton) incorporate a continuous dividend yield.

\medskip
\subsection{Interest Rates (Related Greek: Rho)}
Interest rates affect option prices through the cost of carry --- the opportunity cost of holding or financing a position.

\textbf{Effect on Call Options:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Higher interest rates → Higher call option values


\end{itemize}
\textbf{The reasoning:} When you buy a call instead of the underlying stock directly, you pay a smaller premium (vs. buying the stock outright) and retain the remaining cash. At higher interest rates, that retained cash earns more interest --- making the call option relatively more attractive than owning the stock. This increased attractiveness is reflected in higher call premiums.

\textbf{Effect on Put Options:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Higher interest rates → Lower put option values (opposite direction)


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Rho (\(\rho\))} measures option price sensitivity to interest rate changes.

\textbf{Practical note:} For short-dated equity options, Rho is typically the smallest of all the Greeks --- its effect is modest. It becomes more important for:

\begin{itemize}
\item Long-dated options (LEAPs)


\item Interest rate derivatives (caps, floors, swaptions), where interest rates \emph{are} the underlying


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\section{Volatility --- A Deep Dive}
Since volatility is the most complex and influential factor, it warrants a much deeper treatment.

\subsection{Volatility as a Measure of Risk}
In quantitative finance, understanding any asset requires tracking two quantities:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Mean (\(\mu\)):} The expected return --- the direction you expect the asset to move on average


\item \textbf{Variance (\(\sigma\)²):} The expected risk --- how much the price fluctuates around the mean


\end{itemize}
\[
\text{Mean} = \text{Expected Return} \quad \text{(What we hope to gain)}
\]
\[
\text{Variance} = \text{Expected Risk} \quad \text{(How uncertain that gain is)}
\]

Both must be predicted before investing or trading. An asset may have a high expected return but also high variance --- meaning the actual return could be very different from the expectation. Volatility quantifies this uncertainty.

The \textbf{annualized volatility formula} for scaling across time periods: \[
\sigma_{\text{annualized}} = \sigma_{\text{daily}} \times \sqrt{T}
\]

where T is the number of trading periods in a year (typically 252 trading days). For a 10-day horizon: \[
\sigma_{10\text{-day}} = \sigma_{\text{daily}} \times \sqrt{10}
\]

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Convention:} In Indian markets (NSE), 252 trading days per year is the standard assumption. Global practice sometimes uses 250 or 260. The choice affects the annualized estimate, so consistency is important.

\end{quote}
\medskip
\subsection{Historical Volatility vs. Implied Volatility}
These two concepts appear throughout options trading and quant finance literature. Understanding the distinction is essential.

\textbf{Historical Volatility (HV) --- Also called Realized Volatility:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Computed from \emph{past} price data over a chosen lookback window (e.g., 30 days, 60 days, 1 year)


\item Formula: Standard deviation of daily log returns $\ln(S_t / S_{t-1})$ over the window


\item Answers: "How volatile \emph{was} this asset over the past period?"


\item Assumption: Past volatility patterns will continue --- which may or may not hold


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Implied Volatility (IV):}

\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Forward-looking} --- it is the market's collective expectation of future volatility


\item Not directly observable; it is \emph{back-calculated} ("implied") from observed option prices using a pricing model


\item \textbf{The mechanism:} In any option pricing model (e.g., Black-Scholes), volatility is the \emph{only} input that cannot be observed directly. All other inputs (stock price, strike, time, interest rate) are known. If you take an observed market option price and solve for the volatility that makes the model price equal to the market price --- \emph{that} volatility is the implied volatility.


\item Answers: "What does the market believe volatility will be going forward?"


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Relationship:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Historical volatility \emph{informs} implied volatility --- traders compare the two to assess whether options are "cheap" or "expensive"


\item When IV > HV: market expects more volatility than history suggests --- options are "expensive" (e.g., before earnings)


\item When IV < HV: market expects less volatility --- options are "cheap" (e.g., after a volatility crush)


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\subsection{Computing Historical Volatility: Step-by-Step}
\textbf{Step 1: Compute log returns} \[
r_t = \ln\left(\frac{S_t}{S_{t-1}}\right)
\]

Why log returns? They are symmetric, approximately normal, handle compounding correctly, and are bounded even for large price changes.

\textbf{Step 2: Compute the mean return} \[
\bar{r} = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{t=1}^{n} r_t
\]

\textbf{Step 3: Compute the variance} \[
s^2 = \frac{1}{n-1} \sum_{t=1}^{n} (r_t - \bar{r})^2
\]

\textbf{Step 4: Compute the standard deviation (volatility)} \[
\sigma = \sqrt{s^2}
\]

\textbf{Step 5: Annualize}

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.27\textwidth}L{0.66\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Data Frequency} & \textbf{Annualization} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Data Frequency} & \textbf{Annualization} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Daily & $\sigma_{\text{annual}} = \sigma_{\text{daily}} \times \sqrt{N}$ where N = 252 (or 250/260) \\
Weekly & $\sigma_{\text{annual}} = \sigma_{\text{weekly}} \times \sqrt{52}$ \\
Monthly & $\sigma_{\text{annual}} = \sigma_{\text{monthly}} \times \sqrt{12}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\begin{quote}
\textbf{Why $\sqrt{N}$?} Under the assumption that log returns are i.i.d. (independent and identically distributed), variance scales linearly with time: $\sigma^2_T = T \cdot \sigma^2_1$. Therefore, the standard deviation scales with $\sqrt{T}$.

\end{quote}
\textbf{The assumption behind this:} Daily return changes are uncorrelated with each other (no serial correlation). If serial correlation exists, this scaling is incorrect and more sophisticated methods are needed.

\textbf{Two ways to measure rate change:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Absolute rate change:} The raw change in basis points (e.g., rate moves from 4.50\% to 4.56\% → absolute change = 6 bps). Reported in basis points.


\item \textbf{Percentage rate change:} $\ln(r_t / r_{t-1}) \times 100$. Analogous to log returns for stock prices. More appropriate for comparing volatility across different rate levels (a 1 bp move when rates are 1\% is proportionally much larger than a 1 bp move when rates are 10\%).


\end{enumerate}
\textbf{Numerical Example (from textbook: 1-month LIBOR, weekly data 2004--2005):}

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.19\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Measure} & \textbf{Weekly Std Dev} & \textbf{Annualized (× \(\sqrt{52}\))} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Measure} & \textbf{Weekly Std Dev} & \textbf{Annualized (× \(\sqrt{52}\))} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Absolute rate change & 2.62 basis points & \textbf{18.89 basis points} \\
Percentage rate change & 1.33\% & \textbf{9.62\%} \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\textbf{Trading days in a year --- practical impact:}

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.27\textwidth}L{0.66\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Days Assumed} & \textbf{$\sqrt{\text{Days}}$} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Days Assumed} & \textbf{$\sqrt{\text{Days}}$} \\
\midrule
\endhead
250 & 15.81 \\
252 & 15.87 \\
260 & 16.12 \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
For daily data, this choice of trading day convention can produce meaningfully different annualized volatility estimates --- consistency in methodology matters.

\medskip
\section{A History of Volatility Models}
Understanding where modern volatility models came from reveals \emph{why} they are structured the way they are and what problems each was designed to solve.

\subsection{Pre-Black-Scholes: Bachelier's Model (1900)}
\textbf{Louis Bachelier} presented "The Theory of Speculation" in 1900 --- the first known option pricing model, developed for pricing options on French government bonds.

\textbf{Key characteristics:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Uses \textbf{Arithmetic Brownian Motion (ABM):} The price change itself (not the percentage change) is normally distributed


\item $dS_t = \mu , dt + \sigma , dZ_t$ where $dZ_t$ is a Wiener process


\item Similar structure to Black-Scholes in probability framework and payoff analysis


\item Designed for options on \emph{spreads} (on futures contracts), not on absolute price levels


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Why ABM is problematic for stocks:} Under arithmetic BM, cumulative changes can theoretically bring the price below zero --- which is possible for a spread but impossible for a stock price. This limited the Bachelier model's applicability to standard equity options.

\textbf{Bachelier's surprising revival:} The model never became obsolete. In \textbf{April 2020}, WTI crude oil futures collapsed to \textbf{-\$40 per barrel} (negative prices!) due to storage constraints during COVID-19. CME Group switched its pricing model to Bachelier to accommodate negative strikes --- demonstrating the model's relevance 120 years after its creation. Similarly, in negative interest rate environments (EUR, JPY after the GFC), the Bachelier model is used because standard log-normal models cannot handle negative rates.

\medskip
\subsection{Black-Scholes Model (1973) --- The Cornerstone}
Fischer Black and Myron Scholes published their landmark paper in 1973. Myron Scholes received the Nobel Prize in Economics in 1997 (Fischer Black passed away in 1995, before the prize was awarded posthumously to Scholes; Robert Merton also received the Prize for related work).

\textbf{The key advancement over Bachelier:} B\&S uses \textbf{Geometric Brownian Motion (GBM)} instead of ABM:

\[
dS_t = \mu S_t \, dt + \sigma S_t \, dZ_t
\]

Or equivalently, the continuous GBM simulation formula: \[
dS_t = S_t \mu , dt + S_t \sigma \varepsilon \sqrt{\Delta t}, \quad \varepsilon \sim N(0,1)
\]

\textbf{Why GBM solves the negativity problem:} Because the volatility term is $\sigma S_t$ (proportional to price), when $S_t \to 0$, the stochastic term $\to 0$ as well. The price can approach zero but never cross it. This means \textbf{securities cannot change sign} (prices stay positive), which is economically correct for stocks.

\textbf{GBM log-return distribution:} GBM implies that \emph{log-returns} $\ln(S_t/S_0)$ are normally distributed: \[
\ln\left(\frac{S_t}{S_0}\right) \sim N\left(\left(\mu - \frac{\sigma^2}{2}\right)t, \ \sigma^2 t\right)
\]

And thus the price itself follows a \textbf{log-normal distribution}.

\textbf{The Monte Carlo illustration:} Running 10,000 GBM simulations from a starting price shows how the possible future price paths fan out over time --- with some paths rising dramatically and others falling, but none going negative. The spread of paths (the width of the "cone") represents volatility.

\subsubsection{Black-Scholes Assumptions}
\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.19\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{\#} & \textbf{Assumption} & \textbf{Status} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{\#} & \textbf{Assumption} & \textbf{Status} \\
\midrule
\endhead
1 & European options --- exercised only at expiration & Reasonable \\
2 & Risk-free asset exists (zero-coupon bond) for borrowing/investing & Reasonable \\
3 & No dividends or accrued interest during option's life & Approximate \\
4 & No transaction costs for trading or delta hedging & Approximate \\
5 & Underlying follows \textbf{continuous GBM}; returns are normally distributed; \textbf{no jumps} & \textbf{Empirically violated} \\
6 & Volatility is \textbf{constant} across all strikes and maturities & \textbf{Most problematic} \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\textbf{Assumptions 5 and 6 are the most critical violations:}

\emph{Assumption 5 (No jumps):} Real markets experience discontinuous jumps --- stock prices gap significantly on earnings surprises, macro events, geopolitical shocks. GBM assumes continuous paths, meaning large moves must happen gradually. In reality, they don't.

\emph{Assumption 6 (Constant volatility):} This is the \textbf{single most important limitation}, as it is definitively wrong empirically. Volatility changes over time and varies across strike prices and maturities. This violation gave rise to the entire field of volatility modeling.

\medskip
\subsection{The Volatility Surface: Theory vs. Reality}
Under Black-Scholes, since volatility is constant, the implied volatility extracted from \emph{any} option --- regardless of strike or expiration --- should be the same value \(\sigma\). The "volatility surface" (implied volatility plotted against strike and maturity) should be a \textbf{flat plane}.

\textbf{B\&S Theoretical Volatility Surface:}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.25cm,y=0.72cm]
\draw[diagramAxis] (0,0) -- (5.4,0) node[right]{Maturity};
\draw[diagramAxis] (0,0) -- (0,3.6) node[above]{Strike};
\foreach \x in {1,...,5}{
  \foreach \y in {1,...,3}{
    \node[font=\small] at (\x,\y) {$\sigma$};
  }
}
\draw[diagramBlue,thick] (0.6,0.6) rectangle (5.35,3.35);
\node[align=center,font=\small] at (2.95,-0.65)
{Black--Scholes prediction: constant implied volatility\\at every strike and maturity};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{A flat theoretical Black--Scholes volatility surface.}
\end{figure}
\textbf{Reality (S\&P500 Volatility Surface):} The actual surface is curved and tilted. It shows:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Volatility Skew/Smile:} IV varies across strikes

\begin{itemize}
\item For equity indices: IV is much higher for lower strikes (downside puts) than for higher strikes (upside calls) --- this is the \textbf{put skew} or "volatility skew"


\item For FX markets: roughly symmetric smile (higher IV for both very low and very high strikes)


\end{itemize}

\item \textbf{Term Structure:} IV varies across maturities


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Why the skew exists for equities:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Leverage Effect (Fischer Black, 1976):} When stock prices fall, companies become more levered (debt/equity rises), increasing default probability → higher volatility. This creates a \emph{negative correlation} between stock returns and volatility.


\item \textbf{Demand for downside protection:} Portfolio managers buy puts to hedge their equity portfolios → elevated demand for OTM puts → higher IV for low strikes


\item \textbf{Crash risk:} Investors fear sudden market crashes (fat left tail) and pay premium for protection


\end{enumerate}
\medskip
\subsection{The Stochastic Volatility Era (Post-1973)}
\textbf{The critical insight:} If volatility is not constant, model it as \emph{random}. Just as stock prices follow a random process, so does volatility.

\textbf{Key milestone --- 1976:} Latane and Rendelman published "Standard Deviations of Stock Price Ratios Implied in Option Prices" --- the first paper to suggest that \textbf{implied volatility should be derived from traded options} (called I.S.D. --- Implied Standard Deviation) rather than assumed as a constant input.

\textbf{Black Monday, October 19, 1987:} This event definitively demonstrated that the flat volatility surface assumption fails catastrophically. After Black Monday, out-of-the-money puts became persistently more expensive (higher IV) than at-the-money options --- the volatility skew became a permanent feature of equity markets.

\textbf{Stochastic Volatility: Core Idea} A stochastic volatility model assumes that \textbf{volatility itself follows a random process}, typically correlated with the returns of the underlying asset. Mathematically, the system becomes:

\[
dS_t = \mu S_t \, dt + \sqrt{v_t} \, S_t \, dW_t^1 \quad \text{(asset price)}
\]
\[
dv_t = \kappa(\theta - v_t) \, dt + \xi\sqrt{v_t} \, dW_t^2 \quad \text{(variance process)}
\]

Where:

\begin{itemize}
\item $v_t = \sigma_t^2$ is the instantaneous variance (also random!)


\item $\kappa$ = mean reversion speed of variance


\item $\theta$ = long-run mean variance


\item $\xi$ = volatility of volatility (vol-of-vol)


\item $dW_t^1$ and $dW_t^2$ are correlated: $\text{corr}(dW^1, dW^2) = \rho_{SV}$ (typically negative for equities)


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Heston (1993):} The foundational paper. Offered a \emph{closed-form} (exact) solution for option pricing under stochastic volatility. This is the model from which virtually all modern stochastic volatility research descends.

\medskip
\subsection{Asset Class Specific Volatility Models}
Each asset class has adopted models calibrated to its unique behavior:

\subsubsection{Interest Rates}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Standard models:} Vasicek (1977), Hull-White (1990) --- see Sections 8 and 9


\item \textbf{Modern standard: SABR model} (Hagan, Kumar, Lesniewski, Woodward --- 2002)

\begin{itemize}
\item A two-factor stochastic model where both the forward rate and its volatility follow random processes


\item Parameters:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{\(\alpha\) (alpha):} Controls the overall level of volatility (the scale)


\item \textbf{\(\beta\) (beta):} Controls the variance and shape of the volatility distribution --- specifically the "vol-of-vol." When \(\beta\) = 0: normal/Bachelier dynamics; when \(\beta\) = 1: log-normal dynamics


\item \textbf{\(\rho\) (rho):} Correlation between the spot rate and its volatility --- controls the slope of the volatility skew


\end{itemize}

\item \textbf{Special case --- Negative Rates:} Many interest rates now trade negative (EUR, CHF, JPY). Since Black-Scholes assumes log-normal dynamics (and prices/rates can't be negative), practitioners use the \textbf{Bachelier model} for negative-rate instruments. Example: After WTI crude oil hit -\$40 in April 2020, CME immediately switched its pricing model to Bachelier.


\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}
\subsubsection{Foreign Exchange (FX)}
\begin{itemize}
\item FX options are largely OTC (over-the-counter), enabling more customized structures


\item \textbf{Standard: Vanna-Volga (VV) pricing} (Malz 1997; Lipton \& McGhee 2002; Wystup 2003)

\begin{itemize}
\item Designed to handle exotic options: barrier options, digital payouts (one/no-touch, European digital)


\item \textbf{Core idea:} Quantify the hedging cost of second-order volatility derivatives (Greeks):

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Vanna} = $\frac{\partial \text{Vega}}{\partial S}$ = $\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial S \partial \sigma}$ --- sensitivity of Vega to spot price changes


\item \textbf{Volga} = $\frac{\partial \text{Vega}}{\partial \sigma}$ = $\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial \sigma^2}$ --- sensitivity of Vega to volatility changes (vol-of-vol)


\end{itemize}

\item The model assumes quoted option strategies reflect the implied risk-neutral probability:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Risk Reversal / Collar} → captures Vanna


\item \textbf{Butterfly spread} → captures Volga


\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}
\subsubsection{Equities and Commodities}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The Leverage Effect (Fischer Black, 1976):} Stocks can be thought of as call options on the underlying company assets (Merton, 1974). When stock prices fall, the company's debt/equity ratio rises → company becomes more leveraged → default probability increases → this creates a \textbf{negative correlation between stock returns and volatility}.

\emph{Practical implication:} Markets fall fast and hard (high vol when prices drop), but rise slowly (lower vol on the way up). This asymmetry is the foundation of the volatility skew in equity markets.


\item \textbf{CEV Model (Constant Elasticity of Variance):}

\begin{itemize}
\item Originally developed by John Cox (1975)


\item Extended by Linetsky and Mendoza (2009) to handle jumps, discontinuities, and a wide range of asset types including credit spreads


\item Volatility scales as a power of the asset price: $\sigma(S) = \sigma_0 \cdot S^{\gamma - 1}$


\item Captures the leverage effect through the parameter \(\gamma\) < 1 (when \(\gamma\) = 0: Bachelier/normal; when \(\gamma\) = 1: Black-Scholes log-normal)


\end{itemize}

\item \textbf{Local Volatility Models (Derman \& Kani 1994; Dupire 1994):}

\begin{itemize}
\item Instead of parameterizing volatility dynamics, \emph{extract} volatility directly from the observed option market


\item \textbf{Process:} Given traded options at different strikes and maturities, calculate implied volatility for each → interpolate to create a smooth continuous "local vol surface" $\sigma_{\text{local}}(S, t)$


\item The local volatility is a \emph{deterministic} function of the current asset price and time (not stochastic)


\item \textbf{Advantage:} Perfectly calibrated to current market option prices by construction


\item \textbf{Key limitation:} Local vol models are notoriously poor at capturing the \emph{dynamics} of the vol surface --- they tend to predict a flat smile in the future even when the current surface is skewed


\item More liquid option chain → more accurate local vol surface (better interpolation quality)


\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}
\medskip
\subsection{Rough Volatility --- The Frontier}
As volatility modeling matured, researchers began questioning whether GBM's fundamental assumptions hold for volatility itself.

\textbf{GBM's key property --- The Markov property:} GBM assumes the process has \textbf{no memory}: each increment is independent of all prior increments. Formally, this means volatility increments have \emph{zero autocorrelation}. The process grows proportional to $\sqrt{T}$ (the square root of time):

\[
dS_t = S_t \mu \, dt + S_t \sigma \varepsilon \sqrt{\Delta t}
\]

\textbf{The empirical question:} Do volatility increments actually have zero autocorrelation?

\textbf{The answer is NO.} Empirical evidence shows that volatility increments are \emph{negatively correlated} --- large jumps in volatility tend to be followed by reversals (rough, jagged behavior). This property cannot be captured by standard GBM.

\subsubsection{Fractional Brownian Motion (fBM)}
To model memory in the process, we use \textbf{fractional Brownian motion}, developed by Mandelbrot and Van Ness (1968). Unlike standard BM, fBM allows increments to be correlated.

\textbf{The Hurst Exponent H \(\in\) (0, 1)} characterizes the degree of autocorrelation:

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.15\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Value} & \textbf{Increments} & \textbf{Behavior} & \textbf{Process Type} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Value} & \textbf{Increments} & \textbf{Behavior} & \textbf{Process Type} \\
\midrule
\endhead
H = 0.5 & Uncorrelated & Standard Brownian Motion (GBM) & Memoryless \\
H < 0.5 & Negatively correlated & Rough, jagged time series; \textbf{mean-reverting} & Persistent anti-persistence \\
H > 0.5 & Positively correlated & Smooth time series; \textbf{trending} & Persistent \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
For equity volatility, empirical studies find H ≈ 0.1 to 0.2 --- very rough, strongly mean-reverting behavior.

\textbf{Fractality:} An important property of fBM is self-similarity across scales. Zooming in or out on the time series, the shape looks approximately the same (like snowflakes, coastlines, or cauliflower). In volatility analysis: intraday volatility patterns look similar to daily patterns, which look similar to weekly patterns.

\textbf{Gatheral, Jaisson, and Rosenbaum (2014):} This paper brought rough volatility to mainstream finance. Key findings:

\begin{itemize}
\item Equity index volatility has H ≈ 0.13 on long horizons


\item This means volatility exhibits \textbf{strong mean reversion in normal markets}


\item During periods of financial stress, H rises significantly toward 0.5 → can use H as a \textbf{regime change indicator}


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Applications of Rough Volatility:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Volatility Regime Detection:} Track the Hurst index over time. H is typically low (rough, mean-reverting) in calm markets and rises during crises (Lehman bankruptcy, Flash Crash, Greek debt crisis, Brexit vote all appear as spikes in H time series). Use H as an early warning system.


\item \textbf{Volatility Arbitrage (Glasserman \& He, 2018):} Strategy: Long positions in stocks with \emph{rough} volatility (high mean reversion) + short positions in stocks with \emph{smooth} volatility (trending vol) → earned excess risk-adjusted returns.


\item \textbf{S\&P500/VIX Joint Smile Calibration (Julien Guyon, 2020):} The most significant achievement in rough volatility research. No previous model could simultaneously calibrate the volatility smiles of both S\&P500 and VIX. Guyon demonstrated that rough volatility assumptions successfully solve this long-standing problem.


\end{enumerate}
\textbf{Why rough volatility models are computationally intensive:} Standard models (B\&S, Heston, SABR) are Markovian --- they have analytical or semi-analytical solutions. Rough volatility models are \textbf{non-Markovian} (they have memory), so no closed-form solution exists. \textbf{Monte Carlo simulation} is required, which is computationally expensive --- especially for practitioners needing real-time prices.

\textbf{Market microstructure basis:} The roughness of volatility is believed to stem from order flow dynamics:

\begin{itemize}
\item Buy orders generate more buy orders; sell orders generate more sell orders (autocorrelated order flow)


\item Sell orders have disproportionately large impact on prices relative to buy orders (liquidity asymmetry / leverage effect)


\item Most market activity is between professional trading firms (not end-users), amplifying these dynamics


\item The proliferation of HFT (high-frequency trading) makes the phenomenon more pronounced


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\section{Volatility Modeling Techniques}
\subsection{Historical Approaches (Backward-Looking)}
These methods estimate future volatility from past price data, assuming historical patterns repeat.

\textbf{Simple Historical Volatility:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Select a lookback window (e.g., 30 days, 60 days, 252 days)


\item Compute daily log returns, then their standard deviation


\item Use this as the volatility estimate going forward


\item \textbf{Advantage:} Simple; implementable in Excel


\item \textbf{Disadvantage:} Treats all past observations equally; slow to adapt to regime changes


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Simple Moving Average (SMA):}

\begin{itemize}
\item Compute the standard deviation over a rolling window of fixed length


\item As time passes, add the newest observation and drop the oldest


\item Captures the evolution of volatility over time better than a single fixed estimate


\item \textbf{Disadvantage:} Still treats all observations within the window equally; sharp cutoff at the window boundary


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Exponential Moving Average (EMA):}

\begin{itemize}
\item Gives exponentially declining weight to older observations --- more recent data matters more \[
\sigma^2_{t,\text{EMA}} = (1 - \lambda) \cdot r_t^2 + \lambda \cdot \sigma^2_{t-1,\text{EMA}}
\] where $\lambda \in (0, 1)$ is the \textbf{decay factor} (e.g., \(\lambda\) = 0.94 for daily data --- the RiskMetrics standard)


\item More responsive to recent volatility spikes or drops


\item Still backward-looking


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Common limitation of all historical methods:} The assumption that historical patterns will repeat is strong and often violated. These methods tend to \textbf{overfit historical data} --- they work well in-sample but can badly mislead out-of-sample if the volatility regime changes. Always perform rigorous out-of-sample testing.

\textbf{When to use:} Historical methods are appropriate when computational resources are limited, or when a quick estimate is needed. Suitable for non-research contexts.

\medskip
\subsection{Geometric Brownian Motion (GBM) as a Volatility Framework}
GBM, introduced earlier, provides the mathematical foundation for option pricing and volatility modeling. It is most commonly used in \textbf{asset pricing} and \textbf{option pricing} (as opposed to ARCH/GARCH which are more common in \textbf{econometric forecasting}).

\textbf{The GBM SDE:} \[
dS = \mu S , dt + \sigma S , dz
\]

\textbf{Components:}

\begin{itemize}
\item $\mu S , dt$ = Deterministic drift term (predictable growth)


\item $\sigma S , dz$ = Stochastic component (random fluctuations), where $dz \sim N(0, dt)$


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Key properties:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Returns $dS/S$ are normally distributed: $dS/S \sim N(\mu , dt, \ \sigma^2 , dt)$


\item Price S itself follows a \textbf{log-normal distribution}


\item Volatility \(\sigma\) is a \emph{parameter} --- constant in the basic GBM framework


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Simulation (Monte Carlo):} For option pricing when closed-form solutions are unavailable: \[
S_{t+\Delta t} = S_t \cdot \exp\left[\left(\mu - \frac{\sigma^2}{2}\right)\Delta t + \sigma \varepsilon \sqrt{\Delta t}\right], \quad \varepsilon \sim N(0, 1)
\]

\textbf{Use cases:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Black-Scholes option pricing


\item Monte Carlo simulation for complex derivatives


\item Asset path generation for risk management


\item Regulatory stress testing


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\section{The ARCH Model}
\subsection{Motivation: The Problem with Constant Variance}
Standard time series models --- AR (Autoregressive), MA (Moving Average), and ARIMA --- model the \textbf{conditional mean} of a time series. These models assume that the \textbf{residuals (error terms) have constant variance} (homoskedasticity).

However, in financial time series, this assumption is routinely violated. Consider daily returns of the S\&P 500 or the BP/Dollar exchange rate: periods of large volatility cluster together, followed by periods of calm. \textbf{Volatility clusters.}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\textbf{High-volatility regime:} & large moves tend to follow large moves,\\
\textbf{Low-volatility regime:} & small moves tend to follow small moves.
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
When variance changes systematically over time --- becoming larger in some periods and smaller in others --- we call the series \textbf{heteroskedastic} (unequal variance). If this changing variance is itself predictable from past information, we need a model that captures it.

\textbf{ARCH} (AutoRegressive Conditional Heteroskedasticity) was designed precisely for this.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Context:} Standard AR/ARIMA models cannot model changing variance. ARIMA models the conditional mean and assumes error terms have constant variance. ARCH instead models the \emph{variance} of those error terms as an autoregressive function of past squared errors.

\end{quote}
\textbf{Real-world applications where ARCH is used:} Stock prices, oil prices, bond prices, inflation rates, GDP, unemployment rates, exchange rates --- any financial or economic time series where volatility changes over time.

\medskip
\subsection{The ARCH Framework}
\textbf{ARCH = AutoRegressive Conditional Heteroskedasticity} Introduced by \textbf{Robert Engle (1982)} --- for which he was awarded the Nobel Prize in Economics in 2003.

\textbf{Decomposing the name:}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Autoregressive:} Current variance depends on (is a function of) past values --- there is serial dependence


\item \textbf{Conditional:} The variance is \emph{conditional on past information} --- not unconditional. It changes based on what happened in prior periods


\item \textbf{Heteroskedasticity:} The variance of the series is non-constant --- it varies over time


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Key insight:} ARCH distinguishes between:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Unconditional variance:} The overall long-run variance --- constant


\item \textbf{Conditional variance:} The variance at time t given all information up to t-1 --- time-varying


\end{itemize}
\textbf{When to apply ARCH:} ARCH should only be applied to a series that:

\begin{enumerate}
\item Has \textbf{no remaining trends or seasonal effects} --- the conditional mean is already modeled (i.e., residuals look like white noise in the mean)


\item Has \textbf{mean of residuals equal to zero} (or close to it)


\end{enumerate}
\textbf{Standard workflow:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item Fit an ARIMA (or similar) model to the raw time series to model the conditional mean


\item Examine the residuals: confirm they look like white noise (ACF/PACF of residuals show no serial correlation)


\item \textbf{Square the residuals}: if the ACF/PACF of \emph{squared} residuals show significant patterns → evidence of ARCH effects → apply ARCH to model the variance


\end{enumerate}
\medskip
\subsection{ARCH(1): The Basic Model}
\textbf{Model specification:}

The time series of residuals ${\varepsilon(t)}$ is written as: \[
\varepsilon(t) = w(t) \cdot \sigma(t)
\]

Where:

\begin{itemize}
\item $w(t)$ is \textbf{white noise}: $w(t) \sim N(0, 1)$, i.i.d. --- pure random shocks


\item $\sigma(t)$ is the \textbf{time-varying standard deviation} (conditional volatility)


\end{itemize}
The conditional variance evolves as: \[
\sigma^2(t) = \alpha_0 + \alpha_1 \cdot \varepsilon^2(t-1)
\]

Combining: \[
\boxed{\varepsilon(t) = w(t) \cdot \sqrt{\alpha_0 + \alpha_1 \cdot \varepsilon^2(t-1)}}
\]

\textbf{Constraints} (to ensure valid, non-negative variance and stationarity):

\begin{itemize}
\item $\alpha_0 > 0$ --- base variance must be positive


\item $\alpha_1 \geq 0$ --- non-negative weight on past shock


\item $\alpha_1 < 1$ --- stability condition; if $\alpha_1 \geq 1$, the variance grows without bound (explosive)


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Intuition --- Volatility Clustering:}

\begin{itemize}
\item If $|\varepsilon(t-1)|$ is large (big shock at t-1) → $\sigma^2(t) = \alpha_0 + \alpha_1 \varepsilon^2(t-1)$ is large → $|\varepsilon(t)|$ tends to be large → \textbf{high volatility follows high volatility}


\item If $|\varepsilon(t-1)|$ is small (quiet period at t-1) → $\sigma^2(t)$ is small → \textbf{calm follows calm}


\end{itemize}
This is exactly the volatility clustering pattern observed in financial markets.

\textbf{Why does ARCH model volatility?} From the formula: \[
\text{Var}(\varepsilon(t)) = \alpha_0 + \alpha_1 \cdot \text{Var}(\varepsilon(t-1))
\] The variance at time t is a \textbf{linear combination of the variance at t-1} --- so variance is time-varying and predictable from past variance.

\medskip
\subsection{ARCH(2) and ARCH(p): Higher Order Models}
\textbf{ARCH(2):} \[
\varepsilon(t) = w(t) \cdot \sqrt{\alpha_0 + \alpha_1 \varepsilon^2(t-1) + \alpha_2 \varepsilon^2(t-2)}
\]

\textbf{ARCH(p) --- General Form:} \[
\boxed{\varepsilon_t = w_t \cdot \sqrt{\alpha_0 + \sum_{i=1}^{p} \alpha_i \varepsilon^2_{t-i}}}
\]

The conditional variance: \[
\sigma^2_t = \alpha_0 + \sum_{i=1}^{p} \alpha_i \varepsilon^2_{t-i}
\]

\textbf{Parameters:}

\begin{itemize}
\item $p$ = order of the model = number of lagged squared residual terms included


\item $i = 1, 2, \ldots, p$ indexes the lag periods


\item Constraints: $\alpha_0 > 0$, $\alpha_i \geq 0$ for all $i$, and $\sum_{i=1}^{p} \alpha_i < 1$ for stationarity


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Interpretation:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item If the error was high in period (t-1), the expected error at period (t) is also higher → volatility persistence


\item Conversely, a low error at (t-1) implies lower expected error at (t)


\item $\alpha_1 \geq 0$ ensures positive conditional variance at all times


\item $\alpha_1 < 1$ prevents the process from exploding over time


\end{enumerate}
\medskip
\subsection{Configuring ARCH: ACF and PACF of Squared Residuals}
To determine the appropriate order p for the ARCH model:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Fit a mean model} (e.g., ARIMA) to the raw series; obtain residuals


\item \textbf{Square the residuals}: compute $\varepsilon^2_t$


\item \textbf{Plot ACF and PACF} of $\varepsilon^2_t$ (the squared residuals)


\item \textbf{Interpret:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Significant ACF at lag 1 only → ARCH(1) likely appropriate


\item Significant ACF at lags 1 and 2 → ARCH(2) possibly


\item The pattern is analogous to choosing the AR order in an AR model for the mean


\end{itemize}

\item The ACF and PACF patterns of squared residuals tell you how many past shocks significantly predict today's variance


\end{enumerate}
\begin{quote}
\textbf{Note:} The raw (unsquared) residuals should look like white noise --- no pattern. But the \emph{squared} residuals may show significant autocorrelation, revealing the ARCH structure. This is why ARCH is described as modeling variance rather than the mean.

\end{quote}
\medskip
\subsection{Python Implementation: ARCH(1)}
\begin{python}
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from arch import arch_model

np.random.seed(13)

# ARCH(1) parameters
a0 = 2    # base variance (must be > 0)
a1 = 0.5  # weight on lagged squared error (must be in [0,1) for stability)

# Simulate 1000 observations
y = w = np.random.normal(size=1000)
Y = np.empty_like(y)

for t in range(len(y)):
    Y[t] = w[t] * np.sqrt(a0 + a1 * y[t-1]**2)  # ARCH(1) formula
\end{python}
\textbf{Observation from simulating and plotting:}

\begin{itemize}
\item The raw ARCH(1) series looks like white noise (ACF of Y ≈ 0 at all lags)


\item But the \emph{squared} ARCH(1) series shows significant ACF at lag 1 → evidence of ARCH structure


\item This confirms: volatility is autocorrelated even when the level series appears uncorrelated


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\section{The GARCH Model}
\subsection{From ARCH to GARCH: Adding Persistence}
\textbf{GARCH = Generalized ARCH}, introduced by \textbf{Tim Bollerslev (1986)} in the Journal of Econometrics.

\textbf{The extension:} GARCH adds \textbf{past variance terms} ($\sigma^2_{t-j}$) to the ARCH specification, creating a model analogous to ARMA (vs. ARCH's AR-only structure):

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.27\textwidth}L{0.66\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Model (Mean)} & \textbf{Model (Variance)} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Model (Mean)} & \textbf{Model (Variance)} \\
\midrule
\endhead
AR (autoregressive) & ARCH \\
ARMA (autoregressive + moving average) & GARCH \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\textbf{Why the moving average component helps:}

\begin{itemize}
\item ARCH alone requires many lags (large p) to capture slowly decaying volatility persistence


\item The GARCH moving average (\(\beta\)) term captures this persistence parsimoniously --- with just one \(\beta\) term you can capture long-lived volatility clusters


\item GARCH permits a wider range of behavior and more realistic persistence in volatility


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\subsection{GARCH(1,1) --- The Workhorse}
\textbf{Model specification:} \[
\varepsilon(t) = w(t) \cdot \sigma(t)
\]

\[
\boxed{\sigma^2(t) = \alpha_0 + \alpha_1 \varepsilon^2(t-1) + \beta_1 \sigma^2(t-1)}
\]

Where:

\begin{itemize}
\item $\alpha_0 > 0$: long-run base variance (intercept)


\item $\alpha_1 \geq 0$: ARCH component --- weight on the most recent squared shock


\item $\beta_1 \geq 0$: GARCH component --- weight on the most recent variance estimate


\item $\alpha_1 + \beta_1 < 1$: stationarity condition (otherwise, variance becomes non-stationary / explosive)


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Interpreting GARCH(1,1):}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{$\alpha_1$ (ARCH term):} How quickly variance responds to new shocks. Large $\alpha_1$ → current volatility reacts sharply to recent news


\item \textbf{$\beta_1$ (GARCH term):} How persistent the variance is over time. Large $\beta_1$ → today's volatility is highly correlated with yesterday's → long memory in volatility


\item \textbf{$\alpha_1 + \beta_1$ (persistence):} Values close to 1 indicate highly persistent volatility --- shocks take a long time to decay


\item \textbf{Mean reversion speed of variance:} $(1 - \alpha_1 - \beta_1)$ --- the smaller this is, the slower variance reverts to its long-run mean


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Long-run (unconditional) variance:} \[
\bar{\sigma}^2 = \frac{\alpha_0}{1 - \alpha_1 - \beta_1}
\]

This is the level around which the conditional variance oscillates.

\textbf{Limiting cases:}

\begin{itemize}
\item $\beta_1 = 0$: Reduces to ARCH(1) --- no persistence in variance


\item $p = q = 0$: $\varepsilon(t)$ is simply white noise --- constant variance


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\subsection{GARCH(p,q) --- General Form}
A time series ${\varepsilon(t)}$ with $\varepsilon(t) = w(t) \cdot \sigma(t)$ and conditional variance:

\[
\boxed{\sigma^2_t = \alpha_0 + \sum_{i=1}^{q} \alpha_i \varepsilon^2_{t-i} + \sum_{j=1}^{p} \beta_j \sigma^2_{t-j}}
\]

\textbf{Constraints:} $\alpha_0 > 0$, $\alpha_i \geq 0$ for $i=1,\ldots,q$, $\beta_j \geq 0$ for $j=1,\ldots,p$

\textbf{Interpretive notes:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item A large $\beta_1$ causes $\sigma(t)$ to be highly correlated with $\sigma^2(t-1)$ → long-term persistence in conditional standard deviation


\item If $p = 0$: reduces to ARCH(q) --- no variance lag terms


\item If $p = q = 0$: $\varepsilon(t)$ is simply white noise


\item The moving average component allows the model to capture both conditional increases and decreases in variance


\end{enumerate}
\medskip
\subsection{GARCH Configuration: ACF and PACF}
\textbf{Diagnostic process:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item Fit appropriate mean model → obtain residuals (ensure mean is zero, no serial correlation)


\item Square the residuals: $\varepsilon^2_t$


\item Plot ACF and PACF of $\varepsilon^2_t$


\item Interpret:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{ACF cuts off after lag 1, PACF decays slowly} → MA structure in variance → ARCH-like


\item \textbf{Both ACF and PACF show extended autocorrelation} → Both AR and MA components needed → \textbf{GARCH model appropriate}


\end{itemize}

\item The ARIMA orders for the best-fitting mean model can guide GARCH order selection (e.g., ARIMA(3,0,2) residuals → try GARCH(3,2))


\end{enumerate}
\medskip
\subsection{Heavy Tails in GARCH}
A notable property: even when the innovations ${w(t)}$ are assumed to be \textbf{Gaussian} (normally distributed), GARCH processes produce \textbf{heavy tails} in the return distribution. This happens because the time-varying variance creates occasional very large returns (large $\sigma_t$ at some time steps).

This is a desirable property because:

\begin{itemize}
\item Empirical financial returns have heavier tails than a Gaussian distribution (known as "fat tails" or excess kurtosis)


\item GARCH naturally accommodates this even with normal innovations


\item For even heavier tails, use non-Gaussian innovations (e.g., Student's t-distribution, GED)


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\subsection{Python Implementation: GARCH(1,1)}
\begin{python}
import numpy as np
from arch import arch_model

np.random.seed(1)

# GARCH(1,1) parameters
a0 = 0.2   # base variance
a1 = 0.5   # ARCH component (alpha_1)
b1 = 0.3   # GARCH component (beta_1)
# Note: a1 + b1 = 0.8 < 1 -> stationary

n = 10000
w = np.random.normal(size=n)
eps = np.zeros_like(w)
sigsq = np.zeros_like(w)

for i in range(1, n):
    sigsq[i] = a0 + a1 * (eps[i-1]**2) + b1 * sigsq[i-1]  # GARCH(1,1)
    eps[i] = w[i] * np.sqrt(sigsq[i])
\end{python}
\textbf{Fitting a GARCH model to real data (SPY returns):}

\begin{python}
from arch import arch_model

am = arch_model(eps)      # default: GARCH(1,1) with constant mean
res = am.fit(update_freq=5)
print(res.summary())
\end{python}
\textbf{Practical workflow for real financial data:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item Get log returns: \texttt{lrets = np.log(data / data.shift(1)).dropna()}


\item Find best ARIMA(p,d,q) model by iterating over orders, minimizing AIC


\item Examine ACF/PACF of squared residuals from ARIMA


\item Fit GARCH using the ARIMA orders as a guide: e.g., ARIMA(3,0,2) → try GARCH(3,2)


\item Evaluate: residuals should look like white noise; squared residuals should show no remaining autocorrelation


\end{enumerate}
\textbf{Example output for SPY returns (2012--2015), best model ARIMA(3,0,2) → GARCH(3,2):} The fitted GARCH coefficients (omega = \(\alpha\)₀, alpha[1] = \(\alpha\)₁, beta[1] = \(\beta\)₁) confirm statistically significant volatility clustering in equity returns.

\medskip
\subsection{GARCH Family --- Extensions}
\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.27\textwidth}L{0.66\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Key Feature} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Key Feature} \\
\midrule
\endhead
ARCH-M & Return modeled as a function of its own variance (risk-return tradeoff) \\
TGARCH (Threshold GARCH) & Asymmetric response: negative shocks affect variance more than positive (leverage effect) \\
EGARCH & Exponential GARCH; no non-negativity constraints; captures asymmetry naturally \\
GJR-GARCH & Similar to TGARCH; indicator function captures leverage effect \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\medskip
\subsection{ARCH vs. GARCH vs. GBM: When to Use Which}
\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.19\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Best Application} & \textbf{Sophistication Needed} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Best Application} & \textbf{Sophistication Needed} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Simple historical vol & Quick estimates, limited computing & Low (Excel) \\
SMA / EMA & Monitoring volatility regimes & Low \\
ARCH(p) & Modeling short-memory vol clustering & Moderate (stats software) \\
GARCH(p,q) & Modeling persistent vol clustering (research) & Moderate--High \\
GBM / B\&S & Option pricing, asset simulation & Moderate (math) \\
Vasicek / CIR & Interest rate dynamics & Moderate--High (SDEs, regression) \\
Heston / SABR & Stochastic vol surface calibration & High \\
Rough Volatility & Frontier vol research, regime detection & Very High (Monte Carlo) \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\begin{quote}
\textbf{Practical guideline:} Historical approaches (SMA/EMA) are used by practitioners without sophisticated computing resources. ARCH/GARCH are used in research-driven financial firms for forecasting. GBM and SDE-based models (Vasicek, CIR, Hull-White) are used in asset pricing, derivatives, and regulatory modeling.

\end{quote}
\medskip
\section{Interest Rate Modeling}
\subsection{Why Interest Rates Need Their Own Models}
Interest rates influence option prices through the Rho Greek --- but they are also fundamental underlying assets in the fixed income world. To price:

\begin{itemize}
\item Bond portfolios and their risk


\item Interest rate derivatives: caps, floors, swaptions, options on bonds


\item Mortgage-backed securities


\item Credit risk capital (via Vasicek credit model)


\end{itemize}
…you need a model for how interest rates evolve over time. This is an entirely separate challenge from equity modeling because interest rates behave differently from stock prices in several important ways:

\begin{itemize}
\item Interest rates are \textbf{mean-reverting} (they don't drift up or down indefinitely)


\item They tend toward a long-run equilibrium tied to economic fundamentals


\item The entire \textbf{term structure} (yield curve across maturities) must be consistent


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\subsection{Properties of Interest Rate Processes}
Any sound interest rate model must capture three statistical properties:

\textbf{1. Drift (b)} The expected direction of change in interest rates. Captures the predictable component of rate movement --- including mean reversion toward equilibrium.

\textbf{2. Volatility (\(\sigma\))} The magnitude of random fluctuations in interest rates. Captures the uncertainty and randomness in rate movements --- analogous to \(\sigma\) in a stock price model.

\textbf{3. Mean Reversion} The tendency for interest rates to return to a long-run equilibrium level $\bar{r}$. This is one of the most economically important properties:

\begin{itemize}
\item Very high rates → discourage borrowing → reduce economic activity → policy pressure to reduce rates


\item Very low rates → stimulate borrowing, potentially causing inflation → eventual pressure to raise rates


\item Central bank inflation targeting anchors the long-run rate


\item Economic business cycles create cyclical rate dynamics


\end{itemize}
Mean reversion is the feature that distinguishes interest rate models from stock price models. Stock prices have no economic reason to revert to any level --- but interest rates do.

\medskip
\subsection{Stochastic Differential Equations (SDEs) --- The Mathematical Tool}
The standard mathematical tool for modeling continuous-time interest rate dynamics is the \textbf{Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE)}.

\textbf{Basic SDE for the short rate:} \[
dr = b , dt + \sigma , dz
\]

Where:

\begin{itemize}
\item $dr$ = infinitesimal change in the short rate


\item $b$ = \textbf{drift term} (expected direction of rate change)


\item $\sigma$ = \textbf{volatility term} (standard deviation of rate changes)


\item $dz$ = random \textbf{Wiener process} (or Brownian motion) increment: $dz \sim N(0, dt)$


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Interpretation:} The change in the short rate = a predictable component ($b , dt$) + a random shock ($\sigma , dz$).

\textbf{Key statistical properties under this SDE:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Expected change: $E[dr] = b$ (the drift)


\item Variance of change: $\text{Var}(dr) = \sigma^2 , dt$


\item For a fixed interval of length T (with drift = 0, \(\sigma\) = 1): $\text{Var} = T$, so standard deviation $= \sqrt{T}$


\item Rate changes in different time periods are \textbf{independent} (Markov property)


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Standard Wiener Process:} The special case $b = 0$, $\sigma = 1$ gives $dr = dz$ --- a \textbf{standard Wiener process}. This is the building block of all continuous-time finance models.

\textbf{The Itô Process (Generalized SDE):} \[
dr = b(r,t) , dt + \sigma(r,t) , dz
\]

Here the drift $b(r,t)$ and volatility $\sigma(r,t)$ can both depend on the current rate level $r$ and time $t$. This is the most general single-factor interest rate model form.

Note: SDEs underlie not just interest rate models but also the Black-Scholes formula (described in Chapter 30 of the textbook) and all derivative pricing.

\medskip
\subsection{Specifying the Drift: Mean Reversion}
The mean-reversion drift is specified as: \[
b(r,t) = -\alpha(r - \bar{r})
\]

Where:

\begin{itemize}
\item $\alpha > 0$ = \textbf{speed of adjustment} (must be positive for stability)


\item $\bar{r}$ = \textbf{long-run stable mean} (equilibrium level)


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Mechanism:}

\begin{itemize}
\item If $r > \bar{r}$: $b = -\alpha(r - \bar{r}) < 0$ → drift is negative → rate is pulled downward toward $\bar{r}$


\item If $r < \bar{r}$: $b = -\alpha(r - \bar{r}) > 0$ → drift is positive → rate is pushed upward toward $\bar{r}$


\item If $r = \bar{r}$: $b = 0$ → no drift (rate is at equilibrium)


\end{itemize}
The larger $\alpha$, the faster the rate reverts to $\bar{r}$.

\medskip
\subsection{Specifying the Volatility: The CEV Framework}
The dynamics of the volatility term can be specified using the \textbf{Constant Elasticity of Variance (CEV)} model:

\[
\sigma(r,t) = \sigma \cdot r^{\gamma}
\]

The parameter $\gamma$ determines how volatility scales with the level of interest rates:

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.15\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{\(\gamma\)} & \textbf{Volatility Specification} & \textbf{Model Name} & \textbf{Key Property} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{\(\gamma\)} & \textbf{Volatility Specification} & \textbf{Model Name} & \textbf{Key Property} \\
\midrule
\endhead
0 & $\sigma(r,t) = \sigma$ & \textbf{Vasicek} (Normal model) & Volatility independent of rate level; \textbf{negative rates possible} \\
1 & $\sigma(r,t) = \sigma r$ & \textbf{Dothan} (Proportional vol) & Volatility proportional to rate; no negative rates \\
½ & $\sigma(r,t) = \sigma\sqrt{r}$ & \textbf{CIR} (Square-root model) & Volatility proportional to \(\sqrt{r}\); \textbf{no negative rates} \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\textbf{Normal models (\(\gamma\) = 0):} Volatility is constant in \emph{basis points} --- independent of the rate level. This corresponds to the Vasicek model. The rate changes are normally distributed. A drawback is that negative rates are theoretically possible (though in the Vasicek model, this probability is small for typical parameters).

\textbf{Lognormal models (\(\gamma\) = 1):} Volatility is proportional to the rate level --- interest-rate changes are proportional to the current level of rates. This corresponds to the Dothan model. Negative rates are impossible (as rate approaches zero, volatility also approaches zero). This is analogous to GBM for stock prices.

\textbf{The CIR specification (\(\gamma\) = ½):} Intermediate behavior --- a square-root relationship between rate level and volatility. Eliminates negative rates while being more tractable than the full lognormal.

\textbf{Which model to use? --- Empirical evidence:} Research by Cheyette (examining Treasury yields from 1977 to 1996) and Levin (10-year Treasury and swap rates, 1980--2003) found:

\begin{itemize}
\item For rates \textbf{below 10\%}: volatility is approximately \textbf{independent of the rate level} → normal model (Vasicek, Hull-White) is appropriate


\item For rates \textbf{above 10\%} (e.g., the high-rate environment of 1979--1982): positive correlation between vol and rate level → lognormal models may be more appropriate


\item \textbf{Practical conclusion:} Since rates have remained below 10\% in most developed markets since the 1980s, \textbf{normal models are the dominant choice in practice}


\end{itemize}
\textbf{On negative rates:} While normal models admit the theoretical possibility of negative rates, empirical testing shows that the probability of generating negative rates is very small for typical parameter values and initial term structures. Tests of option pricing with normal models (Cheyette, Levin) confirm that negative rates have \textbf{minimal impact on pricing accuracy} --- so this concern should not be overstated.

\medskip
\subsection{One-Factor Interest Rate Models}
\textbf{Definition:} A one-factor model assumes that \textbf{all interest rates across all maturities} are driven by a \textbf{single stochastic factor} --- the short-term interest rate (the "short rate" r).

Once the short rate r is specified at time t, the rates for all other maturities are determined through no-arbitrage arguments. The short rate is the \emph{only} random variable --- everything else is a deterministic function of it.

\textbf{The standard one-factor model SDE:} \[
dr = b(r,t) , dt + \sigma(r,t) , dz
\]

\textbf{Why one-factor models in practice:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Mathematical tractability:} One-factor models often yield closed-form (or semi-closed-form) solutions for bond and option prices


\item \textbf{Parsimony:} A single factor reduces the number of parameters to estimate


\item \textbf{Empirical justification:} The correlation between rate changes across different maturities is very high. A \textbf{level shift} in the yield curve (parallel shift up or down) explains the \emph{majority} of yield curve variation. One-factor models capture this dominant movement well.


\end{enumerate}
\textbf{Limitation:} One-factor models cannot capture the \emph{shape} of the yield curve changing (twists, butterflies). They should \textbf{not} be used for instruments whose payoff depends on the shape of the term structure (e.g., dual-index floaters, yield curve options, steepener/flattener trades).

\textbf{Multi-factor models:} Two-factor models (short rate + long rate as factors) are richer but significantly harder to implement. In practice, the difficulty of implementing even a two-factor model means one-factor models dominate.

\medskip
\subsection{The Full One-Factor Mean-Reverting Model}
Combining mean-reverting drift with the CIR square-root volatility:

\[
\boxed{dr = -a(r - \bar{r}) \, dt + \sigma\sqrt{r} \, dz}
\]

This is the \textbf{Mean-Reverting Square-Root Model} --- also known as the \textbf{CIR (Cox-Ingersoll-Ross) Model}. It is an equilibrium model with:

\begin{itemize}
\item Mean-reverting drift pushing toward $\bar{r}$


\item Volatility proportional to $\sqrt{r}$ → no negative rates


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\subsection{Arbitrage-Free vs. Equilibrium Models}
Interest rate models fall into two fundamental categories based on how they are designed:

\textbf{Equilibrium Models:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Describe the \emph{dynamics} of interest rates using fundamental economic variables and assumptions
