\item Parameters are estimated from historical data (e.g., using OLS or MLE on historical yield data)


\item Derive closed-form solutions for bond and option prices


\item \textbf{Key limitation:} They are \emph{not} calibrated to current market prices. The model may produce prices inconsistent with what you can actually buy or sell in the market today --- potentially creating arbitrage opportunities.


\item Examples: \textbf{Vasicek (1977), CIR (1985), Brennan-Schwartz, Longstaff-Schwartz}


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Arbitrage-Free Models (No-Arbitrage Models):}

\begin{itemize}
\item Begin with \textbf{observed market prices} of benchmark instruments (e.g., Treasury bonds)


\item Assume these observed prices are correct (fairly priced)


\item Find the interest rate process that \emph{reproduces} these prices exactly


\item Any non-benchmark instrument is then priced using this calibrated process


\item \textbf{Key property:} By construction, the model matches current market prices → no arbitrage opportunity relative to the benchmarks


\item Examples: \textbf{Ho-Lee (1986), Hull-White (1990), KWF, Black-Karasinski, BDT, HJM}


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Key arbitrage-free models:}

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.15\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Mean Reversion} & \textbf{Volatility} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Mean Reversion} & \textbf{Volatility} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\textbf{Ho-Lee (1986)} & No & Normal (constant) & First arbitrage-free model; \(\phi\)(t) time-varying drift \\
\textbf{Hull-White (1990)} & Yes & Normal (constant) & First arbitrage-free + mean-reverting normal model; widely used \\
\textbf{KWF} & No & Lognormal & Models ln(r); used for bonds with embedded options \\
\textbf{Black-Karasinski} & Yes & Lognormal & Lognormal generalization of KWF \\
\textbf{BDT (Black-Derman-Toy)} & Yes (endogenous) & Lognormal & Mean reversion determined by market conditions \\
\textbf{HJM (Heath-Jarrow-Morton, 1992)} & --- & General & Multi-factor, continuous time; models forward rates; many models are special cases \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\textbf{Hull-White Model} is particularly important in practice:

\begin{itemize}
\item It extends Ho-Lee by adding mean reversion


\item It is the first arbitrage-free, mean-reverting, normal model


\item It is widely used for MBS (mortgage-backed securities) pricing (validated by Levin for this application)


\end{itemize}
\textbf{HJM Framework:} Instead of modeling the short rate, HJM models the \emph{entire forward rate curve} simultaneously. It requires only a specification of the volatility structure of forward rates --- no utility function assumptions needed. Many other models (Ho-Lee, Hull-White, etc.) are special cases of HJM.

\textbf{Why arbitrage-free models are preferred in practice:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Equilibrium models may produce prices inconsistent with what can be observed in the market today → creates apparent arbitrage opportunities


\item Arbitrage-free models are calibrated to current market prices → consistent with observable reality


\item However, equilibrium models provide better economic insight and are more useful for long-horizon forecasting


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\section{The Vasicek Interest Rate Model}
\subsection{Overview}
The \textbf{Vasicek model}, developed by \textbf{Oldřich Vašíček (1977)}, was one of the first rigorous analytical models for interest rate dynamics. It is:

\begin{itemize}
\item A \textbf{one-factor model}: driven by the short-term interest rate


\item An \textbf{equilibrium model}: parameters estimated from historical data


\item A \textbf{mean-reverting model}: economically grounded in the stability of long-run equilibrium rates


\item A \textbf{normal model}: volatility is independent of the rate level (\(\gamma\) = 0 in CEV framework)


\end{itemize}
It represents the application of the \textbf{Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) process} from physics (used to model the velocity of a particle under friction) to interest rates.

\medskip
\subsection{The Vasicek SDE}
\[
\boxed{dr_t = a(b - r_t) \, dt + \sigma \, dW_t}
\]

\textbf{Parameters and their roles:}

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.15\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Description} & \textbf{Constraint} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Description} & \textbf{Constraint} \\
\midrule
\endhead
$a$ & Speed of mean reversion & How quickly the rate reverts to equilibrium & $a > 0$ \\
$b$ & Long-term mean (equilibrium) & The level rates gravitate toward in the long run & --- \\
$r_t$ & Current short rate & The interest rate at time t & --- \\
$\sigma$ & Volatility & Standard deviation of random interest rate changes & $\sigma > 0$ \\
$W_t$ & Wiener process & The source of randomness (market risk factor) & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
The model has \textbf{two components}:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Deterministic drift:} $a(b - r_t) , dt$ --- pulls the rate toward equilibrium $b$


\item \textbf{Stochastic component:} $\sigma , dW_t$ --- random market shock


\end{enumerate}
\medskip
\subsection{Mean Reversion Mechanics}
The drift term $a(b - r_t)$ acts like a spring --- the further the rate deviates from equilibrium, the stronger the restoring force:

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.19\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Drift Sign} & \textbf{Market Behavior} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Drift Sign} & \textbf{Market Behavior} \\
\midrule
\endhead
$r_t > b$ (rate above equilibrium) & Negative ($< 0$) & Rate pulled downward → forms \textbf{resistance level} \\
$r_t < b$ (rate below equilibrium) & Positive ($> 0$) & Rate pushed upward → forms \textbf{support level} \\
$r_t = b$ (at equilibrium) & Zero & No systematic drift \\
$dW_t = 0$ (no market shocks) & --- & Rate equals long-term mean exactly \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\textbf{Resistance and support:}

\begin{itemize}
\item When the rate increases above $b$ and then mean-reverts back down to $b$: this forms a \textbf{resistance level} at the high point


\item When the rate decreases below $b$ and bounces back to $b$: this forms a \textbf{support level} at the low point


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Stability condition:} $a$ must be strictly positive ($a > 0$). If $a \leq 0$, the drift would amplify deviations from equilibrium rather than damping them → unstable process.

\medskip
\subsection{Discrete Form and OLS Estimation}
The continuous SDE is hard to estimate directly. We convert it to a \textbf{discrete form} suitable for Ordinary Least Squares (OLS) regression.

\textbf{Discretizing the Vasicek SDE:} Replace the continuous derivative $\frac{dr}{dt}$ with the first difference $\Delta r_t = r_t - r_{t-1}$ and time step $\Delta t$:

\[
\Delta r_t = a(b - r_{t-1})\Delta t + \sigma \varepsilon_t
\]
\[
= (ab)\Delta t - a \cdot r_{t-1} \cdot \Delta t + \sigma \varepsilon_t
\]

For weekly data ($\Delta t = 1$ week), this becomes a simple linear regression:

\[
\boxed{\Delta r_t = \alpha_0 + \alpha_1 \cdot r_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t}
\]

Where:

\begin{itemize}
\item $\alpha_0 = a \cdot b$ (intercept estimates the product of speed and equilibrium)


\item $\alpha_1 = -a$ (slope estimates the \emph{negative} of the speed of mean reversion)


\item $\varepsilon_t \sim N(0, \sigma^2)$ i.i.d. (in the Vasicek model)


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Extracting the structural parameters from OLS:} \[
a = -\hat{\alpha}_1 \quad \text{(speed of reversion)}
\] \[
b=\frac{\hat{\alpha}_0}{a}
 =\frac{\hat{\alpha}_0}{-\hat{\alpha}_1}
 \quad \text{(long-run equilibrium)}
\] \[
\sigma=\hat{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}
\quad \text{(residual standard error from OLS)}
\]

\textbf{Implementation steps:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item Compute first differences: $\Delta r_t = r_t - r_{t-1}$


\item Create lagged rate column: $r_{t-1}$


\item Regress $\Delta r_t$ on constant + $r_{t-1}$ using OLS (LINEST in Excel, or \texttt{np.linalg.lstsq} / \texttt{statsmodels.OLS} in Python)


\item Test significance: compute t-statistics (coefficient ÷ std error); check p-values


\item Extract $a$, $b$, $\sigma$


\end{enumerate}
\textbf{Real-world Example (US Weekly Mortgage Rates, 2010--present, 587 observations):}

\begin{itemize}
\item Regression confirms: \textbf{both coefficients statistically significant at 5\%}


\item Estimated slope $\hat{\alpha}_1 \approx -0.01$ per week → \textbf{$a \approx 0.01$ per week}

\begin{itemize}
\item Interpretation: each week the rate closes approximately 1\% of its gap from equilibrium


\end{itemize}

\item \textbf{$b \approx 3.05\%$} (long-run equilibrium mortgage rate)


\item \textbf{Economic sanity check:} With a 2\% long-run US inflation target, a 3.05\% nominal mortgage rate implies a real rate of ~1\%, which is economically reasonable


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\subsection{Forecasting Mean with the Vasicek Model}
Given current rate $r_t$, the expected interest rate $T$ periods in the future:

\[
\boxed{E[r_{t+T}] = r_t \cdot e^{-aT} + b \cdot (1 - e^{-aT})}
\]

\textbf{Mathematical intuition:}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{First term} $r_t \cdot e^{-aT}$: The current rate's influence, which decays exponentially at rate $a$ over time


\item \textbf{Second term} $b \cdot (1 - e^{-aT})$: The long-run equilibrium's contribution, which grows as the current rate's influence fades


\item The two terms sum to a weighted average of $r_t$ and $b$, with weights $e^{-aT}$ and $(1 - e^{-aT})$ respectively


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Long-run limit:} As $T \to \infty$: $e^{-aT} \to 0$, so $E[r_{t+T}] \to b$ --- the forecast converges to the long-run equilibrium regardless of where rates are today.

\textbf{Short-run limit:} For small $T$: the current rate $r_t$ dominates (the $e^{-aT} \approx 1$ for small $aT$).

\textbf{Example:}

\begin{itemize}
\item $r_t = 3.63\%$, $a = 0.01$ (weekly), $b = 3.05\%$, $T = 52$ weeks \[
E[r_{t+52}] = 3.63 \times e^{-0.52} + 3.05 \times (1 - e^{-0.52})
\] \[
= 3.63 \times 0.595 + 3.05 \times 0.405 \approx 2.16 + 1.24 \approx 3.37\%
\]


\item Long-run forecast: as $T \to \infty$, rate gravitates toward $b = 3.05\%$


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\subsection{Volatility Forecasting with the Vasicek Model}
The Vasicek model provides a \textbf{closed-form formula} for the variance of the interest rate over a future period T:

\[
\text{Var}(r_{t+T}) = \frac{\sigma^2}{2a} \cdot \left(1 - e^{-2aT}\right)
\]

\textbf{Volatility (standard deviation):} \[
\text{Vol}(r_{t+T}) = \sigma \cdot \sqrt{\frac{1 - e^{-2aT}}{2a}}
\]

\textbf{Long-run (T → ∞) limits:} As $T \to \infty$: $e^{-2aT} \to 0$, so: \[
\operatorname{Var}_{\infty}=\frac{\sigma^2}{2a}
\qquad \text{(long-run variance)}
\] \[
\operatorname{Vol}_{\infty}=\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2a}}
\qquad \text{(long-run volatility)}
\]

\textbf{Effect of parameters on volatility:}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Larger \(\sigma\):} Higher instantaneous randomness → higher variance at all horizons (direct, proportional effect)


\item \textbf{Smaller $a$ (slower convergence):} The rate can deviate from equilibrium for a longer time before being pulled back → larger long-run variance. Intuition: a weak spring (small $a$) allows larger swings.


\item \textbf{Larger $a$ (faster convergence):} Shocks are absorbed quickly → smaller long-run variance. Intuition: a stiff spring (large $a$) quickly snaps back, limiting excursions.


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Summary:} $\text{Vol}_\infty = \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2a}}$ --- long-run volatility is determined by the ratio of instantaneous vol to the square root of mean reversion speed. Higher shock intensity AND slower reversion → higher uncertainty.

\textbf{Example:}

\begin{itemize}
\item $\sigma = 0.003$ (weekly vol from regression), $a = 0.01$


\item 52-week variance: $\frac{0.003^2}{2 \times 0.01}(1 - e^{-2 \times 0.01 \times 52}) \approx 0.00045 \times 0.648 \approx 0.000292$


\item 52-week volatility: $\sqrt{0.000292} \approx 0.017$ = \textbf{1.7\% (or about 36 basis points)}


\item Long-run volatility: $\frac{0.003}{\sqrt{0.02}} \approx \frac{0.003}{0.141} \approx 0.021$ = \textbf{2.1\% (≈ 43.5 bps)}


\end{itemize}
\emph{Interpretation:} Over 1 year, the expected mortgage rate is 3.37\%, with an average deviation of about 36 basis points from this forecast. In the very long run, rates are expected to hover near 3.05\% with a volatility of about 43.5 basis points.

\medskip
\subsection{Applications of the Vasicek Model}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Interest Rate Forecasting:} Generate mean forecasts and confidence intervals for future rate levels, informing investment decisions in fixed income


\item \textbf{Bond Pricing and Risk:} Derive the full term structure of interest rates from the estimated parameters; quantify the volatility and risk of fixed-rate bonds over a holding period


\item \textbf{Derivatives Pricing:} Price interest rate options (caps, floors, bond options) using the derived rate distribution


\item \textbf{Regulatory Capital --- Credit Risk:} Used by regulators (Basel II/III) to calculate the worst-case default rate for loan portfolios and hence the capital requirement (see Section 10)


\item \textbf{Risk Management:} For a fixed-rate bond portfolio, use the Vasicek-derived interest rate distribution to quantify risk (Value-at-Risk, stress tests)


\end{enumerate}
\medskip
\subsection{Limitations of the Vasicek Model}
\textbf{1. Single-Factor:} Only one source of randomness (the market risk factor $dW_t$). Real interest rates are affected by multiple factors --- monetary policy surprises, inflation expectations, credit risk, global capital flows. The model cannot capture the relative movement of different points on the yield curve (twists, butterflies).

\textbf{2. Negative Interest Rates:} Since $\sigma$ is constant (not proportional to $r$), the model allows rates to go negative with some probability. In standard rate environments this probability is very small, and empirical evidence suggests this has minimal impact on pricing --- but it is a theoretical limitation. The CIR model avoids this.

\textbf{3. Constant Parameters:} $a$, $b$, and $\sigma$ are assumed constant over time. In reality:

\begin{itemize}
\item The long-run equilibrium $b$ can shift (e.g., from one monetary policy regime to another)


\item Volatility $\sigma$ can change (e.g., during crises)


\item The speed of adjustment $a$ may vary


\end{itemize}
\textbf{4. Equilibrium vs. Arbitrage-Free:} As an equilibrium model, the Vasicek model is calibrated to historical data, not to current market prices. This means it may produce theoretical bond prices that differ from observable market prices, potentially implying arbitrage opportunities. In practice, arbitrage-free models (Hull-White) are preferred for derivatives pricing.

\medskip
\section{The Vasicek Model for Credit Risk}
\subsection{The Problem: Portfolio-Level Default Risk}
The Vasicek model has an important application beyond interest rate modeling: \textbf{calculating regulatory capital for bank loan portfolios} under Basel II/III's Internal Ratings-Based (IRB) approach.

\textbf{The central question:} A bank has 2,000 corporate loans. Each loan has some individual probability of default. But the \emph{portfolio}'s worst-case outcome is not simply the sum of individual probabilities --- because defaults are \textbf{correlated}. When the economy turns bad, many borrowers default simultaneously.

\textbf{Why correlation matters:}

\begin{itemize}
\item If each loan's default is \textbf{independent} of all others: the Law of Large Numbers applies; the portfolio default rate converges to the average PD. No capital needed beyond expected loss provisions.


\item If defaults are \textbf{positively correlated} (systemic risk): bad economic conditions cause many defaults simultaneously. The portfolio can experience catastrophic losses far exceeding the average PD. \textbf{Capital must be held to absorb this.}


\end{itemize}
The Vasicek framework provides an \emph{analytical} (closed-form) solution for the worst-case portfolio default rate, making it far more tractable than Monte Carlo simulation for regulatory purposes.

\medskip
\subsection{The Gaussian Copula and Factor Structure}
\textbf{Fundamental modeling approach:} Each borrower's "creditworthiness" can be represented by a latent variable. Even if individual loan loss distributions are not normal, we can transform them to standard normal distributions. The joint distribution is then modeled using a \textbf{Gaussian copula} --- a mathematical tool for constructing a joint distribution from individual (possibly non-normal) marginals.

\textbf{Factor decomposition:} Each borrower's transformed variable $U_i$ is decomposed into:

\[
\boxed{U_i = \sqrt{\rho} \cdot F + \sqrt{1-\rho} \cdot \varepsilon_i}
\]

Where:

\begin{itemize}
\item $F \sim N(0,1)$: \textbf{Systemic (common) factor} --- represents macro-economic conditions (e.g., GDP growth, unemployment, interest rates). Affects ALL firms simultaneously. Think of it as "the state of the economy."


\item $\varepsilon_i \sim N(0,1)$: \textbf{Idiosyncratic factor} --- firm-specific risk unique to borrower $i$ (e.g., product launch success, regulatory approval, management changes). Independent across firms.


\item $\rho$: \textbf{Pairwise correlation} between any two borrowers (assumed the same for all pairs in the portfolio --- the simplifying assumption)


\item $F$ and $\varepsilon_i$ are independent standard normals


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Interpreting the formula:}

\begin{itemize}
\item $U_i$ is the sum of two independent normal distributions → $U_i \sim N(0,1)$ (Gaussian copula property)


\item The correlation between any two borrowers $U_i$ and $U_j$ is exactly $\rho$ (from the common factor $F$)


\item When $F$ drops drastically (severe economic recession), all $U_i$ drop together → correlated defaults


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Simplifying assumptions of the Vasicek credit model:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{All loans have the same pairwise correlation \(\rho\)} (homogeneous portfolio assumption)


\item \textbf{All loans have the same probability of default PD} (same average PD for all loans)


\end{enumerate}
These assumptions simplify the model from a 2000-dimensional problem to a single-parameter problem, enabling an analytical solution.

\medskip
\subsection{Loss Distribution and Worst-Case Default Rate}
\textbf{Portfolio-level default rate:} Given the state of the economy (systemic factor $F$), the default rate across the portfolio is:

\[
DR(F) = N\left[\frac{N^{-1}(PD) - \sqrt{\rho} \cdot F}{\sqrt{1-\rho}}\right]
\]

Where $N(\cdot)$ is the standard normal CDF and $N^{-1}(\cdot)$ is its inverse.

\textbf{Intuition:} When $F$ is very negative (severe recession), the formula gives a very high portfolio default rate. When $F$ is positive (good economy), the default rate is low.

\medskip
\subsection{The Worst-Case Default Rate (WCDR) Formula}
For regulatory capital purposes, we want the default rate at an extreme confidence level. \textbf{Basel II/III uses 99.9\% confidence} --- meaning we look at the default rate that would occur in the worst 0.1\% of economic scenarios (roughly once every 1,000 years).

In this worst case, the systematic factor $F$ takes its 0.1th percentile value: \[
F_{\text{worst}} = N^{-1}(0.001) = -N^{-1}(0.999) \approx -3.09
\]

Substituting this worst-case $F$:

\[
\boxed{WCDR = N\left[\frac{N^{-1}(PD) + \sqrt{\rho} \cdot N^{-1}(0.999)}{\sqrt{1-\rho}}\right]}
\]

\textbf{Inputs to the formula:}

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.27\textwidth}L{0.66\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Input} & \textbf{Description} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Input} & \textbf{Description} \\
\midrule
\endhead
$PD$ & Average probability of default of individual loans \\
$\rho$ & Pairwise correlation between loans (the transformed distributions) \\
$N^{-1}(0.999) \approx 3.09$ & 99.9th percentile of standard normal --- the extreme economic scenario \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\textbf{Key insight --- Effect of correlation \(\rho\):}

\begin{itemize}
\item If $\rho = 0$ (loans completely uncorrelated): the $\sqrt{\rho}$ terms vanish → $WCDR = N[N^{-1}(PD)] = PD$

\begin{itemize}
\item No portfolio amplification! The worst-case portfolio default rate = individual default rate


\end{itemize}

\item As $\rho$ increases toward 1: WCDR increases dramatically --- systemic risk dominates


\end{itemize}
\medskip
\subsection{Numerical Example}
\textbf{Inputs:} $PD = 1\% = 0.01$, $\rho = 0.5$

\textbf{Step 1:} $N^{-1}(0.01) = -2.326$

\textbf{Step 2:} $\sqrt{0.5} \approx 0.707$, $N^{-1}(0.999) \approx 3.09$, $\sqrt{1-0.5} = \sqrt{0.5} \approx 0.707$

\textbf{Step 3:}
\[
\begin{aligned}
WCDR
&=N\!\left[\frac{-2.326+0.707(3.09)}{0.707}\right]\\
&=N\!\left[\frac{-0.142}{0.707}\right]
=N(-0.201)\approx42\%.
\end{aligned}
\]

\textbf{Interpretation:}

\begin{itemize}
\item Individual loan default probability: \textbf{1\%} (on average, 1 in 100 loans defaults)


\item Portfolio worst-case default rate (99.9\% confidence): \textbf{42\%} (in the worst 1-in-1,000-year scenario, 42\% of loans default simultaneously!)


\end{itemize}
\textbf{This 42× amplification is entirely driven by the correlation \(\rho\) = 0.5.}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Default rate}\\
\midrule
Individual probability of default & $1\%$\\
Portfolio WCDR ($\rho=0.5$) & $42\%$\\
Portfolio WCDR ($\rho=0$) & $1\%$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{What this means practically:} Even if your loan book looks healthy on average (1\% default rate per loan), systemic correlation means the \emph{tail risk} is enormous. This is why banks must hold substantial capital --- to survive the tail scenario.

\medskip
\subsection{Capital Requirement Calculation}
\textbf{The IRB Capital Formula:}

\[
\boxed{\text{Capital} = LGD \times EAD \times (WCDR - PD)}
\]

\textbf{Component definitions:}

\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.15\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Variable} & \textbf{Full Name} & \textbf{Description} & \textbf{Example} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Variable} & \textbf{Full Name} & \textbf{Description} & \textbf{Example} \\
\midrule
\endhead
$LGD$ & Loss Given Default & Fraction of loan not recovered if default occurs & $= 1 - \text{Recovery Rate}$. If 75\% recovered → LGD = 25\% \\
$EAD$ & Exposure at Default & Outstanding loan balance at time of default & ₹10 million outstanding → EAD = ₹10M \\
$WCDR$ & Worst-Case Default Rate & Portfolio default rate at 99.9\% confidence & From Vasicek formula above \\
$PD$ & Probability of Default & Average individual loan PD & 1\% \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\textbf{Why $(WCDR - PD)$ rather than $WCDR$?}

The key distinction is between \textbf{expected loss} and \textbf{unexpected loss}:

\[
\text{Expected Loss (EL)}=LGD\times EAD\times PD,
\]
\[
\text{Worst-Case Loss (WCL)}=LGD\times EAD\times WCDR,
\]
\[
\text{Unexpected Loss (UL)}=LGD\times EAD\times(WCDR-PD).
\]

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Expected Loss} is the average loss the bank anticipates. This should be built into the loan's interest rate spread and provisioned for in the P\&L. It does not require regulatory capital.


\item \textbf{Unexpected Loss} is the \emph{excess} loss beyond what was expected in a tail scenario. This is what capital must cover.


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Capital = Unexpected Loss = Coverage for the gap between the expected and worst-case scenario.}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.82cm,y=1cm]
\draw[diagramAxis] (0,0) -- (0,4.5) node[above]{Loss};
\draw[thick,diagramGreen] (0,1.2) -- (6.8,1.2)
 node[right,black,text width=4.3cm,align=left]{Expected loss (already priced and provisioned)};
\draw[thick,bearc] (0,3.7) -- (6.8,3.7)
 node[right,black]{Worst-case loss};
\draw[diagramArrow,<->] (4.1,1.35) -- (4.1,3.55)
 node[midway,right,align=left]{Capital requirement\\(unexpected loss)};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Regulatory capital covers the gap between expected and worst-case loss.}
\end{figure}
\textbf{Full example:}

\begin{itemize}
\item $PD = 1\%$, $\rho = 0.5$, $LGD = 25\%$, $EAD$ = ₹10 million per loan, 2,000 loans


\item $WCDR = 42\%$ (from above)


\item Capital per loan = $0.25 \times 10M \times (0.42 - 0.01) = 0.25 \times 10M \times 0.41 = ₹1.025M$


\item Total capital for portfolio = ₹1.025M × 2,000 = ₹2,050M = ₹2.05 billion


\end{itemize}
\textbf{Contrast with zero-correlation case ($\rho = 0$):}

\begin{itemize}
\item $WCDR = PD = 1\%$ → Capital = $0.25 \times 10M \times 0 = ₹0$!


\item With zero correlation, diversification is perfect --- no tail risk beyond the expected loss


\end{itemize}
\textbf{This demonstrates why the assumption of correlation \(\rho\) is the most important parameter in credit portfolio risk.} Higher correlation → dramatically higher capital requirements.

\medskip
\subsection{Summary of Vasicek Credit Model Assumptions}
\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.19\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}L{0.36\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Assumption} & \textbf{Description} & \textbf{Effect if Violated} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Assumption} & \textbf{Description} & \textbf{Effect if Violated} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Gaussian Copula & Joint default distribution modeled as multivariate normal & Model may understate tail risk if true dependence is more extreme (e.g., tail dependence) \\
Same \(\rho\) for all pairs & All loans have identical pairwise correlation & Unrealistic; simplifies model but may misprice portfolios with heterogeneous correlations \\
Same PD for all loans & All loans have the same probability of default & Approximate; real portfolios have diverse credit qualities \\
Large/infinite portfolio & Assumes many loans (LLN applies to idiosyncratic risk) & For small portfolios, idiosyncratic risk matters and the formula overstates capital \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
\medskip
\section{Summary and Quick Reference}
\subsection{The Six Factors at a Glance}

\textbf{Factor} & \textbf{Call Option} & \textbf{Put Option} & \textbf{Greek} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Underlying price ↑ & ↑ & ↓ & Delta (\(\Delta\)) \\
Strike price (lower K) & ↑ & ↓ & --- \\
Time to expiration ↑ & ↑ & ↑ & Theta (\(\Theta\)) --- negative \\

\endgroup
\subsection{Volatility Modeling Landscape}
\begingroup
\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.15\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Class} & \textbf{Use Case} & \textbf{Key Assumption} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Class} & \textbf{Use Case} & \textbf{Key Assumption} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Simple historical vol & Backward-looking & Quick estimates & History repeats \\
SMA / EMA & Backward-looking & Regime monitoring & History repeats (with recency weighting for EMA) \\
ARCH(p) & Statistical (conditional variance) & Short-memory vol forecasting & Variance depends on past $p$ squared errors \\
GARCH(p,q) & Statistical & Persistent vol forecasting & Variance depends on past errors + past variance \\
GBM & Stochastic process & Asset pricing, option pricing & Constant vol, log-normal returns \\

\subsection{Interest Rate Models at a Glance}
\begingroup

\midrule

\midrule

\endgroup
\subsection{Key Formulas Reference}
\textbf{Annualized Volatility:} \[
\sigma_{\text{annual}} = \sigma_{\text{daily}} \times \sqrt{252} = \sigma_{\text{weekly}} \times \sqrt{52} = \sigma_{\text{monthly}} \times \sqrt{12}
\]

\textbf{ARCH(p):} \[
\sigma^2_t = \alpha_0 + \sum_{i=1}^{p} \alpha_i \varepsilon^2_{t-i}, \quad \alpha_0 > 0, \ \alpha_i \geq 0, \ \sum \alpha_i < 1
\]

\textbf{GARCH(p,q):} \[
\sigma^2_t = \alpha_0 + \sum_{i=1}^{q} \alpha_i \varepsilon^2_{t-i} + \sum_{j=1}^{p} \beta_j \sigma^2_{t-j}
\]

\textbf{Vasicek SDE:} \[
dr_t = a(b - r_t) , dt + \sigma , dW_t
\]

\textbf{Vasicek Mean Forecast:} \[
E[r_{t+T}] = r_t e^{-aT} + b(1 - e^{-aT}) \xrightarrow{T \to \infty} b
\]

\textbf{Vasicek Variance Forecast:} \[
\text{Var}(r_{t+T}) = \frac{\sigma^2}{2a}(1 - e^{-2aT}) \xrightarrow{T \to \infty} \frac{\sigma^2}{2a}
\]

\textbf{Vasicek WCDR (Credit Risk):} \[
WCDR = N\left[\frac{N^{-1}(PD) + \sqrt{\rho} \cdot N^{-1}(0.999)}{\sqrt{1-\rho}}\right]
\]

\textbf{IRB Capital Requirement:} \[
\text{Capital} = LGD \times EAD \times (WCDR - PD)
\]

\medskip
\paragraph{Sources.}\emph{ SBI Securities (Option Pricing Factors), Harel Jacobson --- "A Brief History of Volatility Models" (Medium), Ranjith Kumar K --- "Time Series Models: ARCH and GARCH" (Medium), CFI --- "Vasicek Interest Rate Model", Fabozzi --- Chapter 17: Interest-Rate Models, Video transcripts on Volatility Modeling, Vasicek Interest Rate Model, and Vasicek for Credit Risk.}



\chapter{Black--Scholes Model}

\section{Overview}

The \textbf{Black-Scholes model} (also called the \textbf{Black-Scholes-Merton}, or \textbf{BSM}, model) is a mathematical equation used to calculate the fair, theoretical price of an options contract. It was developed by \textbf{Fischer Black}, \textbf{Myron Scholes}, and \textbf{Robert Merton} and published in 1973. It is one of the most important ideas in modern financial theory: it was the first widely used method for pricing options mathematically, and it transformed derivatives from an informally-priced, "bargained-over" product into a multi-trillion-dollar, precisely priced industry.

At its core, the model answers a simple question: \textbf{given a set of known variables about a stock and an option on that stock, what should that option be worth today?} It does this by assuming the underlying stock price moves randomly over time (a "random walk"/Brownian motion) and by building a risk-free hedged portfolio that must, in an efficient market, earn exactly the risk-free rate.

The model only prices \textbf{European-style options} --- options that can only be exercised at expiration (as opposed to American-style options, which can be exercised any time up to expiration).

\section{Historical Origins and Development}

\subsection{Options before Black--Scholes: Thales of Miletus (circa 600 BC)}

The earliest known option contract dates back to around 600 BC. The Greek philosopher \textbf{Thales of Miletus} believed the coming olive harvest would be unusually large. Rather than buying olive presses outright (he didn't have the money), he paid press owners a small fee for the \emph{right} --- but not the obligation --- to rent their presses at a fixed price later in the year. When the bumper harvest arrived, demand for presses (and their rental price) skyrocketed. Thales rented the presses at the pre-agreed low price and re-rented them at the new, much higher market rate, pocketing the difference. This was, in effect, the first recorded \textbf{call option}.

\subsection{Louis Bachelier and the random walk (1900)}

For centuries after Thales, options continued to be traded, but no one had a rigorous way to price them --- traders simply haggled to reach an agreed price. The first person to bring real mathematical rigor to the problem was \textbf{Louis Bachelier}, a French mathematician born in 1870. After losing both parents at 18, he took over and later sold his father's wine business, moved to Paris to study physics, and took a job at the \textbf{Paris Stock Exchange (the Bourse)} to support himself. There, amid the chaotic shouting and hand-signaling of traders, he became fascinated by the pricing of options.

Bachelier proposed the mathematics of finance as his PhD topic to his advisor, the renowned mathematician \textbf{Henri Poincaré}, who --- despite this being an unusual subject at the time --- agreed.

Bachelier's key insight:

\begin{itemize}
\item Stock prices are driven by the constant "tug-of-war" between buyers and sellers, which is influenced by countless unpredictable factors (weather, politics, competition, innovation, etc.).


\item Because these influences are effectively unpredictable, the best assumption is that at any moment, a stock price is equally likely to go up or down.


\item Over time, this means stock prices follow a \textbf{random walk} --- like a coin flip repeated over and over.


\item This randomness is actually the hallmark of an \textbf{efficient market}: a market in which prices already reflect all available information, so that no one can reliably profit from predicting future price moves (the \textbf{Efficient Market Hypothesis}). If prices \emph{were} predictable, everyone would trade on that prediction today, which would itself move the price today --- eliminating the predictable move.


\end{itemize}

\textbf{The Galton board analogy:} A Galton board is a vertical board studded with rows of pegs, with balls dropped from the top. Each time a ball hits a peg, it has a 50/50 chance of bouncing left or right --- a random walk. Any \emph{single} ball's path is essentially unpredictable. But when thousands of balls are dropped, the \emph{collection} of their final resting positions forms a predictable bell-shaped \textbf{normal distribution}, concentrated in the middle (since there are vastly more possible paths that lead to the center than to the far edges).

Bachelier modeled a stock price the same way: each moment in time is like an additional row of pegs. Over a short time, the price can only drift a little; over a longer time, a much wider range of outcomes becomes possible. He concluded that \textbf{the future price of a stock is described by a normal distribution centered on the current price, which spreads out (widens) as time passes.}

Remarkably, Bachelier realized that the equation describing this process was mathematically identical to the equation describing how \textbf{heat diffuses} from hot regions to cold regions --- a phenomenon first described by \textbf{Joseph Fourier} in 1822. He called his discovery the "radiation of probabilities." Because he had framed it in terms of finance, the physics community ignored it --- but the same mathematics would later resolve a genuine mystery in physics.

\subsection{Brownian motion: Robert Brown and Einstein}

In 1827, Scottish botanist \textbf{Robert Brown} observed under a microscope that pollen grains suspended in water moved in a random, jittery path. He ruled out biological explanations by testing inorganic particles (dust from lava and meteorite rock), which moved the same way. This unexplained random jittering became known as \textbf{Brownian motion}.

The cause remained a mystery for 80 years until 1905, when \textbf{Albert Einstein} explained it: Brownian motion is caused by countless invisible molecules randomly colliding with the suspended particle from every direction. Most of the time these collisions cancel out, but occasionally more hit from one side than another, causing the particle to visibly jump. Since these individual molecular collisions can't be observed, the particle must, at any instant, be assumed equally likely to move in any direction --- exactly the same "random walk" logic Bachelier had already applied to stock prices five years earlier, without knowing it. Einstein's explanation of Brownian motion also provided strong evidence that atoms and molecules physically exist.

\textbf{Intuition for finance:} Just as a pollen grain is constantly bombarded by water molecules from every direction, a stock's price is constantly bombarded by buy and sell orders from many market participants. The key difference: a pollen grain can move in any direction in 2D/3D space, while a stock price can only move up or down along one axis (it only "collides" with upward and downward pressure).

In the early 20th century, Japanese mathematician \textbf{Kiyoshi Itô} rigorously proved that financial asset prices do indeed follow Brownian motion, in what became known as \textbf{Itô's Lemma} --- the mathematical tool the Black-Scholes model is built on (see Section 8).

\subsection{Ed Thorp: from blackjack to Wall Street (1950s--1960s)}

\textbf{Ed Thorp}, a physics PhD student in Los Angeles in the 1950s, applied mathematical thinking to blackjack in Las Vegas. Because casinos at the time used only a single deck, Thorp could mentally track which cards had already been played, letting him work out when the odds favored him. He bet more when the odds were good and less when they weren't --- inventing \textbf{card counting}. This worked well until casinos responded by adding more decks to dilute the advantage.

Thorp then took his approach to what he called "the biggest casino on Earth" --- the stock market. He started a hedge fund that delivered a 20\% annual return for 20 years, the best track record seen at the time. His key innovation was a form of \textbf{hedging}: protecting against losses using balancing, offsetting transactions, applied mathematically rather than intuitively.

\textbf{Example of Thorp's dynamic hedging (Bob and Alice):} Suppose Bob sells Alice a call option on a stock, and the stock price rises so the option is now "in the money" for Alice. For every \$1 the stock rises further, Bob (the option seller) loses \$1. Bob can neutralize this risk by owning one unit of the underlying stock: if the price rises \$1, he loses \$1 on the option but gains \$1 back on the stock he owns. If the price later falls back out of the money, he sells the stock, since he no longer needs the hedge. This continuous adjustment of the hedge is called \textbf{dynamic hedging}. It effectively lets Bob "manufacture" the option position out of nothing through trading, while remaining close to risk-neutral.

The quantity of stock needed to hedge one option is called \textbf{delta} --- mathematically, how much the option's price changes for a small change in the underlying stock price. As the example shows, delta itself changes as the stock price moves, which is why the hedge must be \emph{dynamically} rebalanced.

Thorp wasn't fully satisfied with Bachelier's original model, because Bachelier's random walk ignored the fact that stocks tend to drift upward (or downward) over time depending on how the underlying business performs --- not purely 50/50 randomness. Thorp built his own improved model incorporating this \textbf{drift} around 1967, and used it privately (and later with his investors) to buy underpriced options and short-sell overpriced ones according to his model --- profiting from the mispricing more often than not.

\subsection{Black, Scholes, and Merton (1973)}

In 1973, this all changed when \textbf{Fischer Black} and \textbf{Myron Scholes} published an equation for option pricing, and \textbf{Robert Merton} independently published his own derivation using stochastic calculus (and is therefore also credited as a co-creator). Thorp had assumed he would have the field to himself, but Black and Scholes got there first --- with, in Thorp's own assessment, a more rigorous mathematical derivation than his.

\textbf{The core idea, building on Thorp:} if you can construct a risk-free portfolio made of the option and the underlying stock (exactly as Thorp was doing with delta hedging), then in an efficient/fair market, that risk-free portfolio should earn no more than the \textbf{risk-free rate} --- i.e., what the same money would earn if invested safely (e.g., in U.S. Treasury bonds). This is a \textbf{no-arbitrage} argument: since the hedged portfolio carries no risk, it cannot be expected to earn more than the risk-free return, or a riskless profit opportunity (arbitrage) would exist.

To describe how the stock price evolves, Black, Scholes, and Merton used an upgraded version of Bachelier's model (like Thorp's) that included both a random component and a drift component. Combining this stock price model with the no-arbitrage hedging argument produced the now-famous \textbf{Black-Scholes equation} --- a partial differential equation that, when solved, yields an explicit formula: plug in the known input parameters, and the formula directly outputs a price. This was one of the first times options traders had a concrete, usable number to trade on, rather than a vague negotiated price.

\textbf{Impact:} The same year the paper was published (1973), the \textbf{Chicago Board Options Exchange (CBOE)} was founded. The Black-Scholes formula was adopted industry-wide within just a couple of years --- one of the fastest adoptions of an academic idea in the social sciences. The exchange-traded options market has since grown into a multi-trillion-dollar industry, with trading volume roughly doubling every five years (compared by some to a "financial Moore's Law"). Related derivative markets that also rest on Black-Scholes-Merton option-pricing logic --- credit default swaps, OTC derivatives, securitized debt --- have grown similarly explosively.

\textbf{Nobel Prize:} In 1997, \textbf{Myron Scholes} and \textbf{Robert Merton} were awarded the \textbf{Nobel Memorial Prize in Economic Sciences} for "a new method to determine the value of derivatives." \textbf{Fischer Black} had died in 1995, two years earlier; since the Nobel Prize is never awarded posthumously, he could not receive it, though the Nobel committee explicitly acknowledged his contribution.

Historical note: Merton helped edit Black and Scholes's original 1973 paper ("The Pricing of Options and Corporate Liabilities," published in the \emph{Journal of Political Economy}) before separately publishing his own paper, "Theory of Rational Option Pricing," in \emph{The Bell Journal of Economics and Management Science} the same year --- in which he coined the term "Black-Scholes theory of options pricing."

\subsection{Real-world use cases after Black-Scholes}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Corporate hedging example:} An airline worried that rising oil prices will hurt profits can use Black-Scholes-Merton-based pricing to buy an option that tracks oil prices. If oil prices rise, the option pays off, offsetting the airline's higher fuel costs.


\item \textbf{The GameStop short squeeze (2021):} Retail traders coordinated on Reddit's WallStreetBets to buy GameStop shares and drive up the price, punishing hedge funds that were short-selling the stock. Buying shares alone wasn't enough --- because a dollar of cash buys only a dollar of stock, but a dollar of options (thanks to their built-in leverage) can control \$10--\$20 worth of stock exposure. Combining direct stock purchases with options purchases caused the price to spike very quickly, inflicting large losses on the short-selling hedge funds.


\item \textbf{Scale of derivatives markets:} Global derivatives markets are estimated at several hundred trillion dollars --- multiples larger than the total value of the underlying securities they are based on. This is possible because options and derivatives let market participants slice one underlying asset into many different risk/reward "versions" tailored to different investors' preferences.


\item \textbf{Effect on market stability:} During normal times, derivatives markets are a major source of liquidity and therefore stability. During periods of market stress, however, these instruments can all move in the same direction (typically down) simultaneously, which can amplify or exacerbate market crashes.


\end{itemize}

\subsection{Jim Simons and Renaissance Technologies}

With the Black-Scholes formula now public, simply knowing "the secret" of option pricing was no longer enough to reliably outperform the market --- hedge funds needed new ways to find inefficiencies.

\textbf{Jim Simons} was a mathematician (not a trader) whose academic work on Riemann geometry contributed to knot theory, quantum field theory, and quantum computing; his \textbf{Chern-Simons theory} laid mathematical groundwork used in string theory, and he won the \textbf{Oswald Veblen Prize in Geometry} in 1976. He had also worked for the U.S. Institute for Defense Analysis breaking Russian codes during the Cold War --- extracting patterns from large volumes of data.

In 1978, Simons founded \textbf{Renaissance Technologies}, applying a code-breaking, pattern-recognition mindset (using what would now be called machine learning) to financial markets, rather than fundamental analysis or traditional trading intuition. He deliberately hired people with \textbf{no finance background} --- instead looking for people with strong PhDs in physics, mathematics, astronomy, or statistics, and a track record of good published research. One key hire was \textbf{Leonard Baum}, a pioneer of \textbf{hidden Markov models} --- statistical tools for inferring hidden, unobservable factors from observable data (conceptually similar to how Einstein inferred the existence of invisible atoms from the visible, observable jittering of a pollen grain).

In 1988, Simons launched the now-famous \textbf{Medallion Fund}. Using hidden Markov models and other data-driven strategies, it became the highest-returning investment fund in history, delivering \textbf{66\% average annual returns for roughly 30 years}. At that rate, \$100 invested in 1988 would be worth about \textbf{\$8.4 billion} today --- making Simons the richest mathematician of all time. Economist Bradford Cornell (UCLA) discussed this performance in a paper titled \emph{"Medallion Fund: The Ultimate Counterexample"}, arguing it challenges the Efficient Market Hypothesis. Simons himself published a 1988 paper testing the EMH on the U.S. stock market and concluded the hypothesis is false --- i.e., there are real, exploitable predictabilities in the market, given the right models, training, data, and computational resources.

\textbf{A cautionary counterexample --- Isaac Newton (1720):} Even brilliant mathematicians can fail badly at markets. In 1720, the 77-year-old Newton --- already wealthy from his academic career and his role as Master of the Royal Mint (net worth then equivalent to roughly \$6 million today) --- invested in the \textbf{South Sea Company} (a business built on shipping enslaved Africans across the Atlantic). As the share price rose, Newton sold his initial position after it had doubled --- but the price kept climbing, so he bought back in, and kept buying even near the peak. When the price began to fall, instead of selling he bought more, believing he was "buying the dip." There was no rebound, and he ultimately lost about a third of his fortune. His own explanation: \emph{"I can calculate the motions of the heavenly bodies, but not the madness of people."} This illustrates that mathematical skill alone doesn't guarantee market success --- Simons succeeded in part because he built on more than a century of accumulated mathematical understanding of markets (Bachelier, Einstein/Itô, Thorp, Black-Scholes-Merton) that Newton simply didn't have access to.

\section{Options Basics (Building Blocks)}

Before the pricing formula itself, it helps to understand what an option actually is and how its payoff behaves.

\begin{itemize}
\item A \textbf{call option} gives the holder the right, but not the obligation, to \textbf{buy} an asset at a later date for a pre-agreed \textbf{strike price}. Useful when you expect the price to rise.


\item A \textbf{put option} gives the holder the right, but not the obligation, to \textbf{sell} an asset at a later date for the strike price. Useful when you expect the price to fall.


\item \textbf{Long} means you are the \emph{buyer} of the option (you pay a premium up front for this right).


\item \textbf{Short} means you are the \emph{seller/issuer} of the option --- you collect the premium now, but take on the risk that the buyer will exercise the option against you later. The short seller is effectively acting as an \textbf{insurer}.


\item \textbf{American options} can be exercised at any time up to and including the expiration date. \textbf{European options} can only be exercised exactly at expiration. The Black-Scholes model in its standard form only prices \textbf{European} options, because it does not account for the extra value of being able to exercise early.


\end{itemize}

\subsection{Worked numeric example (call option)}

Suppose Apple stock currently trades at \textbf{\$100}, and you expect it to rise. You buy a call option for a \textbf{\$10 premium}, giving you the right to buy Apple stock in one year at a strike price of \textbf{\$100}.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{If in one year the price has risen to \$130:} you exercise the option, buying at \$100 and immediately selling at \$130 --- a \$30 gross gain. Subtracting the \$10 premium you paid, your \textbf{net profit is \$20}.


\item \textbf{If in one year the price has fallen to \$70:} you simply don't exercise the option (it would make no sense to buy at \$100 something worth \$70 on the open market). You lose only the \textbf{\$10 premium} you paid --- not the full drop in value.


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Payoff diagram shape:} If the stock ends below the strike price, your loss is capped at what you paid for the option (a flat line at \(-\)\$10). If the stock ends above the strike price, your profit rises linearly, dollar-for-dollar, with the stock price above the strike, minus the cost of the option.

\subsection{Three advantages of options}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Limited downside.} Had you bought the stock outright instead of the option, a drop from \$100 to \$70 would have cost you \$30 (and in the extreme, you could lose up to the full \$100 if the stock went to zero). With the option, your maximum loss is capped at the \$10 premium.


\item \textbf{Leverage.} Buying the stock and seeing it rise from \$100 to \$130 is a 30\% return. Buying the \$10 option and seeing the same move nets a \$20 profit --- a \textbf{200\% return on the capital actually put up}. The flip side: on the downside, the stock only drops in proportion (e.g., \(-\)30\%), while the option can lose its \textbf{entire value (\(-\)100\%)}.


\item \textbf{Hedging.} Options can be used as a form of insurance --- the original motivation for their existence was risk reduction. Once some market participants wanted to buy that "insurance," others were naturally willing to sell it for a profit, which is how these markets developed in the first place.


\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Options as insurance --- long put and long call}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Long put (insurance against a price drop):} Suppose you own a stock worth \$100 and worry it could fall to \$80 or \$60. You buy a put option with a strike of \$100. If the market price falls below \$100, you can still sell at \$100 using the option, offsetting your loss. This is effectively insurance against a decline.


\item \textbf{Long call (insurance/participation for an upside you don't yet own):} Suppose the stock is at \$100 and you believe it will rise, but you don't own it yet. Buying a call with a strike of \$100 means that if the price rises to, say, \$120, you can still buy at \$100 --- capturing the \$20 difference as profit.


\end{itemize}

\subsection{Short call example}

When you are \textbf{short} a call (you sold/issued it), you collect the premium up front but bear the risk that the buyer exercises against you.

\begin{itemize}
\item If the stock price ends up \textbf{below} the strike at expiration, the buyer has no reason to exercise (a European holder cannot exercise early even if the option was briefly in-the-money along the way --- only an American holder could). The option expires worthless, and as the seller you keep the full premium.


\item If the stock price ends up \textbf{well above} the strike at expiration, the buyer will definitely exercise, and as the seller you must deliver --- resulting in a loss for you.


\end{itemize}

At expiration, a call option's value ("payoff") is simply: \(\max(S_T-K,0)\) --- i.e., stock price minus strike if positive, otherwise zero. This much is straightforward. The much harder question the Black-Scholes model answers is: \textbf{what is the option worth at any point \emph{before} expiration}, while its final payoff is still uncertain? Answering this requires modeling the stochastic (random) behavior of the underlying stock over the remaining time --- which is exactly what the mathematics in Sections 8--9 develops.

\section{What the Black-Scholes Model Does}

Framed simply: the Black-Scholes model is a \textbf{multivariate mathematical formula} that returns the fair price of a European stock option, given a defined set of input variables. The formula for a \textbf{call} option is different from the formula for a \textbf{put} option.

\section{Key Input Variables}

The Black-Scholes equation requires the following inputs:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Volatility} (\(\sigma\)) of the underlying asset's returns


\item \textbf{Current price of the underlying asset} (S)


\item \textbf{Strike price of the option} (K)


\item \textbf{Time until the option's expiration} (t)


\item \textbf{Risk-free interest rate} (r)


\item \textbf{Type of option} (call or put)


\end{enumerate}

With these six variables, an options seller can, in theory, set a rational, consistent price for the options they sell.

\section{The Black-Scholes Formula}

The Black-Scholes price of a \textbf{European call option} is calculated as:

\[
\boxed{C_0=S_0\Phi(d_1)-Ke^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)}
\]

where:

\begin{itemize}
\item \(d_1=[\ln(S_0/K)+(r+\tfrac12\sigma^2)T]/(\sigma\sqrt{T})\)


\item \(d_2=d_1-\sigma\sqrt{T}\)


\end{itemize}

and:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{C} = call option price


\item \textbf{S} = current price of the underlying stock


\item \textbf{K} = strike price


\item \textbf{r} = risk-free interest rate


\item \textbf{t} = time to maturity (expiration)


\item \textbf{\(\sigma\)} = volatility of the underlying asset


\item \textbf{N( )} = the cumulative distribution function of the standard normal distribution


\item \textbf{e} = Euler's number (the mathematical constant \(\approx\) 2.71828), used here to discount the strike price back to present value continuously


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Reading the formula intuitively:} The call price is the current stock price weighted by a probability-like factor N(d\(_1\)), minus the present value of the strike price (discounted continuously at the risk-free rate) weighted by another probability-like factor N(d\(_2\)). Loosely, N(d\(_2\)) can be thought of as related to the (risk-neutral) probability that the option will be exercised, and S\(\cdot\)N(d\(_1\)) as the expected benefit of receiving the stock conditional on exercising.

For completeness, the corresponding European put price is
\[
\boxed{P_0=Ke^{-rT}\Phi(-d_2)-S_0\Phi(-d_1)}.
\]

The call and put formulas are connected by put--call parity,
\[
C_0-P_0=S_0-Ke^{-rT}.
\]

\emph{Note:} the formula for a \textbf{put option} is structurally related but different from the call formula (the put-call relationship follows from a no-arbitrage identity called put-call parity, though the individual put formula isn't detailed in the source material here).

\textbf{Practical note:} you do not need to compute this by hand to use it. Most options trading platforms include built-in options calculators and analytical tools that compute Black-Scholes prices (and the associated Greeks --- see Section 10) automatically from the six inputs.

\section{Assumptions of the Black-Scholes Model}

The model rests on a number of simplifying assumptions:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Stock price dynamics:} The underlying price follows a \textbf{geometric Brownian motion} with \textbf{constant drift and volatility}. This implies the asset's \emph{returns} are normally distributed, and its \emph{price} is lognormally distributed (prices can never go negative, but returns --- which can be negative --- are assumed normal).


\item \textbf{No arbitrage:} There is no way to make a riskless profit with no investment.


\item \textbf{Efficient, frictionless markets:} All available information is already reflected in the price, and prices adjust instantly. There are no transaction costs or taxes, and assets are infinitely divisible. Short selling is permitted, with full use of proceeds.


\item \textbf{Constant, known risk-free rate:} The risk-free interest rate is constant and known for the life of the option.


\item \textbf{No dividends:} The underlying stock pays no dividends during the option's life (the standard model can be, and often is, extended/adapted to account for dividends, typically via the ex-dividend-date value of the stock).


\item \textbf{European exercise only:} The option can only be exercised at expiration, not before.


\item \textbf{Continuous trading:} Trading happens continuously in time, and hedge portfolios can be rebalanced instantly and at no cost.


\end{enumerate}

Because many of these assumptions are unrealistic --- volatility isn't really constant, real markets have transaction costs, risk-free rates fluctuate, and many stocks do pay dividends --- the model has drawn substantial criticism (see Sections 9 and 11).

\section{The Mathematics Behind the Model}

\subsection{Geometric Brownian Motion (GBM)}

The Black-Scholes model assumes the stock price S follows \textbf{geometric Brownian motion}:

\[\dd S_t=\mu S_t\,\dd t+\sigma S_t\,\dd W_t.\]

This has two parts:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Drift term: \(\mu\)S dt} --- the deterministic, expected trend of the stock (its average expected growth rate \(\mu\)).


\item \textbf{Volatility (diffusion) term: \(\sigma\)S dW} --- the random component, driven by an increment of Brownian motion dW, scaled by the stock's volatility \(\sigma\).


\end{itemize}

The problem: because this equation contains a Brownian motion term, it cannot be solved with ordinary calculus techniques. Solving it requires \textbf{Itô's Lemma}.

\subsection{Warm-up: the deterministic case}

Start by dividing through by S so all the S terms sit on the left: \(\dd S_t/S_t=\mu\,\dd t+\sigma\,\dd W_t\).

First, temporarily ignore the random (volatility) term and keep only the drift: \(\dd S_t/S_t=\mu\,\dd t\). This is now an ordinary, deterministic differential equation. Expanding with a Taylor series and keeping only first-order terms (since in standard calculus, dt\(^2\) and higher powers vanish as dt shrinks), integrating over time yields the familiar result: \textbf{exponential growth}, i.e., \(S(t)=S_0e^{\mu t}\).

\subsection{Bringing back randomness: Itô's Lemma}

To reintroduce the random term, a simple first-order Taylor expansion is no longer sufficient --- we need Itô's Lemma. The intuition, built up step by step:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Function of time only:} for a smooth function that depends only on time, laying successive-order Taylor expansions on top of the function shows the approximation improving, but at the infinitesimal scale, the first-order term is enough --- i.e., terms involving dt\(^2\) (and higher) can be treated as zero.


\item \textbf{Function of time \emph{and} Brownian motion:} once the function also depends on a Brownian motion term, a first-order (straight-line) approximation is no longer enough to capture the local behavior --- no matter what slope is chosen, it "misses." Second-order terms must be brought back in.


\item \textbf{Switch to an Itô process:} instead of raw Brownian motion, consider a process that is Brownian motion scaled by \(\sigma\), plus a drift term (i.e., exactly the GBM setup above).


\item \textbf{List all Taylor terms up to second order}, then apply two key rules for how infinitesimal increments behave:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{dt\(\cdot\)dW = 0} --- because the increment of Brownian motion over a small time step has mean zero and is independent of the past, so this cross term vanishes in expectation.


\item \textbf{dW\(\cdot\)dW = dt} --- because Brownian motion accumulates "quadratic variation" proportional to time; this term does \emph{not} vanish, which is precisely why the second-order term matters here (unlike in ordinary calculus).


\item The dt\(^2\) term is negligible and dropped.


\end{itemize}


\end{enumerate}

Putting these pieces together yields \textbf{Itô's Lemma}: for a function f(t, X) where X follows an Itô process, the change in f decomposes into a drift part and a diffusion (random) part that together properly account for the extra "curvature" contributed by the dW\(^2\) = dt term.

\subsection{Solving Geometric Brownian Motion with Itô's Lemma}

Apply Itô's Lemma to the function \textbf{f = ln(S)}, where S follows the GBM equation from Section 8.1. Working through the derivative terms of ln(S) and substituting into Itô's Lemma produces the solved equation for the logarithm of the stock price, which after integrating over time gives the closed-form solution:

\[
S_t=S_0\exp\!\left[\left(\mu-\frac12\sigma^2\right)t+\sigma W_t\right].
\]

This is the key result: it shows that under the GBM assumption, the stock price at any future time t is \textbf{lognormally distributed} --- consistent with the model's core assumption in Section 7 that prices are lognormal (since a normally-distributed random variable, W(t), sits inside an exponential). Note the term \textbf{\(-\)\(\sigma\)\(^2\)/2} --- this is a direct mathematical consequence of applying Itô's Lemma (it comes from the second-order term that ordinary calculus would have missed), and it means the median and almost-sure log-growth rate is \(\mu-\sigma^2/2\), while \(\E[S_t]=S_0e^{\mu t}\).

\section{Deriving the Black-Scholes Equation: Replicating Portfolios and Delta Hedging}

\subsection{The core idea}

To price an option before expiration, think from the perspective of the option \textbf{seller}. Having sold (issued) the option, the seller is exposed to risk if it ends up "in the money" for the buyer at expiration. To protect against this, the seller wants to \textbf{hedge}: build a position that doesn't lose money no matter which way the stock moves.

The hedging tool is a \textbf{replicating portfolio}: hold some combination of (a) the underlying stock and (b) cash or a risk-free bond, such that this combination behaves identically to the option, cancelling out the seller's risk.

\begin{itemize}
\item If the stock is moving up and the seller is short a call, they are exposed --- so they buy some stock to compensate.


\item If the option currently has little sensitivity to the stock (e.g., it's far out of the money), less stock is needed, and more of the portfolio can sit in cash earning the risk-free rate.


\end{itemize}

The central question the model answers: \textbf{exactly how much stock vs. how much cash should be held at each moment, and therefore what should the option's fair price be, before expiration?}

\subsection{Setting up the portfolio (formal version)}

Let the replicating portfolio hold:

\begin{itemize}
\item an amount \textbf{A} of the stock, and


\item an amount \textbf{B} of cash/bond.


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Dynamics of each piece:}

\begin{itemize}
\item The stock S follows geometric Brownian motion (drift + random part), as in Section 8.1.


\item The bond/cash B has \textbf{no randomness} --- it grows deterministically at the constant risk-free rate r.


\end{itemize}

Combining these lets us describe how the whole portfolio (A\(\cdot\)S + B) evolves, and this can be split into its own \textbf{drift} (predictable) part and \textbf{diffusion} (random) part.

There is a second way to describe how the portfolio (or equivalently, the option value) evolves: applying \textbf{Itô's Lemma} directly to the option's value function V(S,t), exactly as in Section 8.3.

This produces \textbf{two separate expressions} for the portfolio's drift and diffusion --- one derived from the stock-plus-bond composition, and one derived from Itô's Lemma applied to the option value. \textbf{Matching these two expressions to each other} and solving lets us solve for A and B.

\subsection{Solving for A (delta) and B}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Solving for A:} A turns out to equal how sensitive the option price is to the stock price --- i.e., \(A=\partial V/\partial S=\Delta\), the option's \textbf{delta}. If a small move in the stock produces a big move in the option price, more stock must be held to hedge; if the option barely reacts to the stock (e.g., a deep out-of-the-money call), very little stock is needed.


\item \textbf{Solving for B:} B is the amount of cash/bond needed to complete the hedge once A (the stock holding) is known.


\end{itemize}

\subsection{The Black-Scholes equation and formula}

Substituting the solved \(A\) and \(B\) back into the replicating portfolio
equation produces the \textbf{Black--Scholes equation}:
\[
\boxed{
\frac{\partial V}{\partial t}
+\frac12\sigma^2S^2\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}
+rS\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}-rV=0.
}
\]
For a European call, the terminal condition is
\[
V(S,T)=\max(S-K,0).
\]
This boundary condition states that the option's terminal value equals its
known payoff at expiration.

Solving this differential equation (the derivation itself is not carried out step-by-step in the source material) yields the \textbf{Black-Scholes formula} given in Section 6. Structurally, it contains two cumulative normal distribution terms, \textbf{N(d\(_+\))} and \textbf{N(d\(_-\))} (equivalent to N(d\(_1\)) and N(d\(_2\)) in Section 6), where d\(_+\) is always slightly larger than d\(_-\).

\textbf{Visual intuition for d\(_1\)/d\(_2\) ("sliding a bell curve"):}

\begin{itemize}
\item When the stock price equals the strike price, both d\(_+\) and d\(_-\) sit near the center of the standard normal curve.


\item When the stock price drops, both slide to the left (their N() values shrink toward 0).


\item When the stock price rises a lot, both slide far to the right, and their cumulative distribution values approach 1. In this case the option is \textbf{deep in the money} --- exercise is essentially certain --- and the call price formula simplifies to just \textbf{stock price minus the discounted strike price} (matching intuition: the option behaves almost exactly like owning the stock outright, minus the cost of paying the strike later, discounted back to today because money today is worth more than money in the future).


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Behavior of the option value over time (path intuition):}

\begin{itemize}
\item If the stock drops below the strike and stays there, the call option becomes worth less and less, fading toward zero as expiration approaches (less and less time left for it to recover).


\item If the stock is in the middle of its path and still has time left, the option retains some value, because there's still a chance it moves back above the strike.


\item If the stock rises well above the strike, the option's value increasingly tracks (stock price \(-\) discounted strike), converging to that relationship almost exactly near expiration.


\end{itemize}

\subsection{Alternative framing: hedging a short option position directly}

A second, equivalent way to derive the same hedge:

\begin{itemize}
\item If you are \textbf{short} an option (you sold/issued it), you face a potential future payment obligation if it ends up in the money. Represent this as a \textbf{negative} position: \textbf{\(-\)C} (C = option price).


\item To offset this risk, hold some quantity \textbf{\(\Delta\) (delta)} of the underlying stock, forming the combined portfolio: \(-C+\Delta S\).


\item Break the small changes in this portfolio into a \textbf{predictable (drift)} part and a \textbf{random (risk)} part, driven by the stock's volatility.


\item The goal of hedging is to choose \(\Delta\) so that the \textbf{random part cancels out entirely}.


\item Solving for this condition shows that the correct \(\Delta\) is exactly \textbf{how sensitive the option price is to the stock price} --- i.e., the derivative of the option price with respect to the stock price. This is the same delta as in Section 9.3.


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Two worked examples:}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Deep in-the-money call, near expiration:} the option price moves almost one-for-one with the stock price, so delta is close to 1 (or close to 100\%) --- a large amount of stock must be held to hedge. In practice this often means \emph{borrowing} cash to fund the stock purchase (cash balance goes negative) in order to hold that much stock.


\item \textbf{Deep out-of-the-money call:} the stock price is well below the strike, the option is essentially worthless, and its sensitivity to the stock is near zero --- so delta is near zero, and little or no stock needs to be held.


\end{itemize}

In both cases, if the hedge is constructed correctly, the \textbf{total value of the replicating portfolio (option + stock + cash) stays flat, close to zero, throughout the life of the trade} --- even though the individual pieces move up and down. This flatness demonstrates that the strategy has successfully cancelled out risk: the hedger is neither reliably making nor losing money from the stock's random fluctuations; they are \textbf{risk-neutral} with respect to the stock's movement. This is the essence of \textbf{delta hedging}.

\section{Option Greeks}

Because the Black-Scholes model is a multivariate equation, institutional traders study its \textbf{partial derivatives} with respect to each input variable --- collectively called the \textbf{Greeks}. These let traders decompose overall option risk into distinct, separately manageable and hedgeable types of risk, which is what makes it practical to trade profitably despite the model's unrealistic underlying assumptions.

\begingroup
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.240\textwidth}L{0.690\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Greek} & \textbf{What it measures} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Greek} & \textbf{What it measures} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\textbf{Delta} & Exposure/sensitivity to the \emph{direction} of the underlying's price (Sections 9.3--9.5) \\
\textbf{Gamma} & Exposure to price \emph{fluctuations} in either direction (i.e., how much delta itself changes as the stock price moves) \\
\textbf{Theta} & Exposure to the passage of \emph{time} (time decay of the option's value) \\
\textbf{Vega} & Exposure to changes in \emph{implied volatility} \\
\textbf{Rho} & Exposure to changes in the \emph{risk-free interest rate} \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup

\textbf{Key practical insight:} institutional traders generally don't trade options purely to bet on price direction --- they trade \textbf{volatility}, using the Black-Scholes framework and its Greeks. This is because price \emph{volatility} tends to be easier to predict (or model) than raw price \emph{direction}, given the mathematical nature of how prices behave.

\section{Volatility Skew}

The Black-Scholes model assumes stock prices are \textbf{lognormally distributed} (since prices can never go below zero). Under this assumption, implied volatility should be roughly similar across different strike prices for options on the same underlying with the same expiration.

In practice, this is not what's observed:

\begin{itemize}
\item Equity-index returns commonly show significant \textbf{negative skewness} and \textbf{kurtosis (fat tails)} --- large downward price moves happen more often than a normal distribution would predict.


\item Since the 1987 market crash, \textbf{at-the-money options have consistently shown lower implied volatility than far out-of-the-money or deep in-the-money options}. This produces a "smile" or "skew" shape when implied volatilities across strikes (for a given expiration) are plotted on a graph --- the market is effectively signaling a higher perceived chance of a large drop.


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Empirical evidence of downside/upside asymmetry (COVID-19 crash):} during the COVID-19 pandemic crash, the S\&P 500 took only \textbf{32 days} to fall to its bottom, but took \textbf{153 days} --- roughly five times as long --- to climb back to its pre-crash level. This illustrates that downside volatility is typically much greater than upside volatility, a pattern seen in essentially all historical market crashes, and a real-world violation of the model's constant-volatility assumption. (This asymmetry is also what specialized \textbf{volatility-skewness trading strategies} are designed to exploit.)

\textbf{Consequence:} because of this skew, the Black-Scholes model, in its plain form, is \textbf{not efficient/accurate for calculating implied volatility} directly from market prices.

\section{Benefits and Limitations}

\subsection{Benefits}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Provides a structured framework:} gives investors and traders a tried-and-tested, defined methodology for determining a fair option price, rather than relying on ad hoc negotiation.


\item \textbf{Enables risk management:} knowing an option's theoretical value helps investors understand and manage their exposure, and identify weaknesses or gaps in a portfolio.


\item \textbf{Supports portfolio optimization:} provides a measure of expected returns and risk for different options, helping investors make choices aligned with their risk tolerance and return objectives.


\item \textbf{Enhances market efficiency and transparency:} because traders share a common, transparent method for pricing, options markets have become more efficient and easier to price and trade.


\item \textbf{Streamlines and standardizes pricing:} its wide acceptance among practitioners provides consistency and comparability of prices across different markets and jurisdictions.


\end{itemize}

\subsection{Limitations}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Limited to European options:} the standard model does not account for the possibility of early exercise, so it isn't directly suited to American-style options (which are more commonly traded in the U.S.). Firms instead often use \textbf{binomial or trinomial models}, or the \textbf{Bjerksund-Stensland model}, for pricing American-style options.


\item \textbf{Lacks cash-flow flexibility:} the model assumes dividends and the risk-free rate are constant, which may not reflect real-world cash flows accurately, since the model is somewhat rigid.


\item \textbf{Assumes constant volatility:} in reality, volatility fluctuates with supply and demand, which the standard model does not capture (see Section 11).


\item \textbf{Relies on other simplifying assumptions that can deviate from reality:} including no transaction costs or taxes, a constant risk-free interest rate across all maturities, unrestricted short selling with full use of proceeds, and the absence of any risk-free arbitrage opportunities. Each of these, where violated in the real world, can cause the model's price to diverge from actual market prices.


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Fast fact:} The Black-Scholes model is often contrasted with other pricing approaches such as the \textbf{binomial model} or \textbf{Monte Carlo simulation}, which can more flexibly accommodate early exercise and non-constant assumptions.

\section{Summary}

The Black-Scholes model emerged from a century-long convergence of ideas from physics (Brownian motion, heat diffusion), probability theory (random walks, the normal distribution), and finance (options as insurance, hedging, no-arbitrage pricing). Its central mechanism is the construction of a \textbf{risk-free replicating portfolio} of the underlying stock and a risk-free bond that mirrors the behavior of the option; the requirement that this riskless portfolio earn only the risk-free rate (no arbitrage) pins down a unique, fair option price. Despite resting on a number of assumptions that don't perfectly hold in real markets --- constant volatility chief among them --- its introduction was transformative: it gave the market its first standardized, computable way to price options, and its logical framework (particularly the Greeks and the concept of dynamic delta hedging) remains the backbone of how institutional derivatives desks manage risk today.

\chapter{Deriving the Black--Scholes Equation}

\section{Overview}

There are two classic ways to arrive at the Black-Scholes pricing result, and this topic covers both:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{The PDE / replicating-portfolio approach} --- build a riskless portfolio out of the option and the underlying stock, dynamically rebalanced, and use the no-arbitrage principle (a riskless portfolio must earn exactly the risk-free rate) to derive a partial differential equation (PDE) that the option price must satisfy.


\item \textbf{The risk-neutral pricing / expectation approach} --- directly define the option's price today as the \emph{discounted, risk-neutral expected value} of its future payoff, and use a change-of-measure result called \textbf{Girsanov's theorem} to justify computing this expectation.


\end{enumerate}

Both approaches are mathematically equivalent and arrive at the same final pricing formula; they are simply two different lenses (PDE vs. probabilistic) on the same underlying idea: \textbf{construct something risk-free, and require it to earn only the risk-free rate.}

\section{Derivation 1 --- The PDE Approach (Replicating Portfolio / Delta Hedging)}

This is the more "mechanical," step-by-step derivation, and it directly formalizes the intuitive hedging argument introduced in the previous notes (Section 9).

\subsection{Step 1 --- Stock Price Dynamics}

Assume the underlying stock price S follows \textbf{Geometric Brownian Motion (GBM)}:

\[\dd S_t=\mu S_t\,\dd t+\sigma S_t\,\dd W_t.\]

where:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{\(\mu\)} = the expected drift (return) of the stock


\item \textbf{\(\sigma\)} = the volatility of the stock


\item \textbf{dW} = a standard Brownian motion increment (the random component)


\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 2 --- Defining the Derivative's Value}

Let \textbf{V(S, t)} be the price of a derivative (e.g., a European call option) that depends on both the current stock price S and time t. Because V is a function of a stochastic (random) process, its own change over time, \textbf{dV}, must be found using \textbf{Itô's Lemma} (see previous notes, Section 8.3, for the derivation of Itô's Lemma itself):

\[
\dd V=\left(V_t+\mu S V_S+\frac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}\right)\dd t
      +\sigma S V_S\,\dd W_t.
\]

Reading this: the first (bracketed) term is the \textbf{deterministic/drift part} of the option's price change; the second term, multiplying dW, is the \textbf{random part}, whose size depends on \(\sigma\), S, and how sensitive V is to S (i.e., \(\partial\)V/\(\partial\)S).

\subsection{Step 3 --- Constructing a Riskless Portfolio}

To eliminate the randomness coming from dW, build a portfolio \textbf{\(\Pi\)} consisting of:

\begin{itemize}
\item a \textbf{long} position of one unit of the derivative: \textbf{+V}


\item a \textbf{short} position of \(\Delta\) units of the underlying stock: \textbf{\(-\)\(\Delta\)\(\cdot\)S}


\end{itemize}

\[\Pi=V-\Delta S.\]

Over a small time interval dt, the change in this portfolio's value is:

\[\dd\Pi=\dd V-\Delta\,\dd S.\]

Substituting in the expressions for dV (from Step 2, Itô's Lemma) and dS (from Step 1, GBM):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\dd\Pi={}&\left(V_t+\mu SV_S+\frac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}\right)\dd t
+\sigma SV_S\,\dd W_t\\
&-\Delta\left(\mu S\,\dd t+\sigma S\,\dd W_t\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

Grouping the deterministic (dt) terms and the random (dW) terms separately:

\[
\dd\Pi=\left(V_t+\mu SV_S+\frac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}-\Delta\mu S\right)\dd t
