+\sigma S(V_S-\Delta)\,\dd W_t.
\]

\subsection{Step 4 --- Eliminating Risk (Delta Hedging)}

To make the portfolio \textbf{perfectly riskless}, the random dW term must vanish entirely. This happens if we choose:

\[\Delta=V_S=\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}.\]

--- i.e., the hedge ratio \(\Delta\) is set equal to the option's sensitivity to the stock price (exactly the same "delta" defined in the previous notes, Sections 9.3--9.5 and 10). Substituting \(\Delta\) = \(\partial\)V/\(\partial\)S back into the equation makes the coefficient of dW exactly zero (\(\sigma\)S\(\cdot\)\(\partial\)V/\(\partial\)S \(-\) (\(\partial\)V/\(\partial\)S)\(\cdot\)\(\sigma\)S = 0), leaving only the deterministic part:

\[\dd\Pi=\left(V_t+\frac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}\right)\dd t.\]

Notice that \textbf{\(\mu\) (the stock's real-world expected return/drift) has completely dropped out} of this equation. This is a crucial and somewhat surprising feature of the Black-Scholes framework: because risk has been fully hedged away, the option's price does \textbf{not} depend on how bullish or bearish investors are about the stock's future direction --- only on its volatility, the risk-free rate, and the other known inputs.

\subsection{Step 5 --- Applying the No-Arbitrage Principle}

Since the portfolio \(\Pi\) is now entirely risk-free, it must earn exactly the same rate of return as any other risk-free investment (such as a government bond) with interest rate \textbf{r}. If it earned more, investors would pile into building this exact riskless portfolio for a free profit; if it earned less, they would short it and invest in bonds instead --- either way, in an efficient market, such a \textbf{riskless arbitrage opportunity cannot persist}. This gives:

\[\dd\Pi=r\Pi\,\dd t.\]

Substituting the definition of the portfolio, \(\Pi=V-SV_S\), into this condition:

\[\dd\Pi=r(V-SV_S)\,\dd t.\]

\subsection{Step 6 --- The Black-Scholes Equation}

We now have \textbf{two separate expressions} for d\(\Pi\) --- one from the hedging derivation (Step 4) and one from the no-arbitrage condition (Step 5). Setting them equal:

\[\left(V_t+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}\right)\dd t=r(V-SV_S)\dd t.\]

Dividing out dt (which appears on both sides) and rearranging all terms onto one side gives the final result --- the \textbf{Black-Scholes partial differential equation}:

\[
\boxed{V_t+rSV_S+\frac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}-rV=0.}
\]

This single PDE, together with a \textbf{terminal (boundary) condition} specifying the derivative's known payoff at expiration (e.g., for a call: V(S,T) = max(S \(-\) K, 0)), is what defines the option's price at every earlier point in time. Solving this PDE for that boundary condition produces the explicit closed-form Black-Scholes formula covered in the previous notes and recapped in Section 4 below.

\textbf{Why this derivation is considered elegant:} it converts a problem involving an unpredictable, risky asset into a completely deterministic equation, purely by the trick of continuously rebalancing a hedge (\(\Delta\)) so that all randomness cancels out at every instant. The price of risk (\(\mu\)) never has to be estimated or agreed upon --- the no-arbitrage argument alone pins down a unique price.

\section{Derivation 2 --- The Risk-Neutral Pricing / Expectation Approach}

This is a different (but ultimately equivalent) route to the same destination, developed via probability rather than differential equations.

\subsection{Recap: How the Underlying Evolves}

From prior derivation (see also previous notes, Section 8.4), the price of the underlying asset at a future time \textbf{T}, given today's time is \textbf{t}, follows:

\[
S_T=S_t\exp\!\left[\left(\mu-\frac12\sigma^2\right)(T-t)+\sigma(W_T-W_t)\right].
\]

where \(\Delta\)W = W\_T \(-\) W\_t is the increment of Brownian motion between today and the future time T. The instantaneous dynamics behind this are, as before:

\[\dd S_t=\mu S_t\,\dd t+\sigma S_t\,\dd W_t.\]

\subsection{Two Approaches to Pricing: Risky vs. Risk-Neutral}

There are, broadly, two ways to price any asset or derivative:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Risky pricing} --- pricing that explicitly accounts for investors' risk preferences and required risk premiums.


\item \textbf{Risk-neutral pricing} --- a mathematically convenient shortcut in which we act \emph{as if} all investors were indifferent to risk, and price the derivative purely as a \textbf{discounted expected payoff}.


\end{itemize}

This derivation takes the \textbf{risk-neutral pricing} approach.

\subsection{The Risk-Neutral Pricing Formula}

Under risk-neutral pricing, the price of any asset today (time t) is given by the \textbf{risk-neutral expectation of its future payoff}, discounted back to the present at the risk-free rate:

\[V_t=e^{-r(T-t)}\E^{\mathbb Q}[H(S_T)\mid\Fscr_t].\]

\textbf{Continuous compounding of the risk-free rate:} in continuous time, the risk-free growth factor over a period is defined as \(e^{r\tau}\). For example, if the annual risk-free rate is 5\%, then the risk-free growth factor over half a year (time period = 0.5) is \(e^{0.05(0.5)}=e^{0.025}\approx1.025\).

Substituting this definition of the discount factor into the pricing formula:

Equivalently,
\[
V_t=e^{-r(T-t)}\E^{\mathbb Q}[H(S_T)].
\]

\subsection{Expanding the Expectation}

The derivative's payoff is some function of the underlying's future value: \(H(S_T)=f(S_T)\). We already have a formula for S\_T (Section 3.1), so we can substitute it in:

\[
V_t=e^{-r\tau}\E\!\left[f\!\left(S_t e^{(\mu-\sigma^2/2)\tau+\sigma\Delta W}\right)\right],
\qquad \tau=T-t.
\]

To actually compute this expectation, we need the \textbf{distribution} of the only random component here, \(\Delta\)W. As established previously, Brownian motion increments are \textbf{normally distributed with mean 0 and variance equal to the elapsed time}: \(\Delta\)W ~ Normal(0, T\(-\)t).

\textbf{A useful scaling trick:} if Y is a \textbf{standard} normal random variable (mean 0, variance 1), then multiplying it by \(\sqrt{\phantom{x}}\)(T\(-\)t) produces a normal random variable with mean 0 and variance (T\(-\)t) --- exactly matching the distribution of \(\Delta\)W. (This scaling property holds generally: multiplying a standard normal variable by \(\sqrt{\phantom{x}}\)k produces a normal variable with variance k.) So we can write:

\(\Delta W=\sqrt{T-t}\,Y\), where \(Y\sim\mathcal N(0,1)\), where Y ~ Standard Normal(0, 1)

\subsection{Writing the Expectation as an Integral}

The expectation of a function of a normal random variable is, by definition, the integral of that function weighted by the variable's probability density function (PDF). For a standard normal variable Y, the PDF is:

\[\phi(y)=\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-y^2/2}.\]

So the expectation becomes an explicit integral over all possible values of y, from \(-\)\(\infty\) to \(\infty\):

\[
\E[f(S_T)]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
f\!\left(S_t e^{(\mu-\sigma^2/2)\tau+\sigma\sqrt{\tau}\,y}\right)
\frac{e^{-y^2/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\dd y.
\]

This integral form is completely general: whatever function f (the payoff structure) is plugged in, this integral --- once evaluated --- gives that derivative's expected future payoff.

\subsection{The Problem: This Is a "Normal" Expectation, Not a Risk-Neutral One}

There's a subtlety: the expression above, using the \emph{real-world} drift \(\mu\), is a \textbf{normal (real-world / physical-measure) expectation} --- not the \textbf{risk-neutral} expectation the pricing formula in Section 3.3 actually calls for. To correctly switch from one to the other requires a result called \textbf{Girsanov's Theorem}.

\textbf{Girsanov's Theorem (used here, not derived):} this is an advanced result from stochastic calculus. Recall the underlying's dynamics were written as \(\dd S_t/S_t=\mu\,\dd t+\sigma\,\dd W_t^{\mathbb P}\). Girsanov's theorem tells us that we can compute the \textbf{risk-neutral} expectation using exactly the same machinery as a normal expectation --- the same integral, the same normal-distribution logic --- \textbf{provided we simply replace the real-world drift \(\mu\) with the risk-free rate r} in the underlying's dynamics:

\(\dd S_t/S_t=r\,\dd t+\sigma\,\dd W_t^{\mathbb Q}\) (under the risk-neutral measure)

In other words: to go from a "physical world" expectation to a "risk-neutral" expectation, swap \(\mu\) for r everywhere it appears, and otherwise carry out the same calculation. (The rigorous proof of Girsanov's theorem is advanced and outside the scope of these notes.)

\subsection{The Resulting Master Pricing Equation}

Applying this substitution (\(\mu\) → r) to the integral from Section 3.5, and plugging back into the discounted-expectation pricing formula from Section 3.3, gives what is, in effect, the \textbf{Black-Scholes pricing equation} in its general, implicit integral form:

\[
\boxed{
V_t=e^{-r\tau}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
f\!\left(S_t e^{(r-\sigma^2/2)\tau+\sigma\sqrt{\tau}\,y}\right)
\frac{e^{-y^2/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\dd y.}
\]

This single formula can price \textbf{any} European-style derivative on this underlying --- all that changes is the payoff function f plugged in. For example, if a derivative's payoff were the \emph{square} of the underlying's value at time T, you would substitute f(x) = x\(^2\) into this integral and evaluate it. For a standard \textbf{call option}, the payoff function is f(S\_T) = max(S\_T \(-\) K, 0); evaluating the integral for this specific payoff function (a separate calculus exercise not carried out in this transcript) is exactly what produces the closed-form Black-Scholes call formula recapped in Section 4 below.

\section{Recap: The Black-Scholes-Merton Formula}

Both derivation routes above converge on the same explicit pricing formula for a \textbf{European call option} on a \textbf{non-dividend-paying} asset:

\[\boxed{C(S_t,t)=S_t\Phi(d_1)-Ke^{-r\tau}\Phi(d_2).}\]

where:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{S\(_0\)} = the stock price today


\item \textbf{C(S\(_0\), t)} = the price of the call option, as a function of the stock price and time


\item \textbf{K} = the exercise (strike) price


\item \textbf{(T \(-\) t)} = time to maturity --- the exercise date T, minus the current time t (typically expressed in years; e.g., one month = 1/12 \(\approx\) 0.0833 years)


\item \textbf{r} = the risk-free interest rate (the return available on an assumed risk-free investment, like a T-bill)


\item \textbf{\(\sigma\)} = the underlying's volatility (the standard deviation of its log returns)


\item \textbf{N(d\(_1\))} and \textbf{N(d\(_2\))} = cumulative distribution functions (CDFs) of the \textbf{standard normal distribution}, computed as:


\end{itemize}

\[d_1=\frac{\ln(S_t/K)+(r+\tfrac12\sigma^2)\tau}{\sigma\sqrt{\tau}}.\]

\[d_2=d_1-\sigma\sqrt{\tau}=\frac{\ln(S_t/K)+(r-\tfrac12\sigma^2)\tau}{\sigma\sqrt{\tau}}.\]

(The formula for a European \textbf{put} option is structurally similar, differing in which terms are added vs. subtracted and which probability terms are used --- related to the call formula through \emph{put-call parity}.)

\textbf{Important caveat on notation:} this is the notation standard to the finance industry, but it is not, strictly, standard mathematical notation. What is loosely called "a normal distribution" here is technically the \textbf{cumulative distribution function of a log-normal distribution} --- built from an underlying standard normal distribution (mean 0, standard deviation 1) that is assumed but rarely stated explicitly.

\section{Understanding Volatility in the Model}

\textbf{What volatility measures:} volatility is a measure of how much, and how quickly, an asset's price changes --- fundamentally, a measure of \emph{uncertainty}. If everyone were certain of an asset's true worth, it would trade in a very tight range around that value (buyers wouldn't pay above it, sellers wouldn't accept below it). Highly \emph{uncertain} assets, by contrast, trade across a much wider range of prices. \textbf{Implied volatility} --- the specific volatility value used inside the Black-Scholes-Merton formula --- represents the market's own implied estimate of the underlying's volatility.

\subsection{Worked Comparison: Oil ETF vs. Cow (Live Cattle) ETF}

To make this concrete, consider historical data comparing an \textbf{Oil ETF} and a \textbf{Cow (Live Cattle) ETF} over a three-year window (Nov 2013 -- Nov 2016):

\begin{itemize}
\item The \textbf{Oil ETF} is visibly much more volatile: its price not only trends downward more steeply (a steeper slope in its overall trend line) but also fluctuates much more sharply around that trend line on a day-to-day basis.


\item The \textbf{Cow ETF}, by contrast, shows a gentler downward trend and smaller day-to-day fluctuations.


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Quantifying this with standard deviation of daily returns:}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Cow ETF:} mean price \(\approx\) \textbf{\$26.85}; standard deviation of daily returns \(\approx\) \textbf{3.7}, or about \textbf{13.8\%} of the mean. Since one standard deviation in a normal distribution captures \textbf{68.2\%} of outcomes, this means about 68.2\% of the Cow ETF's daily prices fell within roughly \textbf{\$23.13 to \$30.56}.


\item \textbf{Oil ETF:} mean price \(\approx\) \textbf{\$13.58}; standard deviation of daily returns is much larger --- about \textbf{7.5}, or \textbf{55.4\%} of the mean. So about 68.2\% of the Oil ETF's daily prices fell within a much wider band: roughly \textbf{\$6.03 to \$21.01} --- a far broader (both in absolute dollar terms and in percentage terms) range than the Cow ETF.


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Why volatility increases an option's value:} because of how a call option works, its buyer only profits if the underlying finishes \emph{above} the strike price --- and they don't care \emph{how far} below the strike it might have gone, since their loss is capped at the premium paid either way. But they care a great deal about \emph{how far above} the strike the price ends up, since that directly determines their profit. As a result, a \textbf{more volatile underlying} --- one more likely to swing to extreme high (or low) values --- makes the call option \textbf{more valuable}, since it raises the chance of a large favorable move without increasing the cost of an unfavorable one beyond the already-capped premium.

\subsection{Volatility as Both an Implied Input and a Standard Deviation}

Technically, in the Black-Scholes-Merton model, \textbf{implied volatility is the annualized standard deviation of the asset's returns}, expressed as a decimal or percentage. The single symbol \textbf{\(\sigma\)} in the formula plays a dual role: it is simultaneously the market's \emph{implied} volatility estimate and the \emph{statistical standard deviation} of the (log-normal) distribution of possible future prices --- because a more volatile asset, by definition, has a wider spread (standard deviation) of possible future returns.

\section{High-Level, Intuitive Reading of the Formula}

\[\boxed{C(S_t,t)=S_t\Phi(d_1)-Ke^{-r\tau}\Phi(d_2).}\]

Roughly speaking, this formula computes:

\textbf{[an investor's expected return]} minus \textbf{[the cost of the option]}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{S\(_0\)\(\cdot\)N(d\(_1\))} --- the "return" side: today's stock price, multiplied by a probability-like weighting N(d\(_1\)) that is close to 0 if the stock is likely to finish below the strike price, and represents the stock's expected contribution to value if it finishes above the strike.


\item \(Ke^{-r(T-t)}\Phi(d_2)\) --- the ``cost'' side: the strike price,
\textbf{discounted back to its present value}, multiplied by the
risk-neutral exercise-probability term \(\Phi(d_2)\).


\end{itemize}

\subsection{Discounting to Present Value}

The factor \(e^{-r(T-t)}\) discounts the strike price \(K\) back to today's
dollars using continuous compounding. This adjustment reflects the return
available from investing at the risk-free rate over the same period.

\subsection{The Probability Terms}

These probability weightings come from the standard normal probability distribution (a bell curve). The Black-Scholes-Merton model assumes that if you plotted the periodic (e.g., daily) returns of the underlying, the resulting distribution would be \textbf{normal} (bell-shaped) --- and it further assumes future returns will continue to follow that same normal distribution.

\textbf{N(d\(_1\))} is, roughly, equivalent to \textbf{N(z-score)} --- the area under the standard normal bell curve up to a particular \textbf{z-score}, representing the (risk-adjusted) probability that the future price will finish above the strike price at expiration. Recall the standard definition of a z-score:

\(z=(x-\mu)/\sigma\)

Mapping this general definition onto the Black-Scholes d\(_1\) formula:

\[d_1=\frac{\ln(S_t/K)+(r+\tfrac12\sigma^2)\tau}{\sigma\sqrt{\tau}}.\]

\begin{itemize}
\item The \textbf{numerator}, ln(S\(_0\)/K) + (r + \(\sigma\)\(^2\)/2)(T\(-\)t), plays the role of "\textbf{x \(-\) \(\mu\)}": ln(S\(_0\)/K) is the \emph{value above the strike price} being measured (in log terms), while (r + \(\sigma\)\(^2\)/2)(T\(-\)t) acts as the \textbf{mean} of the underlying normal distribution --- reflecting the risk-free rate plus the spread of possible option outcomes accumulated over time.


\item The \textbf{denominator}, \(\sigma\)\(\sqrt{\phantom{x}}\)(T\(-\)t), plays the role of "\textbf{\(\sigma\)}" (the standard deviation) --- here, a function of volatility scaled by the square root of time remaining.


\end{itemize}

Both \textbf{N(d\(_1\))} and \textbf{N(d\(_2\))} are, mechanically, just \textbf{calculations of area under this bell curve} up to a given point. If the curve represents the full normal distribution of probabilities for the option's outcome, then specifically \textbf{N(d\(_2\)) represents the percentage probability that the option will expire in the money} (i.e., that it will actually be exercised).

\section{Worked Numerical Example}

\textbf{Problem:} You're considering buying a European call option with the following parameters. Using the Black-Scholes-Merton formula, what is the theoretical fair price of this option --- i.e., the maximum you should be willing to pay for it to be worth buying?

\begin{itemize}
\item Stock price (S\(_0\)): \textbf{\$50}


\item Strike price (K): \textbf{\$45}


\item Time to expiration: \textbf{80 days}


\item Risk-free interest rate (r): \textbf{2\%}


\item Implied volatility (\(\sigma\)): \textbf{30\%}


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Step 1 --- Compute d\(_1\):}

\[
d_1=\frac{\ln(50/45)+(0.02+0.3^2/2)(80/365)}
          {0.3\sqrt{80/365}}.
\]

Here \(\ln(50/45)\approx0.105\), the drift contribution is approximately
\(0.014\), and the denominator is approximately \(0.140\).

\(d_1\approx0.851\)

\textbf{Step 2 --- Compute d\(_2\):}

\[
d_2=d_1-\sigma\sqrt{T-t}
   =0.851-0.3\sqrt{80/365}.
\]

\(d_2\approx0.711\)

\textbf{Step 3 --- Look up N(d\(_1\)) and N(d\(_2\))} from a standard normal z-score table:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{N(d\(_1\)) = N(0.851) \(\approx\) 0.8023} (\(\approx\) 80\%)


\item \textbf{N(d\(_2\)) = N(0.711) \(\approx\) 0.7611} (\(\approx\) 76\%)


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Step 4 --- Plug into the formula:}

\[
\begin{aligned}
C_0
&=50(0.8023)-45e^{-0.02(80/365)}(0.7611)\\
&\approx40.12-34.10
=\boxed{6.02}.
\end{aligned}
\]

\textbf{Interpretation:} The stock's expected contribution to value (weighted by the ~80\% probability term) is about \$40.12, out of a \$50 stock price. The strike price of \$45, discounted to its present value of about \$44.80, weighted by the ~76\% probability the option finishes in the money, contributes a "cost" of \$34.10. The difference --- \textbf{\$6.02} --- is the theoretical fair price of the call option: you should pay less than roughly \$6.02 for it to be worth buying.

\section{Practice Problem: Backing Out Implied Volatility}

The formula can also be run ``in reverse'': given a known market price for an option, solve for the implied volatility (\(\sigma\)) that the market must be assuming, since \(\sigma\) is the only input not directly observable in the market.

\textbf{Problem setup:} Suppose you want to know what the market thinks the implied volatility of the S\&P 500 is today.

\begin{itemize}
\item Assume every other investor is using the Black-Scholes-Merton formula for pricing.


\item The SPY ETF (which tracks the S\&P 500) is priced today at \textbf{\$216}.


\item A European call option has a strike price of \textbf{\$210}.


\item There are \textbf{30 days} left until expiration.


\item The risk-free interest rate is \textbf{1.8\%}.


\item The market is currently pricing this call option at \textbf{\$7.93}.


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Question: what is the implied volatility?}

Because \(\sigma\) cannot be isolated algebraically from the Black-Scholes formula, this type of problem is typically solved \textbf{numerically} --- e.g., using a tool like Excel's "Goal Seek" feature to iteratively adjust \(\sigma\) until the formula's output matches the observed market price of \$7.93. (The candidate answer choices given alongside this practice problem were: 1.0\%, 10.0\%, 15.7\%, 16.5\%, 25.2\%, 25.4\%, and 50.6\% --- numerical root-finding gives \(\sigma\approx0.1647\), so the closest listed answer is \(16.5\%\).)

\section{Delta Hedging --- Eliminating Risk in Practice}

Once an option has been priced, the next practical step is to actually \textbf{hedge} the position by buying and selling the underlying asset in just the right proportions, so as to "eliminate risk." This is called \textbf{delta hedging} (or \textbf{dynamic hedging}) --- the same mechanism formally derived in Section 2.4 above.

\textbf{Goal:} maintain a portfolio with \textbf{zero net delta} at all times, where delta (\(\Delta\)) --- one of the "option Greeks" (see previous notes, Section 10) --- measures the sensitivity of the option's price to changes in the underlying asset's price. This is a fairly complex, continuous hedging exercise, principally carried out by large investment institutions (investment banks, hedge funds, private equity funds, etc.).

\textbf{Formal definition:}

\[\Delta=V_S=\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}.\]

--- the first derivative of the option's value with respect to the underlying asset's price. In practice, delta hedging means continually buying or selling units of the underlying asset in response to changes in its price, in order to keep the \emph{combined} position's sensitivity to price (\(\partial\)S) effectively static at zero --- even though the underlying's value itself is constantly changing.

\textbf{Important clarification --- what risk is (and isn't) being eliminated:} delta hedging is designed to offset small continuous moves in the underlying. It does not remove volatility, gamma, transaction-cost, discrete-rebalancing, or jump risk; sharp price gaps are therefore among the risks that remain --- the "tail" events in the return distribution where an asset's price jumps sharply in a single day and then partially or fully corrects in the days that follow. Also worth remembering: \textbf{risk is never actually fully "eliminated"} in practice --- that's the theoretical goal of the model, but real hedged positions still carry residual risks that are harder to control for, such as \textbf{default risk} (the risk that a counterparty fails to honor the contract).

\section{Criticisms of the Black-Scholes-Merton Model}

The model, while foundational, has drawn substantial and well-documented criticism --- most notably from \textbf{Nassim Nicholas Taleb} (author of the 2007 bestseller \emph{"Black Swan"}, about unpredictable events in financial markets) together with \textbf{Espen Gaarder Haug}, who have argued the model is \textbf{"fragile to jumps and tail events"} and can only handle \textbf{"mild randomness."}

Specific, well-known challenges include:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Fragility to tail risk / extreme randomness:} in reality, asset returns do not perfectly follow a normal distribution. Statistical testing (the Anderson-Darling normality test) applied to S\&P 500 returns yields a p-value of 0.000, indicating market returns are actually \textbf{leptokurtic} --- more concentrated around the mean, but with \textbf{fatter tails} (more extreme outliers) than a true normal distribution would predict.


\item \textbf{The model's structure doesn't reflect present-day market realities:} the standard model assumes a market of \textbf{European}-style call options, but most options actually traded today are \textbf{American}-style (exercisable at any time before expiration). It also does not natively account for \textbf{dividends}, which are common in real options markets.


\item \textbf{The risk-free interest rate assumption is imperfect:} a truly theoretical risk-free rate is hard to pin down. In practice, investors substitute proxies such as a maturity-matched government or overnight-index-swap rate --- but this treats Treasury bonds as genuinely "risk-free," when a more accurate characterization is that they're simply the investment the market currently regards as \emph{least} risky.


\item \textbf{The assumption of costless trading is unrealistic:} real trading involves exchange fees, transaction costs for buying/selling stocks and options, and time costs (the delay between placing an order and its execution can itself allow the market price to move). These costs can be managed in practice but are not built into the model itself.


\item \textbf{Gap risk:} the model assumes continuous trading, but real markets close overnight (and on weekends/holidays) and can reopen at significantly different prices once new information arrives --- a discontinuity the model does not capture.


\end{itemize}

\textbf{Despite these issues:} empirically, pricing discrepancies between Black-Scholes-Merton predictions and real market prices occur \emph{more often} than would be expected if returns were truly log-normal. Nonetheless, the model \textbf{continues to be widely used} in practice --- it is simple, computationally easy, well understood, and can be adjusted or extended to compensate for many of its inadequacies.

\subsection{The Volatility Smile}

One of the most common and visible criticisms of the model is the existence of the \textbf{volatility smile}. The Black-Scholes-Merton model assumes \textbf{constant volatility} and log-normal return distributions --- implying that, for options at different strike prices (but the same underlying and expiration), the implied volatility used to price them should be roughly the same. In reality, implied volatility \textbf{varies substantially by strike price}. Specifically, options whose strike prices are \textbf{"deep in the money"} or \textbf{"far out of the money"} (i.e., strikes further away from the current underlying price) tend to command \textbf{higher prices} than a flat, constant-volatility assumption would predict --- meaning their \emph{implied} volatility, backed out from their market price, is higher. When implied volatility is plotted against strike price, the resulting curve is often U-shaped or "smile"-shaped (also discussed as the volatility skew in the previous notes, Section 11).

\section{Advanced Technical Notes (Notation and Modeling Choices)}

These points, while more advanced, help clarify exactly what the model is (and isn't) doing mathematically:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The notation used in finance is not strictly standard mathematical notation} --- it is a convention specific to the finance industry.


\item The functions \(\Phi(d_1)\) and \(\Phi(d_2)\) are values of the \textbf{standard normal CDF}; the stock price itself is lognormally distributed under GBM. This relies on an underlying standard normal distribution (mean 0, standard deviation 1) that is \emph{assumed} but often not explicitly stated.


\item \textbf{Why a log-normal distribution is used at all:} the model is fundamentally describing \textbf{compound interest}, which behaves as a power law (multiplicative growth). Taking the \textbf{logarithm} of growth factors converts this multiplicative process into something nearly \textbf{linear}, and the resulting distribution of log-returns becomes approximately \textbf{normal}. The parameters \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\) in this context represent the expected growth factor (akin to an interest rate) and the expected standard deviation (volatility) for one time period --- so values close to 0 are typically expected for the log-return parameters.


\item \textbf{Continuous functions are used to approximate what are really discrete, periodic processes} (e.g., dividends and interest are, in reality, usually paid periodically --- quarterly, annually --- not continuously). Using continuous compounding simplifies the math considerably, though this simplification is often left unstated.


\item \textbf{What's fundamentally being modeled is a random one-dimensional walk (or martingale).} Since a binomial distribution converges to (approximates) a normal distribution over a large number of trials --- such as many small price changes accumulated over a year --- modeling the stock's price path as normally/log-normally distributed is a reasonable large-sample approximation of an underlying discrete, binomial-like process (this connects to the binomial option pricing model as an alternative/complementary framework).


\item \textbf{A technical constraint:} because the model takes the logarithm of the stock price, that argument must always be a \textbf{positive real number} --- this is required to avoid undefined results (infinities or complex numbers), which is automatically satisfied since stock prices can never be zero or negative.


\end{itemize}

\subsection{The Payoff Functions, Formally}

For a call option, the payoff at expiration is only positive if the underlying finishes above the strike (otherwise the option is simply not exercised, since it would represent an immediate loss):

\[H_{\mathrm{call}}(S_T)=\max(S_T-K,0).\]

Symmetrically, for a put option, the payoff is only positive if the underlying finishes below the strike:

\[H_{\mathrm{put}}(S_T)=\max(K-S_T,0).\]

The option's price today is then the \textbf{discounted, risk-neutral expectation} of this payoff --- matching exactly the general master formula derived in Section 3.7 above, just written out with the specific call/put payoff functions substituted in.

\subsection{Closed-Form via the Error Function (erf)}

As an alternative, mathematically equivalent way of writing the standard normal CDF used throughout this model, some technical references express \textbf{N(x)} using the \textbf{error function}, erf:

\(\Phi(x)=\tfrac12\operatorname{erfc}(-x/\sqrt2)\), where \(\operatorname{erfc}(z)=1-\operatorname{erf}(z)\), and \(\operatorname{erf}(z)=\tfrac2{\sqrt\pi}\int_0^z e^{-u^2}\,\dd u\)

Using this notation, the full closed-form Black-Scholes call and put pricing formulas can be rewritten entirely in terms of erf instead of N( ) --- this is purely a notational variant (common in some symbolic-math and computational tools, such as Mathematica) and represents exactly the same underlying pricing formula given in Section 4, just expressed with a different (but mathematically interchangeable) special function.

\section{Summary}

The Black-Scholes equation can be reached via two complementary derivations: (1) building a continuously-rebalanced, delta-hedged riskless portfolio and applying no-arbitrage logic to derive a PDE that the option's price must satisfy, or (2) directly computing the discounted, risk-neutral expected payoff of the option, using Girsanov's theorem to justify swapping the real-world drift \(\mu\) for the risk-free rate r. Both routes converge on the identical closed-form pricing formula. Understanding \emph{why} the formula works --- the probability interpretation of N(d\(_1\)) and N(d\(_2\)), the role of discounting, and the meaning of volatility (\(\sigma\)) as both an implied-market estimate and a statistical standard deviation --- matters just as much as being able to compute it, especially given the model's well-documented real-world limitations (fat tails, the volatility smile, unrealistic frictionless-market assumptions) discussed in Section 10.

\chapter{Option Pricing}

\section{Options Pricing — Overview}

\subsection{What Is Options Pricing?}

Options pricing is the process of determining the fair value of an option, which is a financial derivative contract. An option gives its holder the right, but not the obligation, to buy or sell an underlying asset (such as a stock) at a specific price (the \textbf{strike price} or \textbf{exercise price}) on or before a specific date (the \textbf{expiry/expiration date}).

\subsection{The Goal of Options Pricing}

The objective is to work out how much this "right" is worth today. This depends on six main inputs:

\begin{itemize}
\item The current price of the underlying stock
\item The strike price of the option
\item The time remaining until expiration
\item The volatility of the underlying stock
\item Prevailing interest rates
\item Dividends paid by the underlying (if any)
\end{itemize}
\subsection{Common Option Pricing Models}

\textbf{1. Black-Scholes Model}
\begin{itemize}
\item Used for pricing European options on non-dividend-paying stocks.
\item Assumes the underlying stock price follows a lognormal distribution.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{2. Binomial Model}
\begin{itemize}
\item Breaks the time to expiry into discrete intervals and models the up/down movement of the underlying price at each interval.
\item Can handle American options (which are exercisable before expiry), unlike the basic Black-Scholes model.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{Recommended Study Approach}

\begin{enumerate}
\item Watch the topic video first.
\item Then work through the supporting blogs alongside the CFA curriculum reading on the basics of derivatives pricing and valuation.
\begin{itemize}
\item Forwards and futures pricing reference: AnalystPrep — \emph{Pricing and Valuation Concepts}
\item Options pricing reference: PrepNuggets — \emph{Pricing and Valuation of Options}
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\section{Foundational Concepts in Derivative Pricing}

\emph{These ideas underpin the pricing of every derivative — forwards, futures, swaps, and options. Understanding them first makes the binomial option pricing model much more intuitive.}

\subsection{The Core Intuition: A Simple Analogy}

Imagine two people agree today (with just a handshake, i.e., no cash changes hands) to trade a specific item — say, an old putter worth \$100 today (its \textbf{spot price}) — six months from now for an agreed price of \$120 (the \textbf{forward price}). This is the simplest possible derivative contract: a \textbf{forward contract}.

\begin{itemize}
\item The person who agreed to \textbf{buy} the putter in the future is \textbf{long} the contract — they benefit if the item's price rises.
\item The person who agreed to \textbf{sell} the putter in the future is \textbf{short} the contract — they benefit if the item's price falls.
\end{itemize}
If, before the six months are up, one party wants to exit ("break") the contract, the question becomes: \textbf{what is this contract worth right now?} The answer depends on how the spot price of the putter has moved relative to the agreed forward price. This is the essence of derivatives pricing and valuation — and the same logic scales up to gold, oil, stock indices, or anything else that can be bought or sold in the future.

\subsection{Arbitrage}

\textbf{Arbitrage} means buying an asset in one market and simultaneously selling it (or an equivalent) in another market at a different price, capturing a profit with \textbf{no risk}.

Conditions for genuine arbitrage:
\begin{itemize}
\item Requires \textbf{no net investment} of your own money.
\item Involves \textbf{no risk}.
\item Generates a \textbf{positive, riskless return}.
\end{itemize}
In real markets, arbitrage opportunities are rare and short-lived, because well-capitalized traders (often backed by large institutions, using algorithms) act on them instantly, causing the two prices to converge. This is why derivative prices in liquid markets rarely deviate from their no-arbitrage value for long.

\subsection{Replication}

\textbf{Replication} means constructing a portfolio of other assets that reproduces the exact payoff of a given asset or derivative.

Example: If you \textbf{own} the putter (long the spot asset) and simultaneously \textbf{agree to sell it} in the future via a forward contract (short the forward), the combination behaves exactly like an investment in a risk-free asset (e.g., a Treasury bill maturing on the same date as the forward). This is because:
\begin{itemize}
\item Owning the spot asset benefits you when prices rise (long).
\item Being short the forward benefits you when prices fall.
\item Combined, the position is insulated from price moves — it behaves like a riskless bond.
\end{itemize}
Replication is one of the two pillars (along with arbitrage) that pin down the \emph{unique} no-arbitrage price of any derivative.

\subsection{Risk Neutrality}

At first this sounds strange — real investors are \emph{not} indifferent to risk. However, decades of research have shown that when \textbf{pricing derivatives}, we can assume the underlying asset grows at the risk-free rate, and this "risk-neutral" assumption gives the correct derivative price. This works because the derivative's payoff can always be replicated using the underlying asset and a risk-free bond (arbitrage + replication), so the \emph{actual} real-world probabilities of the asset going up or down turn out to be irrelevant to the derivative's price. Risk-neutral pricing is a \emph{modeling convenience} built directly on the arbitrage and replication arguments above — not a claim that real investors don't care about risk.

\textbf{Summary of the three pillars:}
\begin{quote}
We can form a risk-free portfolio consisting of the underlying asset and a derivative (forward, future, option, or swap) on it. This portfolio must earn the risk-free rate. There is only \textbf{one} derivative price consistent with that condition — and that price is the one implied by arbitrage and replication.
\end{quote}

\subsection{Value vs. Price — A Critical Distinction}

This distinction is heavily tested and is central to every derivative pricing topic:

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.18\textwidth}L{0.34\textwidth}L{0.38\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Point in time} & \textbf{Value of the derivative} & \textbf{Price of the derivative} \\
\midrule
\textbf{At initiation} (t = 0) & \textbf{Zero} (for forwards, futures, swaps — no cash/assets exchanged at signing, other than an option's premium) & The agreed forward/exercise price is set \\
\textbf{During the contract's life} (0 < t < T) & \textbf{Not zero} — changes as the spot price moves & The forward price agreed at initiation stays fixed \\
\textbf{At maturity/expiration} (t = T) & Equal to the difference between the spot price at expiry and the agreed forward/exercise price & N/A — contract settles \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}

Options are the one exception at initiation: because the buyer pays the seller a \textbf{premium} up front, an option does have value (its time value) at t = 0, even though forwards/futures/swaps have zero value at initiation.

\textbf{General valuation logic (forward contract, no interim cash flows on the underlying):}

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Value during life = Spot price at time t  -  PV(Forward price agreed at t=0)
                   = St - F0(T) / (1+r)^(T-t)
\end{lstlisting}

This is exactly analogous to bond and stock pricing — \textbf{the value of a derivative is always a present value calculation.}

At maturity, whichever party is "in the money" collects the difference between the spot price and the agreed price. In practice, most derivatives are \textbf{cash-settled} rather than requiring physical delivery of the underlying asset.

\subsection{Monetary vs. Non-Monetary Benefits (and Costs) of Holding the Underlying}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Monetary benefits}: coupon payments (bonds) and dividend payments (stocks) — cash received simply for owning the underlying asset. You do \textbf{not} receive these if you only hold the derivative instead of the asset itself.
\item \textbf{Convenience yield}: a \emph{non-monetary} benefit that accrues to whoever physically holds the underlying asset (rather than a paper claim on it) — e.g., being able to immediately capture value if the item's spot price unexpectedly spikes, or (for commodities) not being caught short of an input needed for production.
\item \textbf{Costs of holding the underlying}: storage costs, insurance costs, and other opportunity costs incurred by physically holding the asset until the forward's maturity.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{The Cost-of-Carry Model}

Because benefits (dividends, interest, convenience yield — denoted \textbf{\ensuremath{\gamma}}, "gamma") and costs (storage, insurance, financing — denoted \textbf{\ensuremath{\theta}}, "theta") affect how expensive it is to "carry" the underlying asset to the future delivery date, they must be built into the forward price:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Costs increase} the forward price.
\item \textbf{Benefits decrease} the forward price.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Formula (costs/benefits expressed as currency amounts, compounded to maturity):}

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(T) = [S0 - (gamma - theta)] x (1 + rf)^T
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Worked example:} Spot = \$55, net cost of carry = +\$3 (net cost, i.e., costs exceed benefits), risk-free rate = 3\%.

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(T) = (55 - 3) x (1.03)^1 = $53.56
\end{lstlisting}

Note that here the forward price (\$53.56) is actually \textbf{below} the spot price (\$55) — there is no rule that forward prices must exceed spot prices. Commodities typically have forward prices \emph{above} spot (since storage costs dominate and there's little "dividend-like" benefit to holding a physical commodity), but for financial assets like equity indices the relationship can go either way depending on the balance of dividends vs. financing costs, and can flip during periods of market stress (e.g., 2020 crash) or high inflation (e.g., 2022).


\section{Forward and Futures Contracts — Detailed Pricing and Valuation}

\emph{Deeper, formula-driven treatment of forward/futures pricing — the natural stepping stone before options and the binomial model, since options pricing reuses the same no-arbitrage logic.}

\subsection{Key Definitions}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Forward commitment}: a derivative contract that obligates one party to buy, and the other to sell, an underlying asset at a predetermined price at a future date.
\item \textbf{Price} of a forward/futures contract = the prespecified price the long pays the short at expiration/settlement.
\item \textbf{Value} of a forward/futures contract = its current monetary worth, which changes after inception as the spot price moves.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{The No-Arbitrage Approach}

Two rules define arbitrage-free pricing:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Do not use your own money} — the arbitrageur borrows to buy the underlying, and invests proceeds from any short sale at the risk-free rate.
\item \textbf{Do not take any price risk} — all market price risk from the underlying and the derivative must be eliminated (fully hedged).
\end{enumerate}
\textbf{No-arbitrage principle}: it is impossible to generate riskless profit today with no future liability. This implies:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Law of one price}: two portfolios with identical future cash flows (regardless of what happens) must have the same price today.
\item A portfolio that generates a certain (riskless) payoff must earn exactly the risk-free rate.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Illustrative example}: An investor goes short a September E-Mini S\&P 500 futures contract on 30 October 2020 at \$3,225 (initial value = \$0, as expected). The next trading day, the futures price rises to \$3,324. The short position's value is now \textbf{−\$99} (the short loses as the price they're obligated to sell at falls behind the market).

\subsection{Forward Pricing (No Interim Cash Flows)}

The forward price is the price that prevents riskless arbitrage in a frictionless market — it equals the spot price compounded forward at the risk-free rate over the contract's life:

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(T) = S0 x (1 + rf)^T          or equivalently         S0 = F0(T) / (1 + rf)^T
\end{lstlisting}
Where: F0(T) = forward price; S0 = spot price at contract initiation; rf = risk-free rate; T = term of the contract.

\textbf{Example:} Asset priced at \$130, risk-free rate 4\% annually. One-year forward price:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(1) = 130 x (1.04)^1 = $135.20
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Forward Mispricing and Arbitrage}

An arbitrage opportunity exists whenever \texttt{F0(T) ≠ FV(S0)}, where \texttt{FV(S0) = S0 × (1+rf)\textasciicircum{}T}.

\begin{itemize}
\item If \textbf{F0(T) > FV(S0)} → the forward is \textbf{overpriced}. The arbitrageur \textbf{shorts the forward and longs the underlying} ("carry arbitrage"). This drives the forward price down and the spot price up until they converge.
\item If \textbf{F0(T) < FV(S0)} → the forward is \textbf{underpriced}. The arbitrageur \textbf{buys the forward and shorts the underlying} ("reverse carry arbitrage"), driving the forward price up to equal the future value of the underlying.
\end{itemize}
\subsubsection{a) Carry Arbitrage (forward overpriced)}

\textbf{Example:} Asset priced at \$130, risk-free rate 4\%. Quoted forward price F0(T) = \$140 (overpriced, since fair value is \$135.20).

\emph{Cash flows at initiation:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Short the forward contract at \$140.
\item Borrow +\$130 at the risk-free rate.
\item Purchase the underlying using the loan: −\$130.
\item Borrow the (discounted) arbitrage profit:
\end{itemize}
  \texttt{PV[F0(T) − S0(1+rf)\textasciicircum{}T] = PV[\$140 − \$135.20] = \$4.8 / 1.04 = +\$4.615}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Initial cash flow = −\$130 + \$130 + @@I0@@4.62} (riskless profit today).
\end{itemize}
\emph{Cash flows at time T:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Deliver the underlying to the short-forward counterparty, receive +\$140.
\item Repay the loan: (130 + 4.62) × 1.04 = −\$140.
\item \textbf{Net cash flow at T = −\$140 + @@I0@@0.}
\end{itemize}
The arbitrageur pockets \$4.62 today and has zero net exposure at T — a genuine riskless profit.

\subsubsection{b) Reverse Carry Arbitrage (forward underpriced)}

\textbf{Example:} Asset priced at \$130, risk-free rate 4\%. Quoted forward price F0(T) = \$115 (underpriced).

\emph{At contract initiation:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Long the forward contract at \$130... \emph{(fair value benchmark)} — actually enter long forward at the quoted underpriced rate.
\item Short-sell the underlying at \$130.
\item Lend the \$130 proceeds from the short sale.
\item Borrow the arbitrage profit: \texttt{PV[FV(S0) − F0(T)] = PV[\$135.20 − \$130] = \$5.20/1.04 = +\$5.00}
\item \textbf{Cash flow at time 0: +\$130 − \$130 + @@I0@@5.00.}
\end{itemize}
\emph{Cash flows at time T:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Collect the loan: (\(130)(1.04) = +\)135.20.
\item Repay the borrowed arbitrage profit: −\(5.00(1.04) = -\)5.20.
\item The long forward position delivers −\$130 to acquire the underlying.
\item Use the underlying to cover (close out) the short position.
\item \textbf{Net cash flow at T: +\$135.20 − \$5.20 − @@I0@@0.}
\end{itemize}
Again, riskless profit (\$5.00) is captured today, with zero net exposure later.

\subsection{Valuing a Forward Contract (No Cash Flows on Underlying)}

At initiation (t = 0), the value to the \textbf{long} position:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
V0 = S0 - F0(T)/(1+rf)^T           (this equals $0 by construction)
\end{lstlisting}

During the contract's life, the value to the long position is the discounted difference between the \emph{current} forward price for a matching maturity and the \emph{original} forward price locked in:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Vt(T) = [Ft(T) - F0(T)] / (1+i)^(T-t)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Example:} Long one-year forward purchased at \$130. Three months later, the contract's (new) price is \$132. Risk-free rate = 4\%.
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Vt(T) = ($132 - $130) / (1.04)^(9/12) = $1.9420
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Equity Forward Contracts}

\subsubsection{With Discrete Cash Flows (dividends)}

Dividends (\textbf{\ensuremath{\gamma}}, carry benefit) and costs like interest/storage (\textbf{\ensuremath{\theta}}, carry cost) adjust the forward pricing formula. Costs \emph{increase} the burden of carry (added); benefits \emph{decrease} it (subtracted):

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(T) = [S0 - (gamma - theta)] x (1 + rf)^T
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Example 1:} Stock priced at \$50, expected to pay a \$1 dividend in 30 days and another \$1 in 90 days. Interest rate = 4\%. Price a 70-day forward:
\begin{itemize}
\item Only the 30-day dividend falls within the 70-day contract term.
\item \texttt{\ensuremath{\gamma} = 1 × (1.04)\textasciicircum{}(−30/365) = \$0.9968}
\item \texttt{F0(T) = [\$50 − (\$0.9968 − 0)] × (1.04)\textasciicircum{}(70/365) = \$49.37}
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Example 2:} Spot = \$100, asset pays a \$2 dividend in 6 months, risk-free rate = 8\%. Price the one-year forward:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{\ensuremath{\gamma} = 2 × (1.08)\textasciicircum{}(−6/12) = \$1.9245}, \texttt{\ensuremath{\theta} = 0}
\item \texttt{F0(T) = [100 − (1.9245 − 0)] × (1.08)\textasciicircum{}1 = \$105.92}
\end{itemize}
\subsubsection{With Continuous Cash Flows}

For stock indices with many constituent stocks (e.g., S\&P 500), dividends are typically modeled as continuously compounded:

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(T) = S0 x e^[(rc + theta - gamma) x T]
\end{lstlisting}
Where rc = continuously compounded risk-free rate.

\textbf{Example:} Dow Jones (DJ30) spot = \$30,125. Continuous dividend yield = 3\%. Continuously compounded risk-free rate = 2\%. Six-month forward price:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(T) = 30,125 x e^[(0.02 + 0 - 0.03) x 0.5] = $29,974.75
\end{lstlisting}

\subsubsection{Equity Forward Valuation (Discrete Payments)}

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Vt(T) = St - (gamma - theta)(1+r)^t - F0(T) x (1+r)^(-(T-t))
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Example:} Stock priced at \$50, expected to pay dividends of \$1 in 30 days and 90 days, risk-free rate = 4\%. After 40 days, the stock price is \$40. Value the forward to the long position:

Step 1 — Recompute the (original) forward price using only the dividend that falls within the contract's term (30-day dividend; the 90-day dividend arrives after the 70-day contract already expired):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
gamma = 1 x (1.04)^(-30/365) = 0.9968
F0(T) = [$50 - 0.9968] x (1.04)^(70/365) = $49.37
\end{lstlisting}
Step 2 — Value the contract 40 days in (30 remaining days to expiry):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Vt(t) = 40 - 49.37/(1.04)^(30/365) = -$9.21
\end{lstlisting}
The forward's value to the \textbf{long} is \textbf{−\$9.21}; correspondingly, its value to the \textbf{short} is \textbf{+\$9.21}.

\subsection{Forward and Future Contracts on Fixed-Income Bonds}

The mechanics mirror dividend-paying stocks, except the periodic cash flows are \textbf{coupon payments} rather than dividends.

\textbf{Price of a forward on a fixed-income security:}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(T) = (S0 - PVC0) x (1 + rf)^T
\end{lstlisting}
Where PVC0 = present value of coupon payments expected before the forward expires.

\textbf{Example:} Forward contract duration = 200 days. Bond: \$100 par, 4\% coupon (semi-annual), spot price = \$110, risk-free rate = 8\%, next coupon in 160 days.
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Coupon payment = 4% x 0.5 x 100 = $2
PV(coupon) = 2 / (1.08)^(160/365) = $1.93
Forward Price = (110 - 1.93) x (1.08)^(200/365) = $112.72
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Value of a fixed-income forward at time t:}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Vt(Long) = (St - PVCt) - F0(T)/(1+rf)^(T-t)
\end{lstlisting}
or, if given a current forward price Ft(T) with the same maturity:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Vt(Long) = PV(t,T)[Ft(T) - F0(T)]
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Example:} \$100 par, 4\% semi-annual coupon bond, spot = \$100, matures in 200 days, just paid a coupon (next coupon due in 160 days). After 120 days, the bond's value is \$127. Risk-free rate = 8\%. Value the forward to the long:
\begin{itemize}
\item Only one coupon (due in 40 more days, i.e., 160−120) remains before the contract's maturity (in 80 more days, i.e., 200−120).
\end{itemize}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
PVCt = 2 / (1.08)^(40/365) = $1.9832
V120(Long) = $127 - 1.9832 - 112.72/(1.08)^(80/365) = $14.18
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Futures Contracts on Fixed-Income Bonds}

Bond prices are often quoted \textbf{clean} (excluding accrued interest since the last coupon):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Clean price = Full price - Accrued Interest (AI)
AI = (Days since last coupon / Days between coupons) x Coupon amount
\end{lstlisting}

Because multiple bonds may be eligible for delivery against a bond future, a \textbf{cheapest-to-deliver (CTD)} bond emerges once prices are adjusted by each bond's \textbf{conversion factor (CF)}. The buyer pays the futures price × the conversion factor at expiration:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(T) = QF0(T) x CF(T)
\end{lstlisting}
where QF0(T) is the \emph{quoted} futures price.

\textbf{Price of a bond futures contract} (carry arbitrage model — futures price equals the future value of the underlying, net of carry costs/benefits):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(T) = FV0,T(S0) - AIT - FVCI0,T
\end{lstlisting}
The conversion-factor-adjusted quoted futures price:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
QF0(T) = [1/CF(T)] x (FV0,T[B0(T+Y) + AI0] - AIT - FVCI0,T)
\end{lstlisting}
Where AIT = accrued interest at futures maturity; FVCI0,T = future value of any coupons received and reinvested during the futures' life.

\textbf{Example:} Six-month Euro bond futures, contract value €50,000. Underlying: 5\% bond quoted at €110, accrued interest of €1.00. No coupon payments occur before expiration. Conversion factor = 0.65, accrued interest at expiration = €3.00, risk-free rate = 4\%.
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
T = 6/12,  CF(T) = 0.65,  B0(T+Y) = EUR 110,  FVCI0,T = 0,  AI0 = EUR 1.00,  AIT = EUR 3.00,  r = 4%

QF0(T) = [1/0.65] x [(110 + 1) x (1.04)^0.5 - 3 - 0] = EUR 169.54
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Value of a bond future}: because futures are marked to market daily, their value resets to \textbf{zero} at the end of each trading day once gains/losses are settled — the value only accumulates \emph{within} a single day (the change in price since the previous settlement).

\subsection{Interest Rate Forward and Futures Contracts}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{LIBOR} (London Interbank Offered Rate): the classic reference rate for interbank borrowing, historically quoted as an annualized rate on a 360-day-year basis.
\item \textbf{Eurodollar deposits}: deposits at banks outside the US, denominated in USD.
\end{itemize}
\subsubsection{Forward Rate Agreements (FRAs)}

An FRA is a forward contract on an interest rate (e.g., LIBOR). The counterparties agree today on a fixed interest payment, to be exchanged at a future date for an unknown (floating) interest payment.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Short (fixed receiver / "lender")}: receives the fixed rate, pays the floating rate.
\item \textbf{Long (floating receiver / "borrower")}: receives the floating rate, pays the fixed rate — benefits when rates rise.
\item FRAs are \textbf{cash-settled} — no principal is actually lent or borrowed.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Notation — "X × Y" convention}: X = months until the FRA expires (and the underlying loan starts); Y = months until the underlying loan ends.

Example: a \textbf{6 × 9 FRA}
\begin{itemize}
\item Expires in 6 months.
\item The underlying loan settles in 9 months (i.e., runs for a further 3 months after the FRA expires).
\item Notional loan period = 3 months.
\item Payoff is based on 3-month LIBOR observed when the FRA expires (at month 6).
\end{itemize}
Equivalently written \textbf{FRA(0, h, m)}: fixed forward rate set at time 0, expiring at time h, with the underlying LIBOR deposit maturing m days later. A 6×9 FRA = FRA(0, 180, 90).

\textbf{FRA settlement conventions:}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Advanced set}: the reference rate is set (observed) at the FRA's expiration date h — this is when the underlying loan notionally begins.
\item \textbf{Advanced settled}: the cash settlement itself occurs at time h.
\item \textbf{Settled in arrears}: interest is paid at time h + m (when the underlying loan would have matured).
\item FRAs are usually \textbf{advanced set, advanced settled}; swaps and interest rate options are usually \textbf{advanced set, settled in arrears}.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Pricing an FRA} — find the fixed rate that gives the FRA zero value at inception:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
FRA(0,h,m) = { [1 + L0(h+m) x t(h+m)] / [1 + L0(h) x t(h)]  -  1 } / tm
\end{lstlisting}
This is read as: the compounded value of \$1 invested at the longer-term LIBOR for (h+m) days, divided by the compounded value of \$1 invested at the shorter-term LIBOR for h days, minus 1, then annualized.

Steps to price an FRA:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Lay out a timeline (0 → h → h+m).
\item De-annualize the fixed rate: \texttt{[1+L0(h+m)t(h+m)] / [1+L0(h)t(h)] − 1}
\item Annualize by multiplying by \texttt{360/m}.
\end{enumerate}
\textbf{Example:} Current 30-day LIBOR = 2\%, 180-day LIBOR = 3.5\% (30/360 convention). Price the 1×6 FRA:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
FRA(0,30,150) = { [1+0.035x(180/360)] / [1+0.02x(30/360)]  -  1 } / (150/360) = 3.79%
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Valuing an existing FRA} — done by comparing it to an \emph{offsetting} FRA priced at the \emph{current} market rate for the same remaining exposure:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Vg(0,h,m) = { [FRA(g,h-g,m) - FRA(0,h,m)] x tm } / [1 + Dg(h+m-g) x t(h+m-g)]
\end{lstlisting}
Process:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Compute the new FRA rate as of "today" (time g): \texttt{FRA(g,h−g,m)}.
\item Compute the payoff at \(h+m\):
\[
\text{Payoff}=(\mathrm{FRA}_g-\mathrm{FRA}_0)\left(\frac{m}{360}\right)\times\text{Notional}.
\]
\item Discount that payoff back to time g.
\end{enumerate}
\textbf{Worked example:} ABC Bank entered a 6×9 FRA three months ago as the \textbf{receive-floating} party. FRA rate = 0.75\%, notional = \$300,000. Now (3 months later, so g = 90 days): 90-day USD LIBOR = 0.80\%, 180-day LIBOR = 0.85\%. At the \emph{original} FRA's expiration, 3-month LIBOR = 1.00\%, 6-month LIBOR = 1.10\%. Discount rate for settlement cash flows = 1\%. (Note: since the FRA was entered 3 months ago and is a 6×9, it now has 3 months left until it expires.)

Step 1 — compute the new FRA rate, FRA(90, 90, 90):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
FRA(90,90,90) = { [1+0.0085x(180/360)] / [1+0.0080x(90/360)]  -  1 } x (360/90) = 0.8982%
\end{lstlisting}
Step 2 — payoff at h+m:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Payoff = (0.8982% - 0.75%) x (90/360) x $300,000 = $111.15
\end{lstlisting}
Step 3 — discount back to time g (90 days), at 1\%:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
V(0,180,90) = $111.15 / [1 + (0.01 x 180/360)] = $110.60
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Currency Forward and Futures Contracts}

Foreign currency is quoted in units of the \textbf{domestic} currency. The \textbf{covered interest rate parity} relationship is the carry-arbitrage model applied to FX — it gives the no-arbitrage forward price of one unit of foreign currency (F), in terms of the domestic currency (D):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
FT(D/F) = S0(D/F) x [(1+rD)^T / (1+rF)^T]
\end{lstlisting}
Continuous-compounding version:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
FT(D/F) = S0(D/F) x e^[(rD - rF) x T]
\end{lstlisting}
Convention: if the spot quote is expressed as \emph{base currency per unit of quote/price currency}, the base-currency interest rate is the numerator and the quote-currency rate is the denominator (e.g., in GBP/USD, GBP is the base and USD is the quote currency).

\textbf{Example:} Spot = \$0.06757 per South African Rand (ZAR). US risk-free rate = 4\%, South African risk-free rate = 6\%. Price a 90-day forward (365-day year):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
FT(USD/ZAR) = 0.06757 x [(1.04)^(90/365) / (1.06)^(90/365)] = $0.067
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Valuing a currency forward after inception:}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Vt(T) = PVt,T x [Ft(T) - F0(T)]
\end{lstlisting}
Note: the present value must be computed using the \textbf{quote (price) currency's} interest rate.

\textbf{Example:} A trader bought A\$500,000 against USD at F0(\$/A\() = \)0.76. Four months to expiry remain; the current forward price is Ft(\$/A\() = \)0.70. USD rate = 1.5\%, AUD rate = 1\%. Value the long position:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Vt(T) = (0.70 - 0.76) / (1.015)^(4/12) = -$0.0597 per AUD
Total value = -($0.0597/A$) x A$500,000 = -$59,702.97
\end{lstlisting}
(Note: the source example scales this to a notional of A\$1,000,000 in its final line, giving −\$59,702.97 — the value is negative to the long trader because the AUD has weakened relative to the contracted forward rate.)

\subsection{Futures Contracts — General Note}

The same forward pricing and valuation formulas generally apply to futures:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Forward pricing:    F0(T) = FV0,T(S0 + theta0 - gamma0)
Forward valuation:  Vt(T) = PVt,T[Ft(T) - F0(T)]
\end{lstlisting}
The key structural difference: futures are \textbf{marked to market daily}, so a futures contract's value resets to \textbf{zero} at the end of each trading day (gains and losses are settled in cash daily), whereas a forward's value simply accumulates until the contract is closed out or matures.

\subsection{Practice Questions (Forward Valuation)}

\textbf{Q1.} An investor purchased a one-year Eurobond forward contract six months ago with a notional value of €50,000 at a price of 100. The current forward price for a contract with six months remaining to maturity is 110. Risk-free rate = 3\%. Find the value of the forward position.

\emph{Solution:}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Vt(T) = PVt,T[Ft(T) - F0(T)] = (110 - 100) x (1.03)^(-0.5) = 9.853 per EUR 100 par
Position value = 0.09853 x EUR 50,000 = EUR 4,926.50
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Q2.} XYZ Inc. sold \$50,000 forward against EUR at a forward rate of EUR 0.78 per \$1 at time 0. At time t, the spot rate is EUR 0.72 per \$1; annually compounded risk-free rates are 2\% (EUR) and 5\% (USD). At time t, the forward contract has three months to expiration. Find the forward price at time t.

\emph{Solution:}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
FT(EUR/USD) = S0(USD) x [(1+rEUR)^T / (1+rUSD)^T]
            = 0.7200 x [(1.02)^(3/12) / (1.05)^(3/12)] = 0.7148
\end{lstlisting}


\section{Options — Basic Concepts, Moneyness, and Valuation}

\subsection{Recap of Basic Option Concepts}

\begin{itemize}
\item A \textbf{call option} gives the holder the \textbf{right, but not the obligation, to buy} the underlying asset at a given (exercise/strike) price.
\item A \textbf{put option} gives the holder the \textbf{right, but not the obligation, to sell} the underlying asset at a given price.
\item Options can be:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{American-style}: exercisable \textbf{at any time} up to and including the expiration date.
\item \textbf{European-style}: exercisable \textbf{only on} the expiration date.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Four basic option positions:}

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.28\textwidth}L{0.64\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Position} & \textbf{Right/Obligation} \\
\midrule
Long Call & Has the right to \textbf{buy} at a given price (pays the option premium) \\
Short Call & \textbf{Required to sell} at a given price if the long exercises \\
Long Put & Has the right to \textbf{sell} at a given price (pays the option premium) \\
Short Put & \textbf{Required to buy} at a given price if the long exercises \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}

The buyer of an option always pays a \textbf{premium} to the seller (writer) at initiation for the right they are acquiring. The Level I curriculum focuses primarily on \textbf{European} options.

\subsection{Moneyness}

Even though a European option can't be exercised early, it still has value before expiration — this concept is called \textbf{moneyness}:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{In-the-money (ITM)}: immediate exercise would generate a \textbf{positive payoff}.
\item \textbf{Out-of-the-money (OTM)}: immediate exercise would generate a \textbf{negative payoff} (so rationally, you wouldn't exercise).
\item \textbf{At-the-money (ATM)}: the spot price equals the exercise price — immediate exercise generates \textbf{no payoff}.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Summary of moneyness by position} (St = spot at time t, PV(X) = present value of strike):

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.18\textwidth}L{0.34\textwidth}L{0.38\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{} & \textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Moneyness} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Call} & St > PV(X) & In-the-money \\
\textbf{Call} & St = PV(X) & At-the-money \\
\textbf{Call} & St < PV(X) & Out-of-the-money \\
\textbf{Put} & St > PV(X) & Out-of-the-money \\
\textbf{Put} & St = PV(X) & At-the-money \\
\textbf{Put} & St < PV(X) & In-the-money \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}

\textbf{Value at expiration (T):}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Call:  CT = max(0, ST - X)
Put:   PT = max(0, X - ST)
\end{lstlisting}
Where X = exercise/strike price, ST = spot price at expiration.

\subsection{Intrinsic Value and Time Value}

An option's total value (its premium) is the sum of two components:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Option Value (Ct)  =  Intrinsic Value  +  Time Value (speculative value)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Intrinsic value} — the value the option would have if exercised right now. Importantly, under the current CFA curriculum (a change from the pre-2023 treatment), intrinsic value before expiration is based on the \textbf{present value of the strike price} (discounted at the risk-free rate), not the raw strike price:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Intrinsic value (call) = max(0, St - PV(X))
Intrinsic value (put)  = max(0, PV(X) - St)
where PV(X) = X / (1+Rt)^(T-t)
\end{lstlisting}
(Before 2023, the curriculum defined intrinsic/exercise value before expiration \emph{without} this time-value-of-money discounting — this is a nuance worth remembering for exam purposes.)

\textbf{Worked example:} A one-year European put on ABC stock, strike = \$80. Three months later, ABC trades at \$78; risk-free rate = 5\%. Find the intrinsic value.
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
PV(X) - St = 80/(1.05)^0.75 - 78 = -$0.87
\end{lstlisting}
Since the intrinsic value to the long position can never be negative, the intrinsic value here is \textbf{\$0} (the put is technically out-of-the-money on a present-value basis, even though the raw spot (\$78) is below the raw strike (\$80)).

\textbf{Time value (speculative value)}: the remainder of the premium beyond intrinsic value — it reflects the \emph{possibility} that the option becomes more valuable before expiration. Options (unlike forwards/futures/swaps) have \textbf{positive value at initiation} — this initial value is pure time value (since there's no intrinsic value yet if at-the-money). As the contract progresses, time value decays, and \textbf{at expiration, the option's entire value is its intrinsic value} (time value = 0 at T).

\subsection{Theoretical Bounds for Option Values}

At any time, option values are bounded as follows:

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.18\textwidth}L{0.34\textwidth}L{0.38\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{} & \textbf{Lower Bound} & \textbf{Upper Bound} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Call option} & Intrinsic value & Spot price \\
\textbf{Put option} & Intrinsic value & Strike price \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}

\textbf{Worked example:} XYZ stock trades at \$120. A call option with 3 months to expiration and strike \$105 trades at \$18. Risk-free rate = 4\%.

(a) Time value of the option:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Et = max(0, St - PV(X)) = 120 - 105/(1.04)^0.25 = $16.02
Time value = Ct - Et = $18 - $16.02 = $1.98
\end{lstlisting}
(b) Theoretical bounds:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Lower bound (Ct) = Intrinsic value Et = $16.02
Upper bound (Ct) = Spot price St = $120
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Six Key Factors Affecting European Option Values}

The first two factors affect \textbf{intrinsic value}; the remaining four affect \textbf{time value}.

\textbf{1. Spot Price}
\begin{itemize}
\item Call options: higher spot price → greater intrinsic value → higher option value.
\item Put options: higher spot price → lower option value.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{2. Strike Price}
\begin{itemize}
\item Call options: higher strike price → lower intrinsic value.
\item Put options: higher strike price → higher intrinsic value.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{3. Time to Expiration}
\begin{itemize}
\item Generally, longer time to expiration → higher time value for both calls and puts.
\item Shorter time to expiration → lower time value.
\item Caveat: this relationship reliably holds for calls, but may \textbf{not always hold for European puts} in certain exceptional cases.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{4. Risk-Free Rate}
\begin{itemize}
\item Higher risk-free rate → higher value for European calls, lower value for European puts.
\item Lower risk-free rate → lower value for European calls, higher value for European puts.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{5. Volatility of the Underlying}
\begin{itemize}
\item Higher volatility → higher time value (for both calls and puts) — more volatility means a greater chance of a large favorable move, and losses are capped at the premium either way.
\item Lower volatility → lower time value.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{6. Costs and Benefits of Holding the Underlying Asset}
\begin{itemize}
\item Call options: expected \textbf{costs} of holding the underlying (e.g., storage) \textbf{increase} the call's value; expected \textbf{benefits} (e.g., dividends) \textbf{decrease} it.
\item Put options: expected \textbf{costs} \textbf{decrease} the put's value; expected \textbf{benefits} \textbf{increase} it.
\end{itemize}
\emph{Intuition: a call option holder effectively "misses out" on carrying costs the actual asset owner would pay, so those costs get built into a higher call premium as compensation to the seller/reflection of forgone drag; conversely the call holder also misses out on the benefits the owner enjoys, which reduces relative call value. The logic mirrors, in reverse, how costs/benefits shift the forward price up or down in Section 2.7.}


\section{Put-Call Parity}

\subsection{The Replication Argument}

Put-call parity is a direct application of the \textbf{replication} principle (Section 2.3) to options.

\textbf{Strategy 1 — Protective Put}: own the underlying asset (long, benefits when prices rise) \textbf{and} buy a put option on it (long put, benefits when prices fall). Together, this hedges the position — you own the asset but are protected against downside.
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Value at inception = P0 + S0
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Strategy 2 — Fiduciary Call}: buy a call option (benefits when prices rise) \textbf{and} buy a risk-free zero-coupon bond whose maturity value equals the options' exercise price.
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Value at inception = C0 + PV(Bond)
\end{lstlisting}

If the call and put share the same exercise price and expiration, and the bond's maturity value equals that exercise price, then \textbf{these two portfolios produce identical payoffs at expiration} under every possible future scenario. By the law of one price, they must therefore have the same value (cost) today:

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
C0 + PV(X) = P0 + S0
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{This equation is put-call parity.}

\subsection{Rearranging: Synthetic Positions}

Because put-call parity is an equality, it can be rearranged to construct any of the four components synthetically from the other three:

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Synthetic Call:  C0 = P0 + S0 - PV(X)     (buy put + buy stock + sell/short a risk-free bond)
Synthetic Put:   P0 = C0 - S0 + PV(X)
Synthetic Bond:  PV(X) = C0 - P0 + S0 (rearranged)
\end{lstlisting}

An \textbf{arbitrage opportunity is created whenever put-call parity does not hold} — i.e., whenever the actual market prices of C, P, S, and the bond deviate from this equality.

\subsection{Worked Example}

Given: exercise price X = \$50 (same for both put and call), option expires in 120 days, underlying asset price S0 = \$52, risk-free rate = 4.5\%, put price P0 = \$3.80. Find the call price.

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
C0 = P0 + S0 - PV(X)
   = 3.80 + 52 - 50/(1.045)^(120/365)
   = $6.52
\end{lstlisting}

If the actual quoted call price differs from \$6.52 (e.g., \$7.00), an arbitrage opportunity exists — the mispricing can be captured either by trading directly in whichever leg is mispriced, or by constructing the full replication strategy.

\subsection{Put-Call-Forward Parity}

The same relationship can be restated by substituting a \textbf{forward contract} in place of the underlying stock position:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
C0 + PV(X) = P0 + PV(F0(T))
\end{lstlisting}
By setting the call equal to the synthetic protective-put-on-a-forward, we get \textbf{put-call parity for options on forward contracts} — the same core idea, one layer removed (an option on a forward is, in a loose sense, a "derivative of a derivative").

\chapter{Binomial Model}

\setcounter{section}{5}

\section{The Binomial Option Pricing Model — Core Theory}

\subsection{What Is the Binomial Model, and Why Use It?}

Valuing options is inherently difficult: unlike a bond or a stock, an option's value depends on several interacting variables — the underlying price, strike price, time to expiration, interest rates, and (critically) volatility — combined with genuine uncertainty about the future. The \textbf{binomial model} tackles this by radically simplifying the problem:

\begin{quote}
At any given moment/step, the underlying price can only do one of \textbf{two} things: move \textbf{up} by some factor, or move \textbf{down} by some factor.
\end{quote}

This single simplification — repeated over many small discrete time steps — builds up a \textbf{binomial tree}: a branching diagram of every possible path the underlying's price could take between now and the option's expiration. Although reality is more continuous and complex, chaining enough small up/down steps together lets the tree closely approximate real-world price behavior.

Developed by \textbf{John Cox, Stephen Ross, and Mark Rubinstein in 1979}, the binomial model offered an alternative to the \textbf{Black-Scholes model} (Fischer Black and Myron Scholes, 1973). Black-Scholes uses continuous-time mathematics and closed-form equations, but:
\begin{itemize}
\item It assumes the underlying follows a smooth, continuous price path.
\item It assumes volatility stays \textbf{constant} over the option's life.
\item It is designed for \textbf{European} options and struggles to handle the early-exercise feature of \textbf{American} options.
\end{itemize}
The binomial model addresses these limitations directly: it is built from \textbf{discrete} steps (so it can naturally check, at every step, whether early exercise is optimal — making it well-suited to American options), and it can accommodate \textbf{changing volatility} over time (different u/d factors at different steps, if desired).

\subsection{Key Inputs and Notation}

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.28\textwidth}L{0.64\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\midrule
S (or S0, X) & Current stock/spot price \\
K (or X) & Strike/exercise price of the option \\
T & Time to expiration (in years) \\
N & Number of discrete time steps in the tree \\
\ensuremath{\Delta}t & Length of each step = T / N \\
r (or Rf) & Risk-free interest rate \\
u & "Up" factor — the multiple by which the price rises in an up move (u > 1); commonly modeled as u = \(e^{\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}}\), where \ensuremath{\sigma} = volatility \\
d & "Down" factor — the multiple by which price falls (0 < d < 1); often set as d = 1/u \\
p (or \ensuremath{\pi}, or q) & \textbf{Risk-neutral probability} of an up move (different course materials use different letters for the same concept — see note below) \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}

\textbf{Important note on notation across sources}: different instructors/materials in this course use different symbols for the same risk-neutral probability (\ensuremath{\pi}, p, q) and the same growth factor (1+Rf, R, e\textasciicircum{}(rt)). Throughout this section, whichever symbol a given worked example uses is kept consistent \emph{within} that example, and the underlying concept is always the same: \textbf{the probability under which the underlying's expected growth exactly equals the risk-free rate.}

This risk-neutral probability does \textbf{not} represent real-world (subjective) odds of the price going up or down. It is a purely theoretical construct, engineered specifically to enforce \textbf{no-arbitrage pricing} — the bedrock principle of modern derivatives theory (Section 2.2–2.4).

\subsection{The One-Step Binomial Tree — Setup}

Consider a stock priced at \textbf{S0} today. Over one period (e.g., one year), assume the stock can only move to one of two nodes:

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
                     ????  Su  = S0 x u     (probability p)
        S0  ??????????
                     ????  Sd  = S0 x d     (probability 1 - p)
\end{lstlisting}

For a \textbf{European call option} with strike K (assume Sd < K < Su, i.e., the strike sits between the two possible future prices):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Payoff if price goes up:    Cu = max(Su - K, 0)
Payoff if price goes down:  Cd = max(Sd - K, 0)   [often 0, if Sd < K]
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Deriving the Risk-Neutral Probability (Full Algebra)}

We want the underlying, growing at the risk-free rate under the risk-neutral measure, to have an \emph{expected} value equal to what compounding at (1 + risk-free rate) would give:

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
S0 x R  =  p x Su  +  (1 - p) x Sd          where R = 1 + Rf
\end{lstlisting}
Substitute Su = u × S0 and Sd = d × S0:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
S0 x R = p x u x S0 + (1 - p) x d x S0
\end{lstlisting}
S0 cancels from every term:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
R = pxu + (1-p)xd
\end{lstlisting}
Expand:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
R = pxu + d - pxd
\end{lstlisting}
Isolate the p terms:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
R - d = pxu - pxd = p(u - d)
\end{lstlisting}
Solve for p:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
p = (R - d) / (u - d)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{This is the master formula for the risk-neutral "up" probability} in the discrete one-step binomial world (R = 1 + risk-free rate). The probability of a "down" move is simply (1 − p).

\subsection{Worked Numeric Example (One-Step Tree, Discrete Compounding)}

Stock priced at 100 today. Strike (K) = 110. Expected up level = 120 → \textbf{u = 1.2}. Expected down level = 80 → \textbf{d = 0.8}. Risk-free rate = 8\% → \textbf{R = 1.08}.

\textbf{Step 1 — risk-neutral probability:}
