\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
p = (R - d)/(u - d) = (1.08 - 0.8)/(1.2 - 0.8) = 0.28/0.4 = 70%
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Step 2 — call payoffs at each node:}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
If price rises to 120: Call value = 120 - 110 = 10
If price falls to 80:  Call value = 0  (expires worthless -- out of the money)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Step 3 — probability-weighted expected payoff:}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Expected payoff = p x 10 + (1 - p) x 0 = 0.70 x 10 = 7
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Step 4 — discount to present value at the risk-free rate:}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Call value today = 7 / 1.08 = 6.481   (~ $6.48)
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Alternative Method: The Replicating Portfolio (Delta-Hedging) Approach}

Instead of computing a risk-neutral probability, we can directly build a portfolio of the \textbf{underlying stock} and a \textbf{risk-free bond} that exactly replicates the option's payoff in both the up and down states. Since the portfolio and the option have identical payoffs everywhere, they must have the same value today (no-arbitrage / law of one price).

\textbf{Setup}: Go \textbf{long \ensuremath{\Delta} (delta) shares} of stock and \textbf{short a bond} worth B at expiration (i.e., borrow the present value of B today).

Payoff equations (using the same numbers as above: S0=100, Su=120, Sd=80, Cu=10, Cd=0, R=1.08):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Up state:    Delta x Su  -  B  =  Cu     ->     Deltax120 - B = 10
Down state:  Delta x Sd  -  B  =  Cd     ->     Deltax80  - B = 0
\end{lstlisting}
Subtract the second equation from the first to eliminate B:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Deltax120 - Deltax80 = 10 - 0
Delta x 40 = 10
Delta = 0.25
\end{lstlisting}
So the replicating portfolio is \textbf{long 0.25 shares} (a quarter-share) of stock for every option sold/hedged.

Substitute \ensuremath{\Delta} = 0.25 back into the down-state equation to solve for B:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
0.25 x 80 - B = 0  ->  B = 20
\end{lstlisting}
This B = 20 is the amount \textbf{owed at expiration} (i.e., you must repay 20 at expiration on what you borrowed today).

\textbf{Present value of the replicating portfolio today:}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Value of shares today = Delta x S0 = 0.25 x 100 = 25
Present value of the bond owed = B / R = 20 / 1.08 = 18.41 (rounded)
Portfolio value today = 25 - 18.41 = 6.48 (approx -- matches exactly with rounding: 25 - 18.5185 = 6.48)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{This exactly matches the \$6.48 found via the risk-neutral probability method above} — confirming that both approaches (risk-neutral valuation and portfolio replication) are two routes to the \emph{same} no-arbitrage answer. This is the direct, hands-on demonstration of the "replication" principle introduced in Section 2.3.

\subsection{An Equivalent One-Period Model — Alternate Notation (as taught in the derivatives-basics lecture)}

The same one-period logic is sometimes presented with slightly different labels: c₀ for today's call price, c\textsuperscript{+} and c\textsuperscript{-} for the call's payoff in the up/down states, S\textsuperscript{+} and S\textsuperscript{-} for the up/down stock prices, and \textbf{\ensuremath{\pi}} (pi) for the risk-neutral up-probability:

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
pi = (1 + r - d) / (u - d)
c0 = [pi x c+  +  (1 - pi) x c-] / (1 + r)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Worked example:} Stock = \(50, strike = \)52, risk-free rate = 5\%, u = 1.2, d = 0.75.

\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
S+ = 50 x 1.2 = 60          S- = 50 x 0.75 = 37.50
c+ = max(60 - 52, 0) = 8    c- = max(37.50 - 52, 0) = 0   (out of the money -- expires worthless)

pi = (1.05 - 0.75) / (1.2 - 0.75) = 0.30/0.45 = 0.67  (67%)
1 - pi = 0.33  (33%)

c0 = [0.67 x 8 + 0.33 x 0] / 1.05 = 5.36/1.05 ~ $5.10
\end{lstlisting}

So the fair price to pay today for this call option (giving the right, but not the obligation, to buy the stock at \$52) is about \textbf{\$5.10}.

\textbf{Key intuitive takeaway repeated throughout this derivation}: the price of an option, just like the price of a bond or a stock, is fundamentally a \textbf{present value} calculation — you take an expected future payoff and discount it back at the risk-free rate. The subtlety unique to options/derivatives is that the "expectation" must be taken using \textbf{risk-neutral probabilities}, not real-world subjective probabilities.

\subsection{American vs. European Options in the Binomial Framework}

\begin{itemize}
\item For a \textbf{European} option, the payoff can only be realized at the single expiration date, so the binomial calculation above (discount the expected terminal payoff) is sufficient.
\item For an \textbf{American} option, at \emph{each} node in the tree, the model must compare the value of \textbf{holding} the option (its computed continuation value from later nodes) versus \textbf{exercising immediately} (the intrinsic value at that node), and takes the greater of the two.
\item \textbf{American call prices equal European call prices only when the underlying pays no dividends/interest} (i.e., options on non-dividend-paying stocks, commodities, or zero-coupon bonds). It is \emph{only} the existence of dividends or interest payments on the underlying that can ever create an incentive to exercise a call early — otherwise, holding the option is always at least as good as exercising it early.
\end{itemize}

\section{The Binomial Model — Full Generalization and Multi-Step Trees}

\subsection{Comparing Black-Scholes and the Binomial Model}

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.18\textwidth}L{0.34\textwidth}L{0.38\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Binomial} & \textbf{Black-Scholes} \\
\midrule
Price process & Discrete (up/down moves) & Continuous \\
Volatility & Can accommodate \textbf{changing} volatility & Assumes \textbf{constant} volatility \\
Option styles & Can value \textbf{American and European} & Designed for \textbf{European} only \\
Intuition & Relatively intuitive and easy to understand & Based on more complex mathematics \\
Computation & Can be computationally intensive for many steps & Less computationally intensive \\
Flexibility & More flexible, adaptable to real-world conditions & More restrictive assumptions \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}

In practice, many practitioners use \textbf{both}: Black-Scholes for a quick estimate, then the binomial model to refine or handle more complex features (e.g., early exercise, changing volatility, dividends) — and sometimes the binomial model is even used simply to \textbf{cross-check} a Black-Scholes result.

\subsection{Why the Actual Probability of Up/Down Doesn't Matter — A Clean Illustration}

Suppose a stock trades at \$100. A one-year, at-the-money call option has strike \$100. Assume the stock will either rise to \$110 or fall to \$90 in a year. Two traders disagree on the real-world odds:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Trader A} believes there's a 60\% chance the stock rises to \$110.
\item \textbf{Trader B} believes there's only a 40\% chance the stock rises to \$110.
\end{itemize}
Intuitively, you might expect the more "optimistic" Trader A to value the call higher. But under the binomial (risk-neutral) model, \textbf{both traders arrive at the exact same fair price of approximately \$7.14} (assuming a 5\% risk-free rate), regardless of their differing real-world beliefs.

\textbf{Why?} Because risk-neutral pricing does not use actual/subjective probabilities at all. What matters is purely the \emph{relationship} between the possible future prices (110, 90) and the current price (100) and the risk-free rate — not what anyone personally believes will happen. This is the practical payoff of everything built up in Sections 2.2–2.4 (arbitrage, replication, risk-neutrality).

\subsection{Binomial Calculation by Replicating Portfolio}

Using the \$100/\$110/\$90 example above, risk-free rate = 5\%:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Create a "risk-free" portfolio}: find a combination of stock and the option that gives the \emph{same} outcome regardless of which way the stock moves.
\item \textbf{Build the portfolio}: buy some number of shares ("d" — note: this source's notation for the hedge ratio, equivalent to \ensuremath{\Delta} above) and sell (short) one call option.
\item \textbf{Consider both scenarios:}
\begin{itemize}
\item If the stock rises to \$110: shares worth 110×d; the option written costs you the \$10 it's now in-the-money by: total = 110d − 10.
\item If the stock falls to \$90: shares worth 90×d; the option expires worthless (costs nothing): total = 90d.
\end{itemize}
\item \textbf{Make the portfolio risk-free} by setting both outcomes equal:
\end{enumerate}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
   110d - 10 = 90d
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Solve for d:}
\end{enumerate}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
   20d = 10   ->   d = 0.5
\end{lstlisting}
   You need half a share of stock for every option sold, to create a fully hedged, risk-free position.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Calculate the (certain) portfolio value at expiration}: using the down-state value, \texttt{90 × 0.5 = 45}.
\item \textbf{Apply the risk-free rate} to discount this certain future value back to the present:
\end{enumerate}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
   PV = 45 / (1 + 0.05) = 42.86
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Back out the option price}: the present value of the portfolio (42.86) must equal half a share's current value minus the option's price:
\end{enumerate}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
   42.86 = 0.5 x 100 - Option price
   Option price = 50 - 42.86 = $7.14
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Fair price of the call option = \$7.14.}

This approach never required guessing at the real-world probability of the stock going up or down. The logic is: if any obvious profit \emph{were} available, arbitrage traders would immediately act on it and eliminate that profit — so the price \emph{must} settle at the level that removes all riskless profit opportunities.

\textbf{Where is volatility in this calculation?} It's implicit — baked directly into the choice of the two future price levels (\$110 and \$90, i.e., ±10\% either way in this example). A wider spread between the up and down outcomes corresponds to higher assumed volatility.

\subsection{Full Mathematical Generalization}

Let:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{X} = current market price of the stock.
\item \textbf{X×u} and \textbf{X×d} = the future prices after time \emph{t} years (u > 1 for the up move; 0 < d < 1 for the down move).
\item \textbf{Pup} and \textbf{Pdn} = the option's payoffs in the up and down states, respectively, at expiry.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Build a portfolio}: buy \textbf{s} shares of stock today, and short (write) one call option. After time t:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Value if up move:    VUM = s x X x u - Pup
Value if down move:  VDM = s x X x d - Pdown
\end{lstlisting}
For a genuinely risk-free (hedged) portfolio, these must be equal:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
s x X x u - Pup = s x X x d - Pdown
\end{lstlisting}
Solving for \textbf{s} (the hedge ratio / number of shares per option):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
s = (Pup - Pdown) / [X x (u - d)]
\end{lstlisting}

The present-day value is obtained by discounting the (certain) future portfolio value at the risk-free rate:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
PV = e^(-rt) x [s x X x u - Pup]
\end{lstlisting}

This present value must equal the current holding of s shares minus the call premium (s×X − c). Solving for \textbf{c} (the call price):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
c = e^(-rt) x [(e^(rt) - d)/(u-d) x Pup  +  (u - e^(rt))/(u-d) x Pdown]    [continuous-compounding version]
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Introducing q — the risk-neutral probability (continuous-compounding form):}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
q = (e^(-rt) - d) / (u - d)
\end{lstlisting}
Rearranging the option pricing formula into its cleanest form:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
c = e^(-rt) x [q x Pup + (1 - q) x Pdown]
\end{lstlisting}

This is structurally identical to the discrete-time formula from Section 6.4 (\texttt{p = (R−d)/(u−d)}), just expressed with continuous compounding (e\textasciicircum{}(−rt) in place of 1/(1+r)).

\textbf{Why "q" is a genuine probability}: substituting q back in and simplifying shows that, under this measure, the \emph{expected} stock price at time t grows exactly at the risk-free rate:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Expected Stock Price = q x X x u + (1-q) x X x d  =  X x e^(rt)
\end{lstlisting}
In other words, under the risk-neutral measure, the stock behaves \emph{as if} it were a risk-free asset — investors are (in this modeling framework) indifferent to risk. This is precisely the \textbf{risk-neutral model} referenced conceptually back in Section 2.4.

\subsection{Multi-Step (Two-Step, Three-Step, ...) Binomial Trees}

Real markets don't move in just one discrete jump before an option expires — realistic pricing typically requires \textbf{many small steps}. The extension is conceptually simple:

\begin{itemize}
\item Divide the time to expiry into N small steps.
\item At \textbf{each} step, the price can again only move up (×u) or down (×d), compounding upon the \emph{previous} node's price.
\item This produces a full \textbf{binomial tree}: at each additional step, the number of distinct terminal price paths grows (e.g., 3 steps → 2\textsuperscript{3} = 8 total paths, though many collapse onto the same terminal price levels since "up-then-down" reaches the same node as "down-then-up").
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Working backward through the tree (the general algorithm):}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{At expiration} (the final column of the tree), compute the option's exact payoff at \emph{every} terminal node: for a call, \texttt{max(0, Price − Strike)}; for a put, \texttt{max(0, Strike − Price)}.
\item \textbf{Step back one period.} At each node in the second-to-last column, compute the expected value of the option one period ahead using the risk-neutral probability q (weighting the "up-child" and "down-child" nodes' already-known values).
\item \textbf{Discount} that expected value back one period at the risk-free rate — this gives the option's value at that node in the \emph{present} period (relative to that step).
\item \textbf{Repeat}, working backward node-by-node, column-by-column, until you reach the single starting node at t = 0. That final number is the model's estimate of the option's fair value today.
\end{enumerate}
\emph{Illustrative tree, matching the article's numeric example — starting value 10, with the price evolving down two consecutive branches: 10 → 8 → 6.4 → 5.12, and up two consecutive branches: 10 → 12 → 14.4 → 17.28, with the two "mixed" middle paths converging on 9.6 and 11.52 respectively at intermediate steps — demonstrating how paths recombine onto shared nodes rather than exploding independently.}

Note: multi-step examples in this course typically assume the \textbf{same} u and d factors apply at every step (i.e., they compound in an identical, repeated fashion at each node) — though the model's flexibility does allow different u/d values at different steps if volatility is expected to change over time.

\subsection{Worked Example \#1 — Two-Step Binomial Tree (Put Option)}

\textbf{Setup:} Put option, strike = \$110, underlying spot = \$100, expires in 1 year. Annual risk-free rate = 5\%. The price is expected to move \textbf{+20\% or −15\% every six months} (so two steps of 6 months each): u = 1.2, d = 0.85, t = 0.5 (per step).

\textbf{Step 1 — risk-neutral probability} (continuous-compounding formula):
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
q = (e^(-rt) - d)/(u - d)          ... using the given values ...
q = 0.358 (i.e., 35.8%)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Step 2 — terminal (t = 1 year) underlying prices and put payoffs}, via all combinations of the two 6-month moves:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Up-Up:    100 x 1.2 x 1.2  = $144.00  ->  Pupup = max(110-144, 0) = $0
Up-Down:  100 x 1.2 x 0.85 = $102.00  ->  Pupdn = max(110-102, 0) = $8
Down-Down: 100 x 0.85 x 0.85 = $72.25 ->  Pdndn = max(110-72.25, 0) = $37.75
\end{lstlisting}
(The "Up-Down" and "Down-Up" paths recombine to the same \$102 node.)

\textbf{Step 3 — value at the intermediate (6-month) nodes}, discounting back one step:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Node 2 (after an up-move):    p2 = e^(-rt) x [qxPupup + (1-q)xPupdn]
                                  = 0.975 x [0.358x0 + (1-0.358)x8]  =  5.00897 ~ $5.01

Node 3 (after a down-move):   p3 = e^(-rt) x [qxPupdn + (1-q)xPdndn]
                                  = 0.975 x [0.358x8 + (1-0.358)x37.75]  =  $26.43
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Step 4 — discount back to today (t = 0):}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
p1 = e^(-rt) x [qxp2 + (1-q)xp3]
   = 0.975 x [0.358x5.01 + (1-0.358)x26.43]
   ~ $18.29
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Value of the put option today = \$18.29.}

\subsection{Worked Example \#2 — Three-Step Binomial Tree (European Put)}

\textbf{Setup:} European put, 9 months to expiry, strike = \$12, underlying spot = \$10. Risk-free rate = 5\% for all periods. Price moves ±20\% every 3 months → u = 1.2, d = 0.8, t = 0.25 per step, giving a \textbf{3-step} tree (9 months \ensuremath{\div} 3 months = 3 steps).

\textbf{Step 1 — risk-neutral probability:}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
q = 0.531 (53.1%)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Step 2:} Using q and the terminal payoff values at t = 9 months (the final column of the tree), compute the corresponding intermediate values at t = 6 months by working backward one step at a time (same method as the two-step example above).

\textbf{Step 3:} Using the t = 6-month values, compute the values at t = 3 months; then using those, compute the single value at t = 0.

\textbf{Result: the present-day value of the put option = \$2.18} — reasonably close to the value obtained from the Black-Scholes model for the same inputs, which is \textbf{\$2.30}. (This closeness illustrates that as the binomial tree is built with more steps, it converges toward the continuous-time Black-Scholes result.)

\subsection{When Is the Binomial Model the Right Choice?}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{American options} that can be exercised before expiry — the binomial model can check, at every single node, whether early exercise is optimal.
\item Situations with \textbf{changing volatility} over the option's life (different u/d factors can be applied at different steps).
\item Underlyings with \textbf{dividend payments}, since the model's discrete, node-by-node structure can accommodate cash flows occurring partway through the option's life.
\end{itemize}
\subsection{Effectiveness and Limitations of the Binomial Model}

\textbf{Effectiveness}: As the number of steps (N) increases (i.e., as each \ensuremath{\Delta}t shrinks), the binomial tree's discrete up/down paths increasingly resemble the continuous price movement observed in real markets — the model converges toward the smoother, continuous-time behavior captured by models like Black-Scholes.

\textbf{Handling risk aversion}: The clever mechanism is in how \textbf{u}, \textbf{d}, and the risk-neutral probabilities (\textbf{p}/\textbf{q}, \textbf{1−p}) are chosen/calibrated — based on the underlying's observed volatility and the risk-free rate. This effectively "builds in" the risk premium investors would demand, \emph{without} the model needing to explicitly specify or model investors' individual risk preferences. This elegant shortcut is precisely what is meant by \textbf{risk-neutral valuation}.

\textbf{Limitations:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Requires \textbf{many computations}, especially for options with long times to expiry (more steps needed for accuracy) — this can become computationally intensive.
\item Still relies on \textbf{simplifying assumptions} — typically that the risk-free rate and volatility are \textbf{constant} over the option's life (even though the model \emph{can} be adapted to allow them to vary, doing so adds complexity).
\item The finer the time intervals used, the harder it becomes to specify/predict the exact payoffs at each node with precision — accurately forecasting future price levels remains an inherent limitation of any binomial approach.
\end{itemize}
Despite these constraints, the binomial model remains a valuable and widely-used tool, striking a practical balance between the computational intensity of more advanced numerical methods and the restrictive assumptions of simpler closed-form models like Black-Scholes. Values computed via the binomial model closely match those from Black-Scholes, which supports its accuracy and usefulness — and it can be adapted flexibly to a trader's specific needs as an alternative (or complement) to Black-Scholes.

\subsection{Other Applications of the Binomial Model (Beyond Plain Options)}

Because the binomial model is fundamentally a general framework for valuing anything with sequential, uncertain decision points, it extends naturally to:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Valuing projects or investments} with a high degree of uncertainty.
\item \textbf{Capital budgeting and resource-allocation decisions.}
\item Projects with \textbf{multiple periods} or an \textbf{embedded option to continue or abandon} the project at specific decision points (this is often called "real options" analysis).
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Example}: complex investment decisions such as \textbf{oil drilling projects} can be modeled with the binomial approach by breaking the overall decision into a series of decision points, each with different possible outcomes — allowing a company to assess risk, quantify potential rewards, and retain the flexibility to adapt as new information becomes available over time.

\subsection{Embedded Options}

\textbf{Embedded options} are option-like features woven directly into the terms of certain financial securities, giving either the issuer or the holder the right to alter the security's cash flows or value under pre-defined conditions. Unlike a standalone, separately-traded option, an embedded option is an integral part of the underlying security itself. They are most common in \textbf{fixed-income securities}.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Callable bonds}: give the \textbf{issuer} the right to redeem (buy back) the bond before its stated maturity date — typically exercised when interest rates fall (the issuer can refinance more cheaply). This benefits the issuer but exposes the bondholder to \textbf{reinvestment risk} (having to reinvest the returned principal at now-lower prevailing rates).
\item \textbf{Putable bonds}: give the \textbf{bondholder} the right to sell the bond back to the issuer before maturity — providing the investor a form of \textbf{downside protection}.
\end{itemize}

\section{Consolidated Formula Cheat-Sheet}

\textbf{No-Arbitrage / Forward Pricing}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(T) = S0 x (1+rf)^T                                    [basic forward price, no cash flows]
F0(T) = [S0 - (gamma-theta)] x (1+rf)^T                            [with discrete carry benefits/costs]
F0(T) = S0 x e^[(rc+theta-gamma)xT]                                [continuous carry benefits/costs]
Vt(T) = [Ft(T) - F0(T)] / (1+i)^(T-t)                      [value of forward, no cash flows]
Vt(T) = St - (gamma-theta)(1+r)^t - F0(T)(1+r)^(-(T-t))            [equity forward value, discrete divs]
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Fixed-Income Forwards/Futures}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
F0(T) = (S0 - PVC0) x (1+rf)^T                             [bond forward price]
Vt(Long) = (St - PVCt) - F0(T)/(1+rf)^(T-t)                [bond forward value]
F0(T) = QF0(T) x CF(T)                                      [bond futures price]
Clean price = Full price - Accrued Interest
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{FRAs}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
FRA(0,h,m) = {[1+L0(h+m)t(h+m)]/[1+L0(h)th] - 1} / tm
Payoff = (FRAg - FRA0) x (m/360) x Notional
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Currency Forwards}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
FT(D/F) = S0(D/F) x [(1+rD)^T / (1+rF)^T]                  [covered interest rate parity]
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Put-Call Parity}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
C0 + PV(X) = P0 + S0
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Binomial Model — One Step}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
p (or pi or q) = (R - d)/(u - d)             [discrete: R = 1+rf]
q = (e^(-rt) - d)/(u - d)                   [continuous compounding]
c = e^(-rt) x [qxPup + (1-q)xPdown]
s (hedge ratio) = (Pup - Pdown)/[Xx(u-d)]
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Option Moneyness / Intrinsic Value}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=notesCode]
Intrinsic value (call) = max(0, St - PV(X))
Intrinsic value (put)  = max(0, PV(X) - St)
CT = max(0, ST - X);   PT = max(0, X - ST)
\end{lstlisting}

\end{itemize}

\chapter{Option Greeks}

\emph{Course notes — Introduction to Quantitative Finance (Reading Course)}


\section{Why We Need the Greeks}

An option's price (its \textbf{premium}) is not a fixed number — it is a function of several inputs that are all constantly changing:

\[
V = f(S, K, r, \sigma, T)
\]

where

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.28\textwidth}L{0.64\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\midrule
\(S\) & Current price of the underlying asset \\
\(K\) & Strike (exercise) price \\
\(r\) & Risk-free interest rate \\
\(\sigma\) & Volatility of the underlying (usually \emph{implied} volatility) \\
\(T\) & Time remaining to expiration \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}

The \textbf{Option Greeks} are simply the \textbf{partial derivatives} of the option price with respect to each of these inputs — i.e., they tell you \emph{how much the option's price will move if one input changes, holding everything else constant}. They are the sensitivity measures (and, collectively, the risk-management dashboard) of an option position.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Intuition:} if the stock market is a moving system with several "dials" (price, time, volatility, interest rate), the Greeks tell you how hard the option's price gets tugged when each dial is turned by one unit.
\end{quote}

Each Greek is a first derivative of price with respect to one variable — \textbf{except Gamma}, which is a \emph{second} derivative (the derivative of Delta, which is itself a derivative of price). This is why Gamma is sometimes described as "delta's delta," or the \emph{acceleration} of the option price.

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.14\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.25\textwidth}L{0.24\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Greek} & \textbf{Dependent variable} & \textbf{Independent variable} & \textbf{Order} \\
\midrule
Delta (\(\Delta\)) & Option price & Underlying asset price & 1st derivative of \(V\) w.r.t. \(S\) \\
Gamma (\(\Gamma\)) & Delta & Underlying asset price & 2nd derivative of \(V\) w.r.t. \(S\) \\
Vega (\(\nu\)) & Option price & Volatility & 1st derivative of \(V\) w.r.t. \(\sigma\) \\
Theta (\(\Theta\)) & Option price & Time to maturity & 1st derivative of \(V\) w.r.t. \(T\) \\
Rho (\(\rho\)) & Option price & Interest rate & 1st derivative of \(V\) w.r.t. \(r\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}

These five — \textbf{Delta, Gamma, Theta, Vega, Rho} — are the primary Greeks. Others exist (Vanna, Charm, Volga, etc., "second-order Greeks") but are outside the scope here.

\subsection{Quick refresher on options}

\begin{itemize}
\item A \textbf{call option} gives the buyer the right (not obligation) to \emph{buy} the underlying at the strike price before/at expiry.
\item A \textbf{put option} gives the buyer the right (not obligation) to \emph{sell} the underlying at the strike price before/at expiry.
\item The price paid for this right is the \textbf{premium}. The buyer (long) pays the premium to the writer (short); the writer collects the premium and profits if the option expires worthless/unexercised.
\item Moneyness classification: \textbf{ITM} (in-the-money — has intrinsic value), \textbf{ATM} (at-the-money — strike ≈ spot), \textbf{OTM} (out-of-the-money — no intrinsic value, pure time value).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Context: the Black–Scholes–Merton (BSM) model}

The Greeks are formally derived from the BSM option-pricing model, which extended a simple option-pricing framework by adding volatility as an input. Given \(S, K, r, \sigma, T\), BSM defines:

\[
d_1 = \frac{\ln(S/K) + \left(r + \frac{\sigma^2}{2}\right)T}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}, \qquad d_2 = d_1 - \sigma\sqrt{T}
\]

Interpretation of \(d_1\): the numerator splits into two pieces —
\begin{itemize}
\item \(\ln(S/K)\): the \textbf{return attributable to intrinsic value} (how far the stock is from the strike, in log terms), plus
\item \(\left(r + \frac{\sigma^2}{2}\right)T\): the \textbf{return attributable to time value},
\end{itemize}

—all scaled by \(\sigma\sqrt{T}\) (the total standard deviation of the stock's return over the option's life). This "intrinsic value + time value" decomposition is the intuitive core of the whole BSM framework, and every Greek formula below is built from \(d_1\), \(d_2\), and the standard normal CDF/PDF, \(N(\cdot)\) and \(N'(\cdot)\).

\[
N'(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^2/2} \quad \text{(standard normal density)}
\]

Because Greeks come from a model with idealized assumptions (constant volatility, continuous trading, no jumps, etc.), they are \textbf{theoretical estimates}, not guarantees — real option prices can move differently from what the Greeks predict, especially around unexpected events. In practice, virtually no one calculates these by hand while trading; every options trading platform/broker terminal displays live Greeks per strike, computed automatically.


\section{Delta (\texorpdfstring{\(\Delta\)}{Delta}) — Directional Sensitivity}

\subsection{Definition}

\[
\Delta = \frac{\partial V}{\partial S}
\]

Delta measures how much an option's price changes for a \textbf{1-unit (e.g. ₹1 or \$1) change in the underlying's price}, holding everything else constant.

\subsection{Sign and range}

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.18\textwidth}L{0.34\textwidth}L{0.38\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{} & \textbf{Range} & \textbf{Sign} \\
\midrule
Call option & \(0 \le \Delta \le 1\) & Positive (option gains value as stock rises) \\
Put option & \(-1 \le \Delta \le 0\) & Negative (option gains value as stock falls) \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}

The \textbf{closer Delta is to ±1, the deeper in-the-money} the option is (it starts behaving almost like the stock itself). The closer Delta is to 0, the deeper out-of-the-money (the option barely reacts to the stock).

\subsection{BSM formula}

For a \textbf{non-dividend-paying} stock:
\[
\Delta_{\text{call}} = N(d_1), \qquad \Delta_{\text{put}} = N(d_1) - 1
\]

For a \textbf{dividend-paying} stock with continuous dividend yield \(q\):
\[
\Delta_{\text{call}} = e^{-qT}N(d_1), \qquad \Delta_{\text{put}} = e^{-qT}\big(N(d_1) - 1\big)
\]

The \(e^{-qT}\) term discounts \(N(d_1)\) slightly — e.g. if the non-dividend delta would be \(N(d_1)=0.50\), a modest dividend yield might pull it down to something like \$0.48\$. Since \(e^{-qT} < 1\) always (for \(q>0\)), a dividend-paying stock's call delta is always a little \emph{smaller} than the non-dividend equivalent.

An \textbf{at-the-money option's delta is always close to 0.5} for a call (and −0.5 for a put) — this follows because when \(S \approx K\), the \(d_1\) term is small (near 0), and \(N(0) = 0.5\).

\textbf{Important edge-case behavior:} as the stock price moves deep ITM, both \(N(d_1)\) and \(N(d_2)\) approach 1, and delta approaches 1 (or −1 for puts). At this extreme, BSM essentially collapses into the \emph{simple} (pre-volatility) option-pricing model — this is part of why BSM does a relatively poor job pricing deep-ITM or deep-OTM options: volatility stops mattering much once the outcome is nearly certain either way.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Technical nuance:} delta (\(N(d_1)\)) is often used casually as "the probability the option finishes in-the-money," but strictly speaking the \textbf{risk-neutral probability of finishing ITM is @@I1@@}, not \(N(d_1)\). They are close in value (especially for short-dated, low-volatility options), which is why the rule-of-thumb persists, but they are not mathematically identical.
\end{quote}

\subsection{Delta as a hedge ratio}

Delta tells you \textbf{how many shares of the underlying are needed to offset one option's price risk}. If you know an option's delta, you can hedge your position by buying or shorting (delta × number of shares underlying the option) of the underlying.

\subsection{Worked examples}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Example (CFI/course style):} A call option has Delta = 0.6. If the stock rises by ₹1, the option's price rises by about ₹0.60.

\textbf{Example (Plain Bagel):} An option has Delta = 0.2. If the underlying stock rises \$1, the option rises 20 cents.

\textbf{Example (course, ATM):} Nifty is at 25,500 (ATM strike), call premium = ₹100, Delta = 0.55.
If Nifty moves up by 1 point → premium moves by 0.55 → premium ≈ ₹100.55.

\textbf{Example (FRM):} Stock \(S=\$100\), strike \(K=\$90\), 6-month call, premium ≈ \$16 (from BSM), Delta = 0.5.
If the stock rises to \$101, the call price rises by about 50 cents (delta × \$1 move), i.e. to roughly \$16.50 — \textbf{not} a full \$1 move, because the option is not a 1-for-1 proxy for the stock.
\end{quote}

\subsection{Portfolio Delta}

The Delta of a portfolio of options is simply the \textbf{weighted average of the individual deltas}, weighted by position size (number of contracts × multiplier). E.g., if you write options with deltas of 0.2, 0.4, 0.6, 0.7, 0.8 (in some position-weighted mix) and the weighted average works out to 0.45, then a ₹1 move in the underlying changes your total book value by about ₹0.45 per unit — even though the individual legs move by very different amounts.

\subsection{Delta of Forwards and Futures}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Forward contracts:} Delta ≈ \textbf{1}. Forwards have thin/no secondary markets, so their price tracks the underlying almost exactly.
\item \textbf{Futures contracts:} Delta is \textbf{slightly less than 1} (e.g., 0.94–0.96), because futures are marked-to-market daily; the futures price and spot price can diverge slightly day-to-day even though they must converge by the maturity date. The correlation between changes in the futures price and changes in the spot price is not perfectly 1, which is why futures delta is marginally below 1.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Covered vs Naked Positions — Why Hedging Matters}

Before getting into hedging mechanics, it's worth understanding \textbf{why} an option writer needs to hedge at all.

Say you (the writer) sell someone a call option: stock at \$100, strike \$90, premium collected = \$16. If the buyer exercises (stock price rises above \$90), they can demand a share at \$90 from you.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Covered position:} you already own the underlying share. You simply hand it over — no extra risk.
\item \textbf{Naked position:} you do \emph{not} own the share. You must go buy it in the market at the \emph{current} (higher) price and sell it to the option holder for \$90 — realizing the loss immediately. In practice, options are usually cash-settled rather than requiring this physical scramble, but the economic loss is identical.
\end{itemize}

The danger: if the stock price at expiration is \$110 or \$120, your loss is the difference. If it's \$500 or \$1,000 (hypothetically), your loss keeps growing — \textbf{a naked call writer has theoretically unlimited loss potential}, since a stock price has no upper bound.

\textbf{Solution:} buy enough of the underlying stock \emph{in proportion to Delta} so that gains/losses on the stock offset gains/losses on the written option. This is \textbf{Delta hedging}.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Analogy:} think of the standard deviation (risk) of your unhedged position as a piece of metal in a vise. Delta hedging is cranking the vise — squeezing that variability down. A perfect hedge can, in theory, drive the variability to (near) zero, by holding \textbf{offsetting long and short positions}.
\end{quote}

Practically, writers often start naked and only begin hedging once there's real risk of the option moving in-the-money with momentum — if an OTM option looks likely to stay OTM, the incentive to hedge is low; but predicting this is inherently uncertain, which is exactly why the Greeks-based framework exists.

\subsection{Worked Delta-Hedging Examples}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Example 1 — hedging with stock (simple):}
You are \textbf{long 1 call option} (each option contract = 100 shares of underlying) with Delta = 0.6.
Total position delta = 0.6 × 100 = 60.
To become delta-neutral, \textbf{short 60 shares} of the underlying stock.
(If instead you \emph{wrote} — went short — the call, you'd do the opposite: \textbf{buy} 60 shares. If long a put, you'd need to be long the corresponding number of shares to hedge.)

\textbf{Example 2 — dynamic rebalancing (FRM):}
Non-dividend stock at \$200. You've \textbf{written} (are short) call options on 200 shares (i.e., 2 contracts) with strike \$202, Delta = 0.5 (nearly ATM).

\textbf{Q1: What do you need to do right now to be delta-neutral?}
Total delta from the short calls = \(-0.5 \times 200 = -100\).
To offset this, \textbf{buy 100 shares} of stock (delta = +100 from the shares cancels the −100 from the calls).

\textbf{Q2: The stock falls to \$195 — the option is now more OTM, and Delta falls to 0.4. What now?}
New required hedge = \(0.4 \times 200 = 80\) shares.
You are currently holding 100 shares, so you must \textbf{sell 20 shares} to bring your hedge down to 80.

This illustrates the central fact about Delta hedging: \textbf{it is not a one-time action.} As the stock price moves, Delta itself moves, so the hedge ratio must be continuously recalculated and rebalanced — this is called \textbf{dynamic hedging}.

\textbf{Example 3 — hedging with another option (instead of stock):}
If you hold a position with a net delta of \(+5\), you can instead find another option with a delta of \(-5\) and combine the two so the deltas cancel — e.g., pairing a call and a put with matching (opposite-sign) deltas. This is not typically how long-horizon portfolios are managed, but it's a valid short-term hedging tool available to any options trader.
\end{quote}


\section{Gamma (\texorpdfstring{\(\Gamma\)}{Gamma}) — The Acceleration of Delta}

\subsection{Definition}

\[
\Gamma = \frac{\partial \Delta}{\partial S} = \frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial S^2}
\]

Gamma measures how much \textbf{Delta itself changes} for a 1-unit move in the underlying. It is the \textbf{second derivative} of the option price with respect to the stock price — hence "the delta of delta," or, informally, the option's \emph{acceleration}.

BSM formula (same for calls and puts):
\[
\Gamma = \frac{N'(d_1)}{S\sigma\sqrt{T}}
\]

\subsection{Behavior}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Long options (calls or puts) always have positive Gamma.}
\item Gamma is \textbf{maximum when the option is at-the-money} (strike ≈ spot).
\item Gamma \textbf{decreases} as the option moves deep ITM or deep OTM.
\item Gamma tends to \textbf{rise as expiration approaches} for ATM options (the option's fate becomes more binary/urgent).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Why Gamma matters}

If you're Delta-hedging, Gamma tells you \textbf{how fast your hedge will go stale} — i.e., how often you'll need to rebalance. High-Gamma positions require frequent adjustment; low-Gamma positions are more "set and forget."

Options with the highest Gamma are the \textbf{most responsive to changes in the underlying's price} — a small stock move can meaningfully re-rate the whole position's sensitivity.

\subsection{Worked Examples}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Example (Plain Bagel):} A \$2 option has Delta = 0.2 and Gamma = 0.1.
\begin{itemize}
\item Stock rises \$1 → option rises to \$2.20 (delta effect); simultaneously, Delta rises by the gamma amount, from 0.2 to 0.3.
\item \textbf{Next} time the stock rises \$1, the option now gains \textbf{30 cents} (not 20), because its Delta has grown.
\end{itemize}

This is the compounding/"inception" nature of Gamma: Delta tells you the \emph{current} rate of change, but Gamma tells you how that rate of change is itself evolving.

\textbf{Example (course):} Delta = 0.50, Gamma = 0.02.
After a 1-point move in the underlying, the new Delta = 0.50 + 0.02 = \textbf{0.52}.
The \emph{next} point move will therefore move the option price by 0.52, not 0.50 — the option price is now accelerating upward faster than a purely linear (delta-only) estimate would suggest.
\end{quote}

\subsection{Gamma-Neutral Hedging}

Just as we can build a Delta-neutral position, we can build a \textbf{Gamma-neutral} position — useful because a Delta-neutral position can still have substantial Gamma risk (it will \emph{stop} being delta-neutral the moment the stock moves, precisely because of Gamma).

Formula — the number of hedging options needed:
\[
N_{\text{hedge}} = -\frac{\Gamma_{\text{portfolio}}}{\Gamma_{\text{hedging option}}}
\]

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Worked example (FRM):}
You hold a \textbf{short option position that is already Delta-neutral}, with portfolio Gamma = \textbf{−800}.
A tradable option is available with Delta = 0.8, Gamma = 2.

\textbf{Step 1 — Gamma-hedge:} buy \(N = 800 / 2 = 400\) of this option. This neutralizes the portfolio's Gamma.

\textbf{Step 2 — but this introduces new Delta:} buying 400 options with delta 0.8 each adds \(400 \times 0.8 = 320\) of delta to the portfolio (which was previously delta-neutral).

\textbf{Step 3 — re-hedge Delta:} short \textbf{320 shares} of the underlying stock to bring the portfolio back to delta-neutral, \emph{while remaining gamma-neutral}.

This two-step process (hedge Gamma with another option, then re-hedge the Delta that the Gamma-hedge introduced, usually with stock) is the standard technique for building a position that is neutral to \emph{both} first- and second-order price risk.
\end{quote}

\subsection{The Delta–Theta–Gamma Relationship (BSM PDE)}

The three "price-related" Greeks are not independent — they are linked by an identity derived from the Black-Scholes-Merton differential equation:

\[
rV = \Theta + rS\Delta + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2 \Gamma
\]

Reading the two sides:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Left side:} the risk-free rate multiplied by the option's price — roughly, "the dollar return you should expect to earn on the option's value, at the risk-free rate."
\item \textbf{Right side:} the \emph{same} quantity decomposed into contributions from time decay (\(\Theta\)), the underlying's drift weighted by rate and price (\(rS\Delta\)), and convexity from Gamma weighted by price-squared and variance (\(\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2S^2\Gamma\)).
\end{itemize}

Think of this as a "budget constraint" among the Greeks: an option's assumed fair return has to be accounted for by \emph{some combination} of time decay, directional exposure, and convexity — you can't get a return "for free."


\section{Theta (\texorpdfstring{\(\Theta\)}{Theta}) — Time Decay}

\subsection{Definition}

\[
\Theta = \frac{\partial V}{\partial T} \quad \text{(commonly quoted as the negative of this — value lost per day)}
\]

Theta measures how much an option's price changes with the \textbf{passage of one day}, holding everything else constant. Because time can only move in one direction (an option never "gains" time back), this is called \textbf{time decay}.

BSM formulas (non-dividend paying stock):
\[
\Theta_{\text{call}} = -\frac{S\sigma N'(d_1)}{2\sqrt{T}} - rKe^{-rT}N(d_2)
\]
\[
\Theta_{\text{put}} = -\frac{S\sigma N'(d_1)}{2\sqrt{T}} + rKe^{-rT}N(-d_2)
\]

\subsection{Sign convention}

\begin{itemize}
\item For a \textbf{long} option position (you bought it), Theta is (almost always) \textbf{negative} — the position loses value each day, all else equal. (It can occasionally be positive for some European options in unusual configurations, but negative is the standard case.)
\item For a \textbf{short/written} option position, Theta works in your favor — you gain as the option decays, since you profit if it expires unused.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Behavior}

\begin{itemize}
\item Theta is \textbf{most negative when the option is at-the-money} — the option's ultimate fate (ITM or OTM) is least certain, so each passing day matters most.
\item Theta becomes more pronounced (accelerates) as \textbf{expiration approaches}.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Worked Examples}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Example (course):} Theta = −0.05. All else equal, the option loses ₹0.05 in value per day.

\textbf{Example (course, in an option-chain context):} Theta = −10 (often written as "−10" but interpreted as reducing price by 10). If a hypothetical ATM option is priced ₹100 today (6th of the month) and nothing else in the market changes by the next day (7th), the option's price will automatically fall by ₹10, to ₹90.

\textbf{Key mental model:} Options are \textbf{wasting assets} — if you're long an option and simply hold it without the underlying moving in your favor, time alone erodes your position's value every single day.
\end{quote}


\section{Vega (\texorpdfstring{\(\nu\)}{nu}) — Volatility Sensitivity}

\subsection{Definition}

\[
\nu = \frac{\partial V}{\partial \sigma}
\]

Vega measures how much an option's price changes for a \textbf{1 percentage-point change in implied volatility (IV)}. Unlike the other Greeks, Vega is expressed as a \textbf{money amount}, not a decimal ratio.

BSM formula (same for calls and puts):
\[
\nu = S\sqrt{T}\,N'(d_1) \quad \text{(or } Se^{-qT}\sqrt{T}N'(d_1)\text{ with a dividend yield)}
\]

\subsection{Implied Volatility vs. Historical Volatility}

This distinction matters a great deal for Vega, since Vega is defined with respect to \textbf{implied} volatility specifically:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Historical volatility} is backward-looking — computed directly from a stock's past price data (e.g., standard deviation of past returns).
\item \textbf{Implied volatility (IV)} is forward-looking — it is \emph{backed out} of current option market prices, and represents the market's collective estimate of how much the stock is likely to swing going forward. IV tends to be \textbf{higher} for stocks in high demand and \textbf{during bear markets} (when future moves are assumed to be larger/riskier).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Behavior}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{An increase in volatility raises the price of both calls and puts} — a more volatile stock is more likely to swing far enough to make \emph{either} type of option profitable before expiry, so both become more valuable.
\item Vega is \textbf{highest for at-the-money options with longer time to expiry}.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Intuitive illustration (Plain Bagel):} imagine choosing between two options with identical strikes — one on a volatile stock, one on a stable stock, both starting at the same price. All else equal, the option on the \emph{more volatile} stock is more likely to reach (or exceed) the strike before expiry, and is therefore worth more, even though "more volatile" sounds riskier in a conventional (buy-and-hold) sense.

\subsection{Worked Examples}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Example (CFI/course):} Vega = 0.20. If IV rises by 1 percentage point, the option's price rises by about ₹0.20.

\textbf{Example (course):} IV rises from 8\% to 9\% (a 1-point change) with Vega = 10. The option's premium rises from ₹100 to ₹110.
\end{quote}

Traders use Vega to \textbf{bet on, or hedge against, changes in market volatility itself} — independent of which direction the underlying stock moves.


\section{Rho (\texorpdfstring{\(\rho\)}{rho}) — Interest Rate Sensitivity}

\subsection{Definition}

\[
\rho = \frac{\partial V}{\partial r}
\]

Rho measures how much an option's price changes for a \textbf{1 percentage-point change in the risk-free interest rate} (e.g., the relevant government T-bill rate).

BSM formulas:
\[
\rho_{\text{call}} = KTe^{-rT}N(d_2), \qquad \rho_{\text{put}} = -KTe^{-rT}N(-d_2)
\]

\subsection{Sign convention and behavior}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Calls:} Rho is \textbf{positive} — call options become more valuable as rates rise.
\item \textbf{Puts:} Rho is \textbf{negative} — put options become less valuable as rates rise.
\item Rho is \textbf{larger (more significant) for options with longer time to maturity} — this ties back to the "time value" component of \(d_1\): longer-dated options carry more embedded time value, and interest rates directly affect the discounting of that time value.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Practical significance}

Rho is generally considered the \textbf{least important of the five Greeks}, because option prices are far less sensitive to interest-rate changes than to changes in the underlying price, time, or volatility. Interest rates typically move slowly and in small increments (especially in a stable-rate regime), so Rho's real-world impact on short-dated options is often negligible and is frequently ignored by retail traders. It becomes more relevant for \textbf{long-dated options} and in \textbf{high-rate / rapidly-changing-rate environments}.


\section{Summary Table of the Five Greeks}

{\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.10\textwidth}L{0.20\textwidth}L{0.16\textwidth}L{0.16\textwidth}L{0.16\textwidth}L{0.15\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Greek} & \textbf{Measures sensitivity of...} & \textbf{...to a unit change in} & \textbf{Call sign} & \textbf{Put sign} & \textbf{Peaks when} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Delta (\ensuremath{\Delta})} & Option price & Underlying price & \(0\) to \(1\) & \(-1\) to \(0\) & Deep ITM (→ ±1) \\
\textbf{Gamma (\ensuremath{\Gamma})} & Delta & Underlying price & positive & positive & At-the-money \\
\textbf{Theta (\ensuremath{\Theta})} & Option price & Time (1 day passing) & usually negative (long) & usually negative (long) & At-the-money, near expiry \\
\textbf{Vega (\ensuremath{\nu})} & Option price & Implied volatility (1\%) & positive & positive & ATM + longer maturity \\
\textbf{Rho (\ensuremath{\rho})} & Option price & Interest rate (1\%) & positive & negative & Longer maturity \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}


\section{Practical Risk Management With the Greeks (Applied Trading Framework)}

This section moves from the mathematics to how practitioners actually use the Greeks day-to-day when trading options (as opposed to computing them once and forgetting them).

\subsection{The core mental model: "Risk = Greeks"}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Central idea:} whenever the underlying's price moves, \emph{all} the Greeks of your position move simultaneously — and that collective change \textbf{is} your risk. Managing your Greeks \emph{is} managing your risk. Practically, this means an active options trader should be watching a \textbf{Greeks dashboard}, not a raw profit-and-loss (P\&L) screen — the Greeks tell you \emph{why} your P\&L is about to change, before it changes.
\end{quote}

\subsection{Two pre-trade steps, before you ever place an order}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Money management:} decide, before trading, the \emph{maximum total amount} you are willing to lose overall.
\item \textbf{Risk management:} decide, based on lot size / position size, exactly where your \textbf{stop-loss} will sit for this specific trade.
\end{enumerate}

Only after both are fixed should you move on to selecting \emph{which} trade (which strike) to take.

\subsection{Choosing a strike: let current Implied Volatility decide}

When you open an option chain, there are usually several strikes to choose from — ATM, OTM, ITM. \textbf{There is no universally "best" strike} — the right choice depends on the \emph{current volatility regime}, which you can read directly off the option chain's IV column:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{If IV is currently HIGH} → OTM strikes become reasonable, because a highly volatile underlying has a realistic chance of swinging far enough to bring an OTM option into the money.
\item \textbf{If IV is currently LOW} → avoid OTM strikes; a low-volatility underlying is unlikely to move far enough for an OTM option to become profitable before time decay erodes it. ATM/ITM becomes the more sensible choice.
\end{itemize}

Because volatility itself changes \emph{after} you've entered a trade, the "best" strike at entry may stop being ideal — which is exactly why ongoing Greek monitoring (not a one-time decision) is necessary.

\subsection{Greek behavior and biggest concern, by moneyness}

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.14\textwidth}L{0.20\textwidth}L{0.18\textwidth}L{0.18\textwidth}L{0.18\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Moneyness} & \textbf{Delta} & \textbf{Gamma / Theta / Vega} & \textbf{Biggest concern} & \textbf{What you want to happen} \\
\midrule
\textbf{OTM} & Low & All low & \textbf{Volatility must rise} — otherwise all Greeks decay toward zero as expiry nears, and the position simply bleeds out & IV increase, so Delta starts rising (OTM → ATM) \\
\textbf{ATM} & ≈ 0.5 & All \textbf{high} (Gamma, Theta, Vega peak here) & \textbf{Theta} — as expiry approaches, time decay accelerates fastest here, unless a large move happens soon & Delta should rise quickly (ATM → ITM) before decay eats the premium \\
\textbf{ITM} & High (→1 for deep ITM) & Lower (less time value left) & \textbf{Delta must not fall} — a falling Delta means the option is sliding back toward ATM/OTM & Delta should keep rising toward 1 (ITM → Deep ITM) \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}

\textbf{The common thread across all three cases:} whether you're trying to move OTM→ATM, ATM→ITM, or ITM→Deep ITM, what you need in every case is for \textbf{Delta to increase} — and Delta increases \emph{only} when volatility increases. This is why experienced/professional options traders often say they "trade volatility," not a specific strike or even a specific direction: volatility is the underlying engine that drives Delta, which drives everything else.

\subsection{Worked example: managing an ATM position as conditions drift}

Setup: Nifty at 25,500 (ATM), you buy a call for ₹100. At entry: Delta = 0.55, Gamma = 0.018, Theta ≈ −11, Vega ≈ 11 — all four Greeks arrive "attached" to the position the moment you take the trade.

Now suppose Nifty \textbf{doesn't trend strongly} — it oscillates back toward its mean rather than moving decisively in one direction. This signals \textbf{low volatility} in the market. Consequences:

\begin{itemize}
\item With low/falling IV and the passage of time (Theta), \textbf{Delta starts drifting down} — say, from 0.55 to 0.40 over some days.
\item A Delta of 0.40 now \textbf{behaves like an OTM option}, even though you originally entered at what looked like an ATM strike (ATM delta ≈ 0.55). The position has effectively "become" something you didn't intend to hold.
\item Since your original comfort/target was Delta ≈ 0.55, your job now is to bring the position's Delta \textbf{back} to 0.55 — i.e., add \(0.55 - 0.40 = 0.15\) of delta.
\item You do this by \textbf{buying an additional option whose own delta is around 0.15}, adding it to your existing position. Combined, the position's delta returns to ≈ 0.55.
\end{itemize}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Important distinction:} this is emphatically \textbf{not} the same thing as going Delta-neutral (Delta = 0). Here, the goal is to restore the position to its \emph{original target/comfort-zone} exposure, not to eliminate directional exposure entirely. "Delta-neutral" is a specific hedging strategy (see \S{}2.9, \S{}3.5); "returning to your comfort-level Delta" is a broader risk-management habit that applies to \emph{any} directional position, hedged or not.
\end{quote}

\textbf{General workflow this illustrates:}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Enter a trade based on your read of current conditions (open interest, IV, sentiment, FII/DII activity, etc.).
\item The moment the trade is live, the Greeks attach to it — this is your risk.
\item Monitor the Greeks (not just P\&L). When market conditions shift, your risk profile shifts with them.
\item Adjust the position (usually by adding/removing option legs, or a stock hedge) to bring the risk profile back to a comfortable level.
\item Repeat — this is continuous, not a one-time setup.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{A cautionary tale on \emph{not} doing this}

The practical trading source material includes a real anecdote of a trader who, for a long stretch, made decisions purely by looking at whether Nifty's candle was "red or green" on a given day — buying a large quantity of puts on red days and calls on green days, with no reference to the option chain, open interest, or Greeks, and with no defined risk-management or exit process (simply watching the running loss and squaring off only after losses had already piled up). Losses escalated over time (from roughly ₹12,000, to ₹20,000, past ₹1 lakh, and eventually into several lakhs), including a period after the trader briefly stopped and then resumed once external pressure (e.g., having the trading app removed) was lifted.

\textbf{The lesson drawn from this:} trading options based only on naive directional cues (candle color), without understanding open interest and without monitoring the Greeks that actually describe your risk, is a recipe for large, compounding, poorly-controlled losses. Structured risk management — defined money management, a defined stop-loss, and ongoing Greek monitoring — is what separates disciplined options trading from gambling on direction.


\section{What Actually Drives Volatility: Supply, Demand, and Market Participants}

Since \S{}8.4 concluded that "everything comes down to volatility," it's worth understanding \emph{what moves volatility itself}. Like any market, options prices (and hence implied volatility) are governed by \textbf{supply and demand}.

\subsection{Hedgers}

Investors/institutions holding an underlying portfolio (stocks, bonds, mutual funds, futures contracts) who want to \textbf{protect} it from a decline will buy protective options (e.g., puts against a long stock portfolio). When hedgers are actively buying protection — especially heavily, e.g. ahead of a perceived risk event — this \textbf{increases demand for options}, which pushes premiums (and implied volatility) \textbf{up}.

\subsection{Speculators}

Traders with a directional view often prefer buying an \textbf{option} over buying the underlying stock outright, because it requires a smaller premium outlay for similar (leveraged) directional exposure. A bullish speculator buys calls; a bearish speculator buys puts. Either way, this is \textbf{additional demand} for options, again pushing volatility \textbf{up}.

\subsection{Arbitrageurs}

Arbitrageurs try to \textbf{eliminate directional exposure} entirely and trade in a Delta-neutral fashion (see \S{}2.9). As the underlying moves and their position's Delta drifts away from zero, they continuously buy/sell options to bring Delta back to zero (essentially performing the delta-neutral rebalancing described in \S{}2.9's worked examples, repeatedly). This constant rebalancing activity also \textbf{adds to options demand} in aggregate.

\textbf{Put together:} when hedgers, speculators, and arbitrageurs are all actively \emph{buying} options, demand rises, and the market experiences \textbf{higher implied volatility}.

\subsection{What causes volatility to fall}

The reverse also holds. If there is no major perceived risk on the horizon (no anticipated earnings surprise, no macro event), hedgers reduce their protective buying. Speculators, seeing weak buying demand, may shift toward \textbf{selling/writing} options instead (to collect premium/income, effectively harvesting Theta) rather than buying them. When \textbf{sellers outnumber buyers}, supply increases relative to demand, and implied volatility \textbf{falls}.

\subsection{Greed, fear, and news}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Rumors of good news} → greed increases → more people rush to \emph{buy} the stock/option → demand rises → volatility (and price) rises. This is described colloquially as \textbf{"buying the rumor."}
\item \textbf{Bad or fake news} (e.g., a scandal at a company) → fear increases → people rush to \textbf{sell} → supply rises. This is described as \textbf{"selling the news."}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{"Buying" and "Selling" volatility — trader shorthand}

Because everything ultimately traces back to volatility, experienced traders often talk in terms of volatility itself rather than in terms of specific option legs:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{"Buying volatility"} = buying options (calls or puts) — you benefit if implied volatility (or realized movement) increases.
\item \textbf{"Selling volatility"} = writing/selling options — you benefit if implied volatility stays low or falls, and from time decay.
\end{itemize}

Practically:
\begin{itemize}
\item When \textbf{IV is high} (lots of demand/buying) → favor \textbf{selling} volatility (write/short options).
\item When \textbf{IV is low} (lots of supply/selling) → favor \textbf{buying} volatility (go long options) \emph{if you expect it to rise} — otherwise avoid, per \S{}8.3.
\end{itemize}


\section{Classifying Options Strategies by Direction-Dependence}

Strategies traded in the market can broadly be grouped into three categories based on how their P\&L depends on the underlying's direction. (Learn them in this order, per the source material — direction-dependent strategies first, since they're conceptually simplest.)

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Directional-bias trades} — both profit \emph{and} loss depend fully on the underlying moving in the anticipated direction. Simple long option positions fall here: \textbf{Long Call, Long Put} (pure directional bets — net \emph{buying} of options, i.e. "buying volatility" with a directional view).

\item \textbf{Directional-neutral trades} — the position does not necessarily \emph{lose} regardless of which way the market goes, but it only \emph{profits} if the market moves in one particular intended direction (e.g., certain hedged/limited-risk directional constructions).

\item \textbf{Direction-indifferent trades} — the position can \textbf{profit regardless of which direction} the market moves. Examples include:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Non-directional / net-selling strategies} ("selling volatility"): \textbf{Short Call, Short Put, Covered Call, Short Straddle, Short Strangle, Ratio Call Spread} — these tend to profit when the market stays range-bound (low realized movement), regardless of which side of the range it drifts to.
\item Pure \textbf{direction-indifferent, big-move strategies} (net \emph{buying}, "buying volatility"): \textbf{Long Straddle, Long Strangle} — profit from a large move in \emph{either} direction.
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Strategies that can fall into more than one bucket, depending on construction:} Bull Call Spread, Bear Call Spread, Bull Put Spread, Bear Put Spread, Calendar Spread, Butterfly, Iron Butterfly, Broken Wing Butterfly, Condor, Diagonal, Double Diagonal, Collar. These combine both buying and selling legs, so they can be structured to either buy or sell volatility depending on the trader's view — they don't sit purely in one category.

The recommended learning path (per the source): understand pure directional-bias strategies first (simplest — one leg, direction determines everything), then move to strategies with capped risk/direction-dependent profit, then finally to fully direction-indifferent multi-leg strategies (straddles, strangles, and the spread/condor/butterfly family), which require the deepest understanding of how all the Greeks interact simultaneously.


\section{Key Takeaways}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{The Greeks are the sensitivities of an option's price} to the five BSM inputs: Delta (price), Gamma (delta's rate of change), Theta (time), Vega (volatility), Rho (rate) — each is a partial derivative of option price with respect to one variable (Gamma is second-order).
\item \textbf{Delta ≈ hedge ratio} and (loosely) a probability-of-finishing-ITM proxy; it is the single most-watched Greek in practice.
\item \textbf{Gamma tells you how fast your Delta hedge goes stale} — high Gamma near ATM strikes means frequent rebalancing is required.
\item \textbf{Theta is the enemy of long option holders} — options are wasting assets, and decay accelerates as expiry nears, especially ATM.
\item \textbf{Vega ties option value to \emph{implied} (forward-looking) volatility}, not historical volatility — both calls and puts gain value as IV rises.
\item \textbf{Rho matters least day-to-day}, but grows more relevant for long-dated options and in high/rising-rate environments.
\item \textbf{All the Greeks change together} whenever the underlying moves — this collective shift \emph{is} your risk, which is why practitioners describe "managing the Greeks" and "managing risk" as the same activity.
\item \textbf{Delta increasing is the common goal} across every moneyness bucket (OTM→ATM→ITM→Deep ITM) — and Delta increases only when volatility rises, which is why professionals frame their trading around volatility itself rather than a fixed strike or pure direction call.
\item \textbf{Volatility itself is driven by supply and demand}, rising with buying by hedgers, speculators, and arbitrageurs (and with greed-driven rumor-buying) and falling with increased selling (and fear-driven news-selling).
\item \textbf{Disciplined risk management (position sizing + stop-loss + continuous Greek monitoring) is what separates structured options trading from directional gambling} — the Greeks dashboard, not the raw P\&L screen, is the right thing to watch while a trade is live.
\end{enumerate}
